42उपनिषद् · The Upanishads

Eknath Easwaran, translator, The Upanishads, Nilgiri Press, second edition 2007

Linked to 22 chapters: supports 12 · contradicts 7 · mechanism 4 · same claim 6 · pairing 3

The Upanishads are not a book. They are a heap of teaching sessions, set down by nobody who signed his name, attached to the ends of the four Vedas and preserved by families who recited them for centuries before anyone wrote them down. About two hundred texts carry the name; one list fixes the number at a hundred and eight; ten have been treated as principal since the eighth century, when Shankara wrote commentaries on them and by doing so made a canon out of an inheritance. Easwaran's edition gives those ten, adds the Shvetashvatara, which is no less treasured and may once have had a commentary of Shankara's that has not survived, and appends four short later texts. What holds the collection together is not a doctrine but a habit of attention. The rest of the Vedas look outward at fire, wind, dawn and rain; the Upanishads turn the instrument around and ask what is doing the looking. Easwaran calls the result a science, and means the word strictly: a discipline with a method, a laboratory and a repeatable result, where the mind is at once the instrument and the specimen. What follows takes the eleven principal texts in the order Easwaran prints them, with the introductions Michael Nagler wrote for each. Four are short enough to be given entire. The Isha is eighteen verses and states the problem; the Katha is a boy's interview with Death and supplies the working model of a person; the Mandukya is twelve sentences and maps consciousness; the Kena asks who is asking. The other seven are long, and what is given from them is the verses their arguments turn on, which for the Brihadaranyaka and the Chandogya is still a good many. The renderings here are made from the Sanskrit and are meant to be plain; Easwaran's own verse translation is the one to read with them.

The supreme science

Easwaran opens with his own case. In middle life, a professor of English literature at an Indian university under British rule, he found that literature, music, friends and teaching had stopped satisfying him, and went to the library to find out what human beings had learned about life and death. A colleague in psychology noticed that a man from the English department had been checking out every significant book in his field and demanded an explanation. Nothing Easwaran read answered the hunger. Then he came across a copy of the Upanishads, expecting no more from four-thousand-year-old texts than from Alice in Wonderland, and read a passage comparing the joy of a young, healthy, cultured man who has everything the earth can offer with a joy a hundred times greater. He was reading his own life. What arrested him was not argument but the confidence of the voice.

The historical setting he gives is the standard one. Indo-European speakers calling themselves arya entered the subcontinent through the Hindu Kush from about 2000 BC and met a civilization in the Indus valley already a thousand years old. The hymns they brought, from perhaps 1500 BC, worship the forces of life — Agni the fire, Indra the storm, Vayu the wind, Usha the dawn, Yama the first being to die — and worship them without fear. Even in the oldest layer one Being is being addressed under many aspects: truth is one, the Rig Veda says, and the wise call it by many names. Priests performed the rites for the warriors and rulers, and wrote commentaries to explain rites already too old to be understood. Hymns and commentaries together are the Vedas, from vid, to know. Each of the four collections has a first and larger part concerned with ritual action, karma kanda, and a second concerned with wisdom, jnana kanda. The Upanishads are the second part, and because they come at the end of the collections and were learned last, they are called vedanta, the end of the Vedas.

The word itself suggests sitting down near: at the feet of a teacher, in a session of instruction, usually in a forest settlement on the upper Ganges where a household of students lived with a teacher for what tradition set at twelve years. Easwaran insists on the difference from Plato. These are not dialogues in which a position is argued; they are darshana, something seen, and the student was expected to realize what he heard rather than agree with it. The settings are dramatic and various: a wife asks her husband about immortality, a king asks a sage for instruction, a boy is taught by Death, another by fire and beasts and birds. Some of the teachers are women. Ritual, the basis of the religion the texts are attached to, is almost ignored in them, and the word brahman in the sense they give it — the single power that pervades creation and exceeds it — does not appear in the hymn portion of the Rig Veda at all. Easwaran takes this seriously enough to suggest the Upanishads may not be a late flowering of Vedic thought but an independent tributary; a seated figure excavated in the Indus valley would be identified by a villager today without hesitation as Shiva, lord of yoga, in meditation.

What the forest schools did that has no parallel elsewhere, on Easwaran's account, is to apply the assumption that a law of nature holds uniformly to the medium of knowing itself. The sages observed dreaming and dreamless sleep and asked what is known in each, and what could be called the knower. They looked for invariants: in a flow of experience that never stops changing, is there anything that stays the same. And they did not stop at debating it, because they found that as concentration deepens the mind actually passes through the states under discussion, so that the strata can be separated and watched as a botanist watches a flower. The study was called brahmavidya, which means both the supreme science and the science of the Supreme, and its method was nididhyasana, meditation — not reflection, not discursive thought, not intuition, but concentration so complete that the senses close and the outside world is forgotten while inner wakefulness increases. Easwaran quotes William James on the faculty of voluntarily bringing back a wandering attention, which James called the root of judgment, character and will, and notes that James taught himself the skill well enough to lift himself out of a life-threatening depression. James's phrase for an education built on that faculty, education par excellence, is close to a literal translation of brahmavidya.

The report the sages bring back is set out in stages. Dreaming and waking are made of the same stuff: a man dreaming that he is being chased by an elephant has the heart rate and the stress hormones of a man actually chased. Waking from a dream is therefore not a passage from unreality to reality but from a lower grade of reality to a higher one, which leaves open the question whether there is a grade above waking. In dreamless sleep the observer detaches not only from the body but from the mind — as a tethered bird, the Brihadaranyaka says, tires of flying about and settles on its perch. Easwaran's claim is that one can wake up in that state, and that this is what the Upanishads call turiya, the fourth. In meditation, as concentration deepens, physical identification dissolves first; then awareness withdraws from the senses, emotions, intellect and will, and the sages found that these have no life of their own, that the mind is not conscious but is an instrument of consciousness. What is left when even the sense of "I" goes is what the Upanishads call Brahman, from the root brih, to expand: the ground of existence. The same withdrawal discovers that this ground is also the core of one's own personality, called Atman, the Self, and that the two are the same thing. That equation is what the collection is for. Its most famous statement is tat tvam asi, you are That, and Easwaran is careful about the pronoun: "you" is not the finite personality but, in his words, the consciousness "which makes the eye see and the mind think".

He is equally careful about what the state is not. It is not emptiness but purnata, fullness: in it reality is condensed into pure potential the way all the matter of the universe was condensed before the Big Bang, and physicists, asked what came before, answer that the question does not apply. The triad sat-chit-ananda names it — absolute reality, pure awareness, unconditioned joy — and the practical claim that follows is an economic one. Finite things cannot satisfy an infinite hunger; every human activity is an attempt to fill the same void from outside. Nagler draws the same conclusion in political terms in his introduction to the Isha: materialism enforces a paradigm of scarcity, spiritual economics begins from the infinitude of consciousness, and there is no shortage of love, respect or meaning. Neither of them claims that the world is unreal. The illusion, Easwaran says repeatedly, is only that we appear separate.

Isha: the inner ruler

The Isha stands first in most Indian collections, and Gandhi said that if every scripture were burned and only its first verse remained in memory, Hinduism would live. Asked by a journalist for the secret of his life in three words, he gave two from that verse: renounce and enjoy. It is the only Upanishad that has come down as part of a Samhita rather than attached to one, which is why it is entirely in verse and is still recited in the Vedic manner. Its name is taken from its first word and is unusually personal for the collection; Nagler notes that isha is cognate with German eigen, one's own, and glosses it as the inner ruler.

ईशा वास्यमिदं सर्वं यत्किञ्च जगत्यां जगत् ।
तेन त्यक्तेन भुञ्जीथा मा गृधः कस्यस्विद्धनम् ॥ १ ॥
कुर्वन्नेवेह कर्माणि जिजीविषेच्छतं समाः ।
एवं त्वयि नान्यथेतोऽस्ति न कर्म लिप्यते नरे ॥ २ ॥
īśā vāsyamidaṃ sarvaṃ yatkiñca jagatyāṃ jagat
tena tyaktena bhuñjīthā mā gṛdhaḥ kasyasviddhanam
2
kurvanneveha karmāṇi jijīviṣecchataṃ samāḥ
evaṃ tvayi nānyatheto'sti na karma lipyate nare

1All this, whatever moves in this moving world, is to be covered by the Lord. By that which is given up, enjoy; do not covet the wealth of anyone. Doing works here, one should want to live a hundred years. So it stands for you and not otherwise: action does not cling to a man.

The first word is isha, the lord, and the second, vasyam, is a verbal form of a root with five homonyms, so that it can mean to be pervaded, to be dwelt in, to be clothed, or to be hidden — Max Müller took the last. The opening phrase for "all this" is jagatyam jagat, all that moves in what is ceaselessly moving, which is what samsara also means. The pair of demands in the first two verses is the whole text in miniature and the reason Gandhi valued it: possession is denied and enjoyment commanded in one sentence, and then a full working life, a hundred years of it, is prescribed on the ground that action performed this way leaves no residue.

असुर्या नाम ते लोका अन्धेन तमसाऽऽवृताः ।
तांस्ते प्रेत्याभिगच्छन्ति ये के चात्महनो जनाः ॥ ३ ॥
अनेजदेकं मनसो जवीयो नैनद्देवा आप्नुवन्पूर्वमर्षत् ।
तद्धावतोऽन्यानत्येति तिष्ठत्तस्मिन्नपो मातरिश्वा दधाति ॥ ४ ॥
तदेजति तन्नैजति तद्दूरे तद्वन्तिके ।
तदन्तरस्य सर्वस्य तदु सर्वस्यास्य बाह्यतः ॥ ५ ॥
asuryā nāma te lokā andhena tamasā''vṛtāḥ
tāṃste pretyābhigacchanti ye ke cātmahano janāḥ
4
anejadekaṃ manaso javīyo nainaddevā āpnuvanpūrvamarṣat
taddhāvato'nyānatyeti tiṣṭhattasminnapo mātariśvā dadhāti
5
tadejati tannaijati taddūre tadvantike
tadantarasya sarvasya tadu sarvasyāsya bāhyataḥ

3Sunless are those worlds, wrapped in blind darkness. To them, after death, go the people who kill the Self. Unmoving, one, swifter than the mind; the gods did not reach it, for it went before them. Standing, it outruns others who run. In it Matarishvan sets the waters. It moves and it does not move. It is far and it is near. It is inside all this, and it is outside all this.

Verse 3 is the only threat in the text and the word it turns on is atmahan, killers of the Self, which the commentators take to mean not suicides but people who live as though there were nothing in them to kill. The worlds they go to are called asurya, sunless, and the darkness is described twice over, as blind and as a covering.

यस्तु सर्वाणि भूतान्यात्मन्येवानुपश्यति ।
सर्वभूतेषु चात्मानं ततो न विजुगुप्सते ॥ ६ ॥
यस्मिन्सर्वाणि भूतान्यात्मैवाभूद्विजानतः ।
तत्र को मोहः कः शोक एकत्वमनुपश्यतः ॥ ७ ॥
स पर्यगाच्छुक्रमकायमव्रणमस्नाविरं शुद्धमपापविद्धम् ।
कविर्मनीषी परिभूः स्वयम्भूर्याथातथ्यतोऽर्थान् व्यदधाच्छाश्वतीभ्यः समाभ्यः ॥ ८ ॥
yastu sarvāṇi bhūtānyātmanyevānupaśyati
sarvabhūteṣu cātmānaṃ tato na vijugupsate
7
yasminsarvāṇi bhūtānyātmaivābhūdvijānataḥ
tatra ko mohaḥ kaḥ śoka ekatvamanupaśyataḥ
8
sa paryagācchukramakāyamavraṇamasnāviraṃ śuddhamapāpaviddham
kavirmanīṣī paribhūḥ svayambhūryāthātathyato'rthān vyadadhācchāśvatībhyaḥ samābhyaḥ

6One who sees all beings in the Self alone, and the Self in all beings, shrinks from nothing after that. When, for one who knows, all beings have become the Self, what bewilderment and what grief can there be for one who sees oneness? It has reached everywhere: bright, bodiless, unscarred, without sinews, clean, untouched by evil. Seer, thinker, encompassing, self-existent, it has set out the things as they are for the everlasting years.

Verse 4 uses the contradiction deliberately, and verse 5 makes it a method: whatever is said of the Self, the opposite has to be said in the same breath. Matarishvan in verse 4 is a barely known figure, something like Prometheus, distributing the offices and functions of life; the same motif of a first apportionment comes back in verse 8. The verb in verse 6 that Easwaran renders as knowing no fear is vijugupsate, a strong word carrying revulsion: the one who sees this way shrinks from nothing and is disgusted by no part of creation. Augustine, Nagler notes, says the same in the Confessions — there is no sanity in those who dislike any part of what was made.

अन्धं तमः प्रविशन्ति येऽविद्यामुपासते ।
ततो भूय इव ते तमो य उ विद्यायां रताः ॥ ९ ॥
अन्यदेवाहुर्विद्ययाऽन्यदाहुरविद्यया ।
इति शुश्रुम धीराणां ये नस्तद्विचचक्षिरे ॥ १० ॥
विद्यां चाविद्यां च यस्तद्वेदोभयं सह ।
अविद्यया मृत्युं तीर्त्वा विद्ययाऽमृतमश्नुते ॥ ११ ॥
andhaṃ tamaḥ praviśanti ye'vidyāmupāsate
tato bhūya iva te tamo ya u vidyāyāṃ ratāḥ
10
anyadevāhurvidyayā'nyadāhuravidyayā
iti śuśruma dhīrāṇāṃ ye nastadvicacakṣire
11
vidyāṃ cāvidyāṃ ca yastadvedobhayaṃ saha
avidyayā mṛtyuṃ tīrtvā vidyayā'mṛtamaśnute

9Into blind darkness enter those who follow ignorance; into darkness greater than that, as it were, those who delight in knowledge. One thing, they say, comes of knowledge; another thing, they say, comes of ignorance. So we have heard from the wise who explained it to us. One who knows both knowledge and ignorance together crosses death by ignorance and reaches the deathless by knowledge.

The pattern of the four verses is fixed and worth watching before the argument about them begins. Two terms are named; both are condemned when taken alone, and the second is condemned more heavily; then the two are prescribed together, with one of them used to cross death and the other to reach what does not die. The same four lines are then repeated with a new pair of terms.

अन्धं तमः प्रविशन्ति येऽसम्भूतिमुपासते ।
ततो भूय इव ते तमो य उ सम्भूत्यां रताः ॥ १२ ॥
अन्यदेवाहुः सम्भवादन्यदाहुरसम्भवात् ।
इति शुश्रुम धीराणां ये नस्तद्विचचक्षिरे ॥ १३ ॥
सम्भूतिं च विनाशं च यस्तद्वेदोभयं सह ।
विनाशेन मृत्युं तीर्त्वा सम्भूत्याऽमृतमश्नुते ॥ १४ ॥
andhaṃ tamaḥ praviśanti ye'sambhūtimupāsate
tato bhūya iva te tamo ya u sambhūtyāṃ ratāḥ
13
anyadevāhuḥ sambhavādanyadāhurasambhavāt
iti śuśruma dhīrāṇāṃ ye nastadvicacakṣire
14
sambhūtiṃ ca vināśaṃ ca yastadvedobhayaṃ saha
vināśena mṛtyuṃ tīrtvā sambhūtyā'mṛtamaśnute

12Into blind darkness enter those who follow non-becoming; into darkness greater than that, as it were, those who delight in becoming. One thing, they say, comes of becoming; another thing, they say, comes of non-becoming. So we have heard from the wise who explained it to us. One who knows both becoming and destruction together crosses death by destruction and reaches the deathless by becoming.

Verses 9 to 14 are what scholars call a crux, and the difficulty is not textual but doctrinal. The structure is plain — two terms, both condemned when taken alone, both prescribed when taken together — but the terms are unstable. Vidya and avidya are knowledge and ignorance; sambhuti and asambhuti are becoming and its absence, the manifest and the unmanifest; and the sentence that pairs them says that the way through death runs by the lower term and the way to the deathless by the higher. Easwaran reads the first pair as the outer world and the inner, and the second as the immanent and the transcendent, and takes the passage as a rule against half-measures in either direction. Nagler draws attention to the harder half of the lesson: in each pair it is the higher term that the text says is the darker trap. Being hypnotized by one's own sensations and forgetting the world is worse than materialism, and the point lands, he says, on solipsistic escapes such as drugs, where the private reality manufactured is falser than the multiplicity outside.

हिरण्मयेन पात्रेण सत्यस्यापिहितं मुखम् ।
तत्त्वं पूषन्नपावृणु सत्यधर्माय दृष्टये ॥ १५ ॥
पूषन्नेकर्षे यम सूर्य प्राजापत्य
व्यूह रश्मीन् समूह तेजः ।
यत्ते रूपं कल्याणतमं तत्ते पश्यामि
योऽसावसौ पुरुषः सोऽहमस्मि ॥ १६ ॥
वायुरनिलममृतमथेदं भस्मान्तं शरीरम् ।
क्रतो स्मर कृतं स्मर क्रतो स्मर कृतं स्मर ॥ १७ ॥
अग्ने नय सुपथा राये अस्मान्
विश्वानि देव वयुनानि विद्वान् ।
युयोध्यस्मज्जुहुराणमेनो
भूयिष्ठां ते नमौक्तिं विधेम ॥ १८ ॥
hiraṇmayena pātreṇa satyasyāpihitaṃ mukham
tattvaṃ pūṣannapāvṛṇu satyadharmāya dṛṣṭaye
16
pūṣannekarṣe yama sūrya prājāpatya
vyūha raśmīn samūha tejaḥ
yatte rūpaṃ kalyāṇatamaṃ tatte paśyāmi
yo'sāvasau puruṣaḥ so'hamasmi
17
vāyuranilamamṛtamathedaṃ bhasmāntaṃ śarīram
krato smara kṛtaṃ smara krato smara kṛtaṃ smara
18
agne naya supathā rāye asmān
viśvāni deva vayunāni vidvān
yuyodhyasmajjuhurāṇameno
bhūyiṣṭhāṃ te namauktiṃ vidhema

15The face of truth is covered over by a golden vessel. Uncover it, Pushan, for one whose law is truth, that he may see. Pushan, sole seer, controller, sun, child of Prajapati, spread out your rays and draw in your blaze. I see that form of yours which is the most kindly. That person there: I am he. Let this breath go to the immortal wind, and let this body end in ashes. OM. Purpose, remember what was done; purpose, remember what was done. Agni, lead us by the good road to what is ours, you who know all our ways. Turn from us the crooked wrong. We would make you the fullest speech of praise.

The last four verses change register completely. They are addressed to the sun, to the wind and to fire, in the vocabulary of the hymns the Upanishads elsewhere leave behind, and they are said at a cremation. The golden disc of the sun is the cover that hides the face of truth, the same disc whose brightness must be dimmed before the form behind it can be looked at. The final line of verse 16, so'ham asmi, that am I, is one of the four great formulae; the Sanskrit carries a pun that the English cannot, since the demonstrative used for the person in the sun is also the one used for the person in the heart. Verse 17 asks the mind to remember what it has done, at the moment when there is nothing else left to do, and the text that opened by refusing possession closes by asking a fire god for the good road.

Katha: death as teacher

The Katha belongs to the Black Yajur Veda, whose home was the midland region around what is now Agra and Delhi. Of the sage Katha after whom it is named, tradition records only that he founded the school and studied under Vaishampayana. On the evidence of metre, language and the style of its thinking it is among the earliest of the verse Upanishads, and it shares whole verses with the Bhagavad Gita and whole sections with the later Maitri. Its six chapters are called vallis; editions that group them into two parts of three number the chariot verses I.3.3 to I.3.9, which counted straight through the vallis are 3.3 to 3.9. That is the numbering used here, and the same rule of counting the sections straight through is applied to the other texts below wherever their divisions run three deep.

The story is older than the Upanishad and smaller. A brief myth in the Taittiriya Brahmana tells of a boy called Nachiketa to whom Yama reveals a sacrifice that is then named after him, and in that version the boy's third request is for the secret of punar mrityu, redeath: the ability to come back each time one dies. The Upanishad takes that request and enlarges it into the question of whether there is anything that does not die at all.

उशन् ह वै वाजश्रवसः सर्ववेदसं ददौ ।
तस्य ह नचिकेता नाम पुत्र आस ॥ १ ॥
तं ह कुमारं सन्तं दक्षिणासु
नीयमानासु श्रद्धाविवेश सोऽमन्यत ॥ २ ॥
पीतोदका जग्धतृणा दुग्धदोहा निरिन्द्रियाः ।
अनन्दा नाम ते लोकास्तान् स गच्छति ता ददत् ॥ ३ ॥
uśan ha vai vājaśravasaḥ sarvavedasaṃ dadau
tasya ha naciketā nāma putra āsa
1.2
taṃ ha kumāraṃ santaṃ dakṣiṇāsu
nīyamānāsu śraddhāviveśa so'manyata
1.3
pītodakā jagdhatṛṇā dugdhadohā nirindriyāḥ
anandā nāma te lokāstān sa gacchati tā dadat

1.1Wanting the reward, Vajashravasa gave away everything he had. He had a son by the name of Nachiketa. While the gifts were being led away, faith entered the boy, and he thought: Their water drunk, their grass eaten, their milk drawn off, their strength gone — joyless are the worlds that man goes to who gives such cattle.

What Nachiketa objects to is a sacrifice in which the cows given away have stopped giving milk. He is not a rebel; his complaint is that his father is not taking his own religion seriously. The word the text gives for what enters him is shraddha, which Nagler translates as determined seriousness rather than belief, and which is the only trait the boy is ever assigned.

स होवाच पितरं तत कस्मै मां दास्यसीति ।
द्वितीयं तृतीयं तं होवाच मृत्यवे त्वा ददामीति ॥ ४ ॥
बहूनामेमि प्रथमो बहूनामेमि मध्यमः ।
किं स्विद्यमस्य कर्तव्यं यन्मयाऽद्य करिष्यति ॥ ५ ॥
अनुपश्य यथा पूर्वे प्रतिपश्य तथाऽपरे ।
सस्यमिव मर्त्यः पच्यते सस्यमिवाजायते पुनः ॥ ६ ॥
sa hovāca pitaraṃ tata kasmai māṃ dāsyasīti
dvitīyaṃ tṛtīyaṃ taṃ hovāca mṛtyave tvā dadāmīti
1.5
bahūnāmemi prathamo bahūnāmemi madhyamaḥ
kiṃ svidyamasya kartavyaṃ yanmayā'dya kariṣyati
1.6
anupaśya yathā pūrve pratipaśya tathā'pare
sasyamiva martyaḥ pacyate sasyamivājāyate punaḥ

1.4He said to his father: father, to whom will you give me? A second time and a third. Death, said the father: to Death I give you. Of many I go first; of many I go in the middle. What is it that Yama has to do that he will do today through me? Look back at how those before us were; look ahead at how those after will be. Like grain a mortal ripens; like grain he is born again.

Nagler's reading of the consignment is that the boy is given to death by his own father because every parent does exactly that: birth is the beginning of a trajectory to death, and love cannot stop it. Verse 5 is the boy reasoning about his own usefulness to Death, and verse 6 is the answer he reaches before Yama says a word — that the crop is cut and comes up again, and that this is not a consolation.

वैश्वानरः प्रविशत्यतिथिर्ब्राह्मणो गृहान् ।
तस्यैतां शान्तिं कुर्वन्ति हर वैवस्वतोदकम् ॥ ७ ॥
आशाप्रतीक्षे संगतं सूनृतां
चेष्टापूर्ते पुत्रपशूंश्च सर्वान् ।
एतद्वृङ्क्ते पुरुषस्याल्पमेधसो
यस्यानश्नन्वसति ब्राह्मणो गृहे ॥ ८ ॥
तिस्रो रात्रीर्यदवात्सीर्गृहे मेऽनश्नन् ब्रह्मन्नतिथिर्नमस्यः ।
नमस्तेऽस्तु ब्रह्मन् स्वस्ति मेऽस्तु
तस्मात्प्रति त्रीन्वरान्वृणीष्व ॥ ९ ॥
vaiśvānaraḥ praviśatyatithirbrāhmaṇo gṛhān
tasyaitāṃ śāntiṃ kurvanti hara vaivasvatodakam
1.8
āśāpratīkṣe saṃgataṃ sūnṛtāṃ
ceṣṭāpūrte putrapaśūṃśca sarvān
etadvṛṅkte puruṣasyālpamedhaso
yasyānaśnanvasati brāhmaṇo gṛhe
1.9
tisro rātrīryadavātsīrgṛhe me'naśnan brahmannatithirnamasyaḥ
namaste'stu brahman svasti me'stu
tasmātprati trīnvarānvṛṇīṣva

1.7A brahmin guest enters a house as fire itself. Men make this peace for him: fetch water, son of the sun. Hope and expectation, company, kind speech, sacrifices and good works, sons and cattle and all — these it tears from the man of little sense in whose house a brahmin stays without eating. Three nights you have stayed in my house without eating, brahmin, an honoured guest. Homage to you, brahmin, and let it be well with me. Choose three wishes in return.

He waits three nights at Yama's door without eating, a detail that is both hospitality law and a folk-tale rule, since a visitor to the land of the dead who eats its food cannot leave. Yama's apology is exact about what is at stake: an unfed guest consumes the merit of the house. The three nights buy three wishes, one for each.

शान्तसंकल्पः सुमना यथा स्याद्
वीतमन्युर्गौतमो माऽभि मृत्यो ।
त्वत्प्रसृष्टं माऽभिवदेत्प्रतीत
एतत् त्रयाणां प्रथमं वरं वृणे ॥ १० ॥
यथा पुरस्ताद् भविता प्रतीत
औद्दालकिरारुणिर्मत्प्रसृष्टः ।
सुखं रात्रीः शयिता वीतमन्युः
त्वां ददृशिवान्मृत्युमुखात् प्रमुक्तम् ॥ ११ ॥
śāntasaṃkalpaḥ sumanā yathā syād
vītamanyurgautamo mā'bhi mṛtyo
tvatprasṛṣṭaṃ mā'bhivadetpratīta
etat trayāṇāṃ prathamaṃ varaṃ vṛṇe
1.11
yathā purastād bhavitā pratīta
auddālakirāruṇirmatprasṛṣṭaḥ
sukhaṃ rātrīḥ śayitā vītamanyuḥ
tvāṃ dadṛśivānmṛtyumukhāt pramuktam

1.10That Gautama may be calm in purpose, kind, and free of anger towards me, Death; that he may greet me, released by you, and know me — this of the three I choose as the first. As before he will greet you, Auddalaki Aruni, released by me; he will sleep at night with his anger gone, seeing you freed from the mouth of death.

The first wish is domestic. The boy asks that his father's anger be gone and that he be recognized and received when he comes back, and the two things he asks for, recognition and the end of anger, are the two things a returning child would want. Yama grants it without comment.

स्वर्गे लोके न भयं किंचनास्ति
न तत्र त्वं न जरया बिभेति ।
उभे तीर्त्वाऽशनायापिपासे
शोकातिगो मोदते स्वर्गलोके ॥ १२ ॥
स त्वमग्निं स्वर्ग्यमध्येषि मृत्यो
प्रब्रूहि त्वं श्रद्दधानाय मह्यम् ।
स्वर्गलोका अमृतत्वं भजन्त
एतद् द्वितीयेन वृणे वरेण ॥ १३ ॥
प्र ते ब्रवीमि तदु मे निबोध
स्वर्ग्यमग्निं नचिकेतः प्रजानन् ।
अनन्तलोकाप्तिमथो प्रतिष्ठां
विद्धि त्वमेतं निहितं गुहायाम् ॥ १४ ॥
svarge loke na bhayaṃ kiṃcanāsti
na tatra tvaṃ na jarayā bibheti
ubhe tīrtvā'śanāyāpipāse
śokātigo modate svargaloke
1.13
sa tvamagniṃ svargyamadhyeṣi mṛtyo
prabrūhi tvaṃ śraddadhānāya mahyam
svargalokā amṛtatvaṃ bhajanta
etad dvitīyena vṛṇe vareṇa
1.14
pra te bravīmi tadu me nibodha
svargyamagniṃ naciketaḥ prajānan
anantalokāptimatho pratiṣṭhāṃ
viddhi tvametaṃ nihitaṃ guhāyām

1.12In the world of heaven there is no fear at all; you are not there, and one does not fear old age. Crossing both hunger and thirst, past sorrow, one is glad in the world of heaven. You know the fire that leads to heaven, Death; tell it to me, for I have faith. Those in the world of heaven share in the deathless. This I choose with my second wish. I will tell it to you; learn it from me. Know the fire that leads to heaven, Nachiketa: the winning of endless worlds, and their support, set down in the hidden place.

लोकादिमग्निं तमुवाच तस्मै
या इष्टका यावतीर्वा यथा वा ।
स चापि तत्प्रत्यवदद्यथोक्तं
अथास्य मृत्युः पुनरेवाह तुष्टः ॥ १५ ॥
तमब्रवीत् प्रीयमाणो महात्मा
वरं तवेहाद्य ददामि भूयः ।
तवैव नाम्ना भविताऽयमग्निः
सृङ्कां चेमामनेकरूपां गृहाण ॥ १६ ॥
त्रिणाचिकेतस्त्रिभिरेत्य सन्धिं
त्रिकर्मकृत्तरति जन्ममृत्यू ।
ब्रह्मजज्ञं देवमीड्यं विदित्वा
निचाय्येमां शान्तिमत्यन्तमेति ॥ १७ ॥
त्रिणाचिकेतस्त्रयमेतद्विदित्वा
य एवं विद्वांश्चिनुते नाचिकेतम् ।
स मृत्युपाशान् पुरतः प्रणोद्य
शोकातिगो मोदते स्वर्गलोके ॥ १८ ॥
lokādimagniṃ tamuvāca tasmai
yā iṣṭakā yāvatīrvā yathā vā
sa cāpi tatpratyavadadyathoktaṃ
athāsya mṛtyuḥ punarevāha tuṣṭaḥ
1.16
tamabravīt prīyamāṇo mahātmā
varaṃ tavehādya dadāmi bhūyaḥ
tavaiva nāmnā bhavitā'yamagniḥ
sṛṅkāṃ cemāmanekarūpāṃ gṛhāṇa
1.17
triṇāciketastribhiretya sandhiṃ
trikarmakṛttarati janmamṛtyū
brahmajajñaṃ devamīḍyaṃ viditvā
nicāyyemāṃ śāntimatyantameti
1.18
triṇāciketastrayametadviditvā
ya evaṃ vidvāṃścinute nāciketam
sa mṛtyupāśān purataḥ praṇodya
śokātigo modate svargaloke

1.15He told him that fire, the beginning of the worlds, what the bricks are and how many and how laid. And the boy said it back as it had been said, and Death, pleased, spoke again. Delighted, the great one said to him: I give you here today a further gift. This fire shall go by your name; and take this chain of many shapes. Whoever lays the triple Nachiketa fire, joins with the three and does the three works, crosses over birth and death. Knowing the god born of Brahman, the adorable, and seeing him, he comes to peace without end. Whoever knows the three and, so knowing, piles the Nachiketa fire, throws off the snares of death before him and is glad in the world of heaven, past sorrow.

The second wish is for a ritual, and Yama teaches it, names it after the boy and adds a gift of his own. The three of verse 17 are the three meetings named in tradition as father, mother and teacher, and the three works are sacrifice, study and giving. What the fire buys is heaven, and the text will shortly say what heaven is worth. The boy repeats the instruction back word for word, which is how the schools tested a student, and the repetition is what pleases Yama.

एष तेऽग्निर्नचिकेतः स्वर्ग्यो
यमवृणीथा द्वितीयेन वरेण ।
एतमग्निं तवैव प्रवक्ष्यन्ति जनासः
तृतीयं वरं नचिकेतो वृणीष्व ॥ १९ ॥
येयं प्रेते विचिकित्सा मनुष्येऽस्तीत्येके नायमस्तीति चैके ।
एतद्विद्यामनुशिष्टस्त्वयाऽहं
वराणामेष वरस्तृतीयः ॥ २० ॥
देवैरत्रापि विचिकित्सितं पुरा
न हि सुविज्ञेयमणुरेष धर्मः ।
अन्यं वरं नचिकेतो वृणीष्व
मा मोपरोत्सीरति मा सृजैनम् ॥ २१ ॥
देवैरत्रापि विचिकित्सितं किल
त्वं च मृत्यो यन्न सुज्ञेयमात्थ ।
वक्ता चास्य त्वादृगन्यो न लभ्यो
नान्यो वरस्तुल्य एतस्य कश्चित् ॥ २२ ॥
eṣa te'gnirnaciketaḥ svargyo
yamavṛṇīthā dvitīyena vareṇa
etamagniṃ tavaiva pravakṣyanti janāsaḥ
tṛtīyaṃ varaṃ naciketo vṛṇīṣva
1.20
yeyaṃ prete vicikitsā manuṣye'stītyeke nāyamastīti caike
etadvidyāmanuśiṣṭastvayā'haṃ
varāṇāmeṣa varastṛtīyaḥ
1.21
devairatrāpi vicikitsitaṃ purā
na hi suvijñeyamaṇureṣa dharmaḥ
anyaṃ varaṃ naciketo vṛṇīṣva
mā moparotsīrati mā sṛjainam
1.22
devairatrāpi vicikitsitaṃ kila
tvaṃ ca mṛtyo yanna sujñeyamāttha
vaktā cāsya tvādṛganyo na labhyo
nānyo varastulya etasya kaścit

1.19This is your fire that leads to heaven, Nachiketa, which you chose with the second wish. Men will call this fire yours. Choose the third wish, Nachiketa. When a man is dead there is this doubt: he exists, say some; he does not, say others. Let me know this, taught by you. Of my three wishes this is the third. Even the gods were in doubt about this long ago. It is not easy to know; this matter is fine. Choose another wish, Nachiketa. Do not press me; release me from this one. Even the gods were in doubt about it, you say; and you yourself, Death, say it is not easily known. No other teacher of it like you can be found, and no other wish is the equal of this.

Yama says the gods themselves were in doubt about it, that the matter is subtle, and asks to be released from his promise. The boy's counter is a piece of logic: the difficulty of the question is a reason to ask the one person qualified to answer it, not a reason to withdraw. Then Death makes his offer.

शतायुषः पुत्रपौत्रान्वृणीष्वा
बहून्पशून् हस्तिहिरण्यमश्वान् ।
भूमेर्महदायतनं वृणीष्व
स्वयं च जीव शरदो यावदिच्छसि ॥ २३ ॥
एतत्तुल्यं यदि मन्यसे वरं
वृणीष्व वित्तं चिरजीविकां च ।
महाभूमौ नचिकेतस्त्वमेधि
कामानां त्वा कामभाजं करोमि ॥ २४ ॥
ये ये कामा दुर्लभा मर्त्यलोके
सर्वान् कामांश्छन्दतः प्रार्थयस्व ।
इमा रामाः सरथाः सतूर्या
न हीदृशा लम्भनीया मनुष्यैः ।
आभिर्मत्प्रत्ताभिः परिचारयस्व
नचिकेतो मरणं माऽनुप्राक्षीः ॥ २५ ॥
śatāyuṣaḥ putrapautrānvṛṇīṣvā
bahūnpaśūn hastihiraṇyamaśvān
bhūmermahadāyatanaṃ vṛṇīṣva
svayaṃ ca jīva śarado yāvadicchasi
1.24
etattulyaṃ yadi manyase varaṃ
vṛṇīṣva vittaṃ cirajīvikāṃ ca
mahābhūmau naciketastvamedhi
kāmānāṃ tvā kāmabhājaṃ karomi
1.25
ye ye kāmā durlabhā martyaloke
sarvān kāmāṃśchandataḥ prārthayasva
imā rāmāḥ sarathāḥ satūryā
na hīdṛśā lambhanīyā manuṣyaiḥ
ābhirmatprattābhiḥ paricārayasva
naciketo maraṇaṃ mā'nuprākṣīḥ

1.23Choose sons and grandsons who live a hundred years, many cattle, elephants, gold and horses. Choose a great stretch of earth, and live yourself as many autumns as you wish. If you think any wish the equal of this, choose it: wealth and long life. Be great on the wide earth, Nachiketa; I make you the enjoyer of your desires. Whatever desires are hard to get in the world of mortals, ask for them all as you please. Here are women with chariots and music, such as are not to be had by men; be served by these, whom I give you. Do not ask about dying, Nachiketa.

Nagler notes that the offer is a piece of psychology and that Yama gives himself away with a single word: all of it is to be enjoyed svargaloke, in the world of heaven, which in this scheme is not the goal but a cul-de-sac where the results of good acts are consumed and after which one returns. The list also runs in an order — sons, cattle, land, a lifetime of one's own choosing, then pleasure — which is the order in which a man is usually bought.

श्वोभावा मर्त्यस्य यदन्तकैतत्
सर्वेंद्रियाणां जरयन्ति तेजः ।
अपि सर्वं जीवितमल्पमेव
तवैव वाहास्तव नृत्यगीते ॥ २६ ॥
न वित्तेन तर्पणीयो मनुष्यो
लप्स्यामहे वित्तमद्राक्ष्म चेत्त्वा ।
जीविष्यामो यावदीशिष्यसि त्वं
वरस्तु मे वरणीयः स एव ॥ २७ ॥
अजीर्यताममृतानामुपेत्य
जीर्यन्मर्त्यः क्वधःस्थः प्रजानन् ।
अभिध्यायन् वर्णरतिप्रमोदान्
अतिदीर्घे जीविते को रमेत ॥ २८ ॥
यस्मिन्निदं विचिकित्सन्ति मृत्यो
यत्साम्पराये महति ब्रूहि नस्तत् ।
योऽयं वरो गूढमनुप्रविष्टो
नान्यं तस्मान्नचिकेता वृणीते ॥ २९ ॥
śvobhāvā martyasya yadantakaitat
sarveṃdriyāṇāṃ jarayanti tejaḥ
api sarvaṃ jīvitamalpameva
tavaiva vāhāstava nṛtyagīte
1.27
na vittena tarpaṇīyo manuṣyo
lapsyāmahe vittamadrākṣma cettvā
jīviṣyāmo yāvadīśiṣyasi tvaṃ
varastu me varaṇīyaḥ sa eva
1.28
ajīryatāmamṛtānāmupetya
jīryanmartyaḥ kvadhaḥsthaḥ prajānan
abhidhyāyan varṇaratipramodān
atidīrghe jīvite ko rameta
1.29
yasminnidaṃ vicikitsanti mṛtyo
yatsāmparāye mahati brūhi nastat
yo'yaṃ varo gūḍhamanupraviṣṭo
nānyaṃ tasmānnaciketā vṛṇīte

1.26Tomorrow's things, Ender, and they wear out the vigour of all the senses. And the whole of life is short. Keep the horses; keep the dance and the song. A man is not to be filled full by wealth. Shall we get wealth, having seen you? Shall we live as long as you rule? The wish I choose is that one alone. Having come near the undecaying and the deathless, what decaying mortal down here, knowing, delighting in the beauty and pleasure of the long life below — what man would take joy in it? Tell us that about which they are in doubt, Death, what is in the great passing over. This wish, which has entered the hidden place, and no other, Nachiketa chooses.

The refusal has three parts and the middle one is the argument. Whatever Yama can give wears out the faculty that would enjoy it; the goods are shvobhava, things that only last until tomorrow; and a man who has stood in front of Death cannot be paid in a currency Death controls. The last line returns the question in Yama's own words, and the interview becomes a lesson.

अन्यच्छ्रेयोऽन्यदुतैव प्रेयस्ते उभे नानार्थे पुरुषं सिनीतः ।
तयोः श्रेय आददानस्य साधु
भवति हीयतेऽर्थाद्य उ प्रेयो वृणीते ॥ १ ॥
श्रेयश्च प्रेयश्च मनुष्यमेतः
तौ सम्परीत्य विविनक्ति धीरः ।
श्रेयो हि धीरोऽभि प्रेयसो वृणीते
प्रेयो मन्दो योगक्षेमाद्वृणीते ॥ २ ॥
anyacchreyo'nyadutaiva preyaste ubhe nānārthe puruṣaṃ sinītaḥ
tayoḥ śreya ādadānasya sādhu
bhavati hīyate'rthādya u preyo vṛṇīte
2.2
śreyaśca preyaśca manuṣyametaḥ
tau samparītya vivinakti dhīraḥ
śreyo hi dhīro'bhi preyaso vṛṇīte
preyo mando yogakṣemādvṛṇīte

2.1One thing is the good, and another is the pleasant; the two, with different ends, bind a man. It goes well for the one who takes the good; the one who chooses the pleasant falls short of the goal. The good and the pleasant come to a man. The thoughtful one walks round them both and tells them apart. The thoughtful chooses the good over the pleasant; the dull chooses the pleasant, for getting and keeping.

Shreya is what conduces to well-being and preya is what one is fond of, often by conditioning. Nagler's gloss is that the choice is faced at every moment, waking and even sleeping, and that the distinction has to be attended to for exactly as long as the compartments in a personality last.

स त्वं प्रियान्प्रियरूपांश्च कामान्
अभिध्यायन्नचिकेतोऽत्यस्राक्षीः ।
नैतां सृङ्कां वित्तमयीमवाप्तो
यस्यां मज्जन्ति बहवो मनुष्याः ॥ ३ ॥
दूरमेते विपरीते विषूची
अविद्या या च विद्येति ज्ञाता ।
विद्याभीप्सिनं नचिकेतसं मन्ये
न त्वा कामा बहवोऽलोलुपन्त ॥ ४ ॥
अविद्यायामन्तरे वर्तमानाः
स्वयं धीराः पण्डितंमन्यमानाः ।
दन्द्रम्यमाणाः परियन्ति मूढा
अन्धेनैव नीयमाना यथान्धाः ॥ ५ ॥
sa tvaṃ priyānpriyarūpāṃśca kāmān
abhidhyāyannaciketo'tyasrākṣīḥ
naitāṃ sṛṅkāṃ vittamayīmavāpto
yasyāṃ majjanti bahavo manuṣyāḥ
2.4
dūramete viparīte viṣūcī
avidyā yā ca vidyeti jñātā
vidyābhīpsinaṃ naciketasaṃ manye
na tvā kāmā bahavo'lolupanta
2.5
avidyāyāmantare vartamānāḥ
svayaṃ dhīrāḥ paṇḍitaṃmanyamānāḥ
dandramyamāṇāḥ pariyanti mūḍhā
andhenaiva nīyamānā yathāndhāḥ

2.3You have turned away, Nachiketa, from the desires that are dear and wear a dear shape, having looked them over. You have not taken the road paved with wealth, in which many men sink. Far apart and opposed are these two: ignorance, and what is known as knowledge. I take Nachiketa to be one who wants knowledge; the many desires did not tear you away. Living in the middle of ignorance, wise in their own eyes, thinking themselves learned, the deluded go round and round, like blind men led by one who is blind.

Verse 4 sets the pair the Isha also works with, and sets it as a fork rather than a ladder: vidya and avidya run in opposite directions. Verse 5 is the sharpest sentence in the chapter and the one most often lifted out of it. What it condemns is not ignorance but the confidence that comes with a certain amount of learning, and the image of the blind following the blind is repeated almost word for word in the Mundaka.

न साम्परायः प्रतिभाति बालं
प्रमाद्यन्तं वित्तमोहेन मूढम् ।
अयं लोको नास्ति पर इति मानी
पुनः पुनर्वशमापद्यते मे ॥ ६ ॥
श्रवणायापि बहुभिर्यो न लभ्यः
श‍ृण्वन्तोऽपि बहवो यं न विद्युः ।
आश्चर्यो वक्ता कुशलोऽस्य लब्धा
आश्चर्यो ज्ञाता कुशलानुशिष्टः ॥ ७ ॥
न नरेणावरेण प्रोक्त एष
सुविज्ञेयो बहुधा चिन्त्यमानः ।
अनन्यप्रोक्ते गतिरत्र नास्ति
अणीयान् ह्यतर्क्यमणुप्रमाणात् ॥ ८ ॥
नैषा तर्केण मतिरापनेया
प्रोक्तान्येनैव सुज्ञानाय प्रेष्ठ ।
यां त्वमापः सत्यधृतिर्बतासि
त्वादृङ्नो भूयान्नचिकेतः प्रष्टा ॥ ९ ॥
na sāmparāyaḥ pratibhāti bālaṃ
pramādyantaṃ vittamohena mūḍham
ayaṃ loko nāsti para iti mānī
punaḥ punarvaśamāpadyate me
2.7
śravaṇāyāpi bahubhiryo na labhyaḥ
śaṛṇvanto'pi bahavo yaṃ na vidyuḥ
āścaryo vaktā kuśalo'sya labdhā
āścaryo jñātā kuśalānuśiṣṭaḥ
2.8
na nareṇāvareṇa prokta eṣa
suvijñeyo bahudhā cintyamānaḥ
ananyaprokte gatiratra nāsti
aṇīyān hyatarkyamaṇupramāṇāt
2.9
naiṣā tarkeṇa matirāpaneyā
proktānyenaiva sujñānāya preṣṭha
yāṃ tvamāpaḥ satyadhṛtirbatāsi
tvādṛṅno bhūyānnaciketaḥ praṣṭā

2.6The crossing does not appear to the careless boy, deluded by the trance of wealth. Thinking this world is and there is no other, he falls into my power again and again. Many do not get so much as to hear of it; many who hear do not know it. A wonder is the one who tells it, an adept the one who wins it, a wonder the one who knows it, taught by an adept. Told by a lesser man it is not well understood, for it is thought about in many ways. Where there is no teaching from one who has become it, there is no going; it is finer than fine, beyond argument. This thought is not to be got by argument. Told by another, dear one, it is well known. You have reached it, being fixed in truth. May we find a questioner like you, Nachiketa.

Verse 8 contains the whole doctrine of the teacher: ananya-prokte gatir atra nasti, where the teaching does not come from one who is not other than it, there is no way forward. Verse 9 adds the negative half of the same claim by ruling out the alternative method, and the word it rules out is tarka, reasoning. The Katha does not say that reasoning is wrong; it says that reasoning does not terminate, since the matter is finer than any premise available to it.

जानाम्यहं शेवधिरित्यनित्यं
न ह्यध्रुवैः प्राप्यते हि ध्रुवं तत् ।
ततो मया नाचिकेतश्चितोऽग्निः
अनित्यैर्द्रव्यैः प्राप्तवानस्मि नित्यम् ॥ १० ॥
कामस्याप्तिं जगतः प्रतिष्ठां
क्रतोरानन्त्यमभयस्य पारम् ।
स्तोममहदुरुगायं प्रतिष्ठां दृष्ट्वा
धृत्या धीरो नचिकेतोऽत्यस्राक्षीः ॥ ११ ॥
तं दुर्दर्शं गूढमनुप्रविष्टं
गुहाहितं गह्वरेष्ठं पुराणम् ।
अध्यात्मयोगाधिगमेन देवं
मत्वा धीरो हर्षशोकौ जहाति ॥ १२ ॥
एतच्छ्रुत्वा सम्परिगृह्य मर्त्यः
प्रवृह्य धर्म्यमणुमेतमाप्य ।
स मोदते मोदनीयं हि लब्ध्वा
विवृतं सद्म नचिकेतसं मन्ये ॥ १३ ॥
jānāmyahaṃ śevadhirityanityaṃ
na hyadhruvaiḥ prāpyate hi dhruvaṃ tat
tato mayā nāciketaścito'gniḥ
anityairdravyaiḥ prāptavānasmi nityam
2.11
kāmasyāptiṃ jagataḥ pratiṣṭhāṃ
kratorānantyamabhayasya pāram
stomamahadurugāyaṃ pratiṣṭhāṃ dṛṣṭvā
dhṛtyā dhīro naciketo'tyasrākṣīḥ
2.12
taṃ durdarśaṃ gūḍhamanupraviṣṭaṃ
guhāhitaṃ gahvareṣṭhaṃ purāṇam
adhyātmayogādhigamena devaṃ
matvā dhīro harṣaśokau jahāti
2.13
etacchrutvā samparigṛhya martyaḥ
pravṛhya dharmyamaṇumetamāpya
sa modate modanīyaṃ hi labdhvā
vivṛtaṃ sadma naciketasaṃ manye

2.10I know that the treasure is not lasting, for the steady is not reached by things that do not stay. Therefore I laid the Nachiketa fire, and by things that do not last I have reached what lasts. The end of desire, the ground of the world, the endlessness of the rite, the far shore of fearlessness, the wide-praised greatness, the standing-place — seeing these, Nachiketa, you let them go, being steady. Hard to see, hidden, entered in, set in the cave, lying deep, ancient — thinking on that god by the discipline that turns on the self, the wise man leaves behind both joy and grief. Hearing this and taking hold of it, drawing out the subtle thing that is law, a mortal is glad, having got what is worth gladness. I take the house to be open for Nachiketa.

Yama's admission in verse 10 is remarkable for a god: he says he built his own position out of impermanent materials and knows the limit of what that buys. The phrase in verse 12 for where the Self is found, guha-hitam, set in the cave, is the standard Upanishadic location, and the cave is the heart. Verse 13 closes the frame of the interview; the door is open and the teaching proper begins.

अन्यत्र धर्मादन्यत्राधर्मादन्यत्रास्मात्कृताकृतात् ।
अन्यत्र भूताच्च भव्याच्च
यत्तत्पश्यसि तद्वद ॥ १४ ॥
सर्वे वेदा यत्पदमामनन्ति
तपांसि सर्वाणि च यद्वदन्ति ।
यदिच्छन्तो ब्रह्मचर्यं चरन्ति
तत्ते पदं सङ्ग्रहेण ब्रवीम्योमित्येतत् ॥ १५ ॥
एतद्ध्येवाक्षरं ब्रह्म एतद्ध्येवाक्षरं परम् ।
एतद्ध्येवाक्षरं ज्ञात्वा यो यदिच्छति तस्य तत् ॥ १६ ॥
एतदालम्बनं श्रेष्ठमेतदालम्बनं परम् ।
एतदालम्बनं ज्ञात्वा ब्रह्मलोके महीयते ॥ १७ ॥
anyatra dharmādanyatrādharmādanyatrāsmātkṛtākṛtāt
anyatra bhūtācca bhavyācca
yattatpaśyasi tadvada
2.15
sarve vedā yatpadamāmananti
tapāṃsi sarvāṇi ca yadvadanti
yadicchanto brahmacaryaṃ caranti
tatte padaṃ saṅgraheṇa bravīmyomityetat
2.16
etaddhyevākṣaraṃ brahma etaddhyevākṣaraṃ param
etaddhyevākṣaraṃ jñātvā yo yadicchati tasya tat
2.17
etadālambanaṃ śreṣṭhametadālambanaṃ param
etadālambanaṃ jñātvā brahmaloke mahīyate

2.14Other than the right and other than the wrong, other than this done and not done, other than what has been and what is to be — what you see as that, tell me. The step that all the Vedas speak of, that all austerities declare, wanting which men live the disciplined life — that step I will tell you in brief. It is OM. This syllable is Brahman; this syllable is the highest. Knowing this syllable, whatever one wants is his. This is the best support; this is the highest support. Knowing this support, one is great in the world of Brahman.

The boy's question in verse 14 asks for something outside every pair the moral vocabulary has, including the pair of done and not done, and the answer is a sound rather than a description. That is the Katha's way of making the same point the Mandukya will make at length: what is beyond every category can be held by a syllable and not by a sentence.

न जायते म्रियते वा विपश्चिन्नायं कुतश्चिन्न बभूव कश्चित् ।
अजो नित्यः शाश्वतोऽयं पुराणो
न हन्यते हन्यमाने शरीरे ॥ १८ ॥
हन्ता चेन्मन्यते हन्तुं हतश्चेन्मन्यते हतम् ।
उभौ तौ न विजानीतो नायं हन्ति न हन्यते ॥ १९ ॥
अणोरणीयान्महतो महीयानात्माऽस्य जन्तोर्निहितो गुहायाम् ।
तमक्रतुः पश्यति वीतशोको
धातुप्रसादान्महिमानमात्मनः ॥ २० ॥
na jāyate mriyate vā vipaścinnāyaṃ kutaścinna babhūva kaścit
ajo nityaḥ śāśvato'yaṃ purāṇo
na hanyate hanyamāne śarīre
2.19
hantā cenmanyate hantuṃ hataścenmanyate hatam
ubhau tau na vijānīto nāyaṃ hanti na hanyate
2.20
aṇoraṇīyānmahato mahīyānātmā'sya jantornihito guhāyām
tamakratuḥ paśyati vītaśoko
dhātuprasādānmahimānamātmanaḥ

2.18The knower is not born and does not die. He has not come from anywhere and has not become anyone. Unborn, lasting, everlasting, ancient, he is not slain when the body is slain. If the slayer thinks to slay, and if the slain thinks himself slain, neither of them understands. This one does not slay and is not slain. Smaller than the small, greater than the great, the Self is set in the hidden place of this creature. Free of craving, free of sorrow, one sees the greatness of the Self by the grace of the ordainer.

Verses 18 and 19 are the two the Bhagavad Gita takes over almost unchanged into its second chapter, where they are spoken on a battlefield and have been read ever since as an argument about killing. In the Katha they are spoken to a boy who has been given away to Death, and they answer his question and no other: what is asked is whether anything survives, and the answer is that the thing in question was never in the class of things that begin.

The last line of verse 20 turns on a variant that the tradition has never settled. Read dhatuh prasadat, it means by the grace of the creator; read dhatu prasadat, it means by the settling of the constituents, that is, of the senses. Nagler's remark is that it makes remarkably little difference, since what grace does is still the senses.

आसीनो दूरं व्रजति शयानो याति सर्वतः ।
कस्तं मदामदं देवं मदन्यो ज्ञातुमर्हति ॥ २१ ॥
अशरीरं शरीरेष्वनवस्थेष्ववस्थितम् ।
महान्तं विभुमात्मानं मत्वा धीरो न शोचति ॥ २२ ॥
नायमात्मा प्रवचनेन लभ्यो
न मेधया न बहुना श्रुतेन ।
यमेवैष वृणुते तेन लभ्यः
तस्यैष आत्मा विवृणुते तनूं स्वाम् ॥ २३ ॥
नाविरतो दुश्चरितान्नाशान्तो नासमाहितः ।
नाशान्तमानसो वाऽपि प्रज्ञानेनैनमाप्नुयात् ॥ २४ ॥
āsīno dūraṃ vrajati śayāno yāti sarvataḥ
kastaṃ madāmadaṃ devaṃ madanyo jñātumarhati
2.22
aśarīraṃ śarīreṣvanavastheṣvavasthitam
mahāntaṃ vibhumātmānaṃ matvā dhīro na śocati
2.23
nāyamātmā pravacanena labhyo
na medhayā na bahunā śrutena
yamevaiṣa vṛṇute tena labhyaḥ
tasyaiṣa ātmā vivṛṇute tanūṃ svām
2.24
nāvirato duścaritānnāśānto nāsamāhitaḥ
nāśāntamānaso vā'pi prajñānenainamāpnuyāt

2.21Sitting, it travels far; lying down, it goes everywhere. Who but I is fit to know that god, who is joy and no joy? Bodiless among bodies, standing firm among the unstable, great and everywhere — thinking on that Self, the wise man does not grieve. This Self is not to be won by teaching, nor by intelligence, nor by much hearing. It is won by the one whom it chooses; to that one the Self uncovers its own body. Not one who has not stopped from bad conduct, not one unquiet, not one uncollected, not one whose mind is not at peace can reach it by knowing.

Verse 23 is the harder statement, and the alternative reading is worth having: it can also be construed as saying that the Self is won by the one who chooses it, which is equally at home in the collection. Verse 24 is its practical counterweight, and it is a list of disqualifications rather than a list of qualifications. Conduct comes first on the list, before quiet and before concentration.

यस्य ब्रह्म च क्षत्रं च उभे भवत ओदनः ।
मृत्युर्यस्योपसेचनं क इत्था वेद यत्र सः ॥ २५ ॥
yasya brahma ca kṣatraṃ ca ubhe bhavata odanaḥ
mṛtyuryasyopasecanaṃ ka itthā veda yatra saḥ

2.25For whom the priesthood and the nobility are both a dish of rice, and death a sauce upon it — who knows in that way where he is?

The last verse of the chapter is the most violent image in the text. Everything a society reckons as power is food for what the boy is asking about, and what seasons the food is death. The line is meant to close off the thought that the Self is a very large item among items.

ऋतं पिबन्तौ सुकृतस्य लोके
गुहां प्रविष्टौ परमे परार्धे ।
छायातपौ ब्रह्मविदो वदन्ति
पञ्चाग्नयो ये च त्रिणाचिकेताः ॥ १ ॥
यः सेतुरीजानानामक्षरं ब्रह्म यत् परम् ।
अभयं तितीर्षतां पारं नाचिकेतं शकेमहि ॥ २ ॥
ṛtaṃ pibantau sukṛtasya loke
guhāṃ praviṣṭau parame parārdhe
chāyātapau brahmavido vadanti
pañcāgnayo ye ca triṇāciketāḥ
3.2
yaḥ seturījānānāmakṣaraṃ brahma yat param
abhayaṃ titīrṣatāṃ pāraṃ nāciketaṃ śakemahi

3.1Two drink the law in the world of good works, entered into the cave, in the highest place beyond. The knowers of Brahman call them shadow and light, as do those who lay the five fires and the triple Nachiketa fire. May we be able to lay the Nachiketa fire, the bridge for those who sacrifice, and the deathless highest Brahman, fearless, for those who wish to cross to the far shore.

The two in the cave have been read in two ways for as long as there has been a commentary. Shankara takes them as the individual and the supreme Self, the one that eats the fruit of works and the one that only watches; others take the second as the intellect. Both drink rita, the order of things, and only one of them casts a shadow.

आत्मानं रथिनं विद्धि शरीरं रथमेव तु ।
बुद्धिं तु सारथिं विद्धि मनः प्रग्रहमेव च ॥ ३ ॥
इन्द्रियाणि हयानाहुर्विषयां स्तेषु गोचरान् ।
आत्मेन्द्रियमनोयुक्तं भोक्तेत्याहुर्मनीषिणः ॥ ४ ॥
यस्त्वविज्ञानवान्भवत्ययुक्तेन मनसा सदा ।
तस्येन्द्रियाण्यवश्यानि दुष्टाश्वा इव सारथेः ॥ ५ ॥
यस्तु विज्ञानवान्भवति युक्तेन मनसा सदा ।
तस्येन्द्रियाणि वश्यानि सदश्वा इव सारथेः ॥ ६ ॥
ātmānaṃ rathinaṃ viddhi śarīraṃ rathameva tu
buddhiṃ tu sārathiṃ viddhi manaḥ pragrahameva ca
3.4
indriyāṇi hayānāhurviṣayāṃ steṣu gocarān
ātmendriyamanoyuktaṃ bhoktetyāhurmanīṣiṇaḥ
3.5
yastvavijñānavānbhavatyayuktena manasā sadā
tasyendriyāṇyavaśyāni duṣṭāśvā iva sāratheḥ
3.6
yastu vijñānavānbhavati yuktena manasā sadā
tasyendriyāṇi vaśyāni sadaśvā iva sāratheḥ

3.3Know the Self as the rider in the chariot, and the body as the chariot. Know the intellect as the driver, and the mind as the reins. The senses, they say, are the horses; the objects of sense are their roads. The Self joined to body and senses and mind is what the wise call the enjoyer. The one who is without understanding, whose mind is never yoked — his senses are not to be controlled, like the bad horses of a driver. The one who has understanding, whose mind is always yoked — his senses are to be controlled, like the good horses of a driver.

यस्त्वविज्ञानवान्भवत्यमनस्कः सदाऽशुचिः ।
न स तत्पदमाप्नोति संसारं चाधिगच्छति ॥ ७ ॥
यस्तु विज्ञानवान्भवति समनस्कः सदा शुचिः ।
स तु तत्पदमाप्नोति यस्माद्भूयो न जायते ॥ ८ ॥
विज्ञानसारथिर्यस्तु मनः प्रग्रहवान्नरः ।
सोऽध्वनः पारमाप्नोति तद्विष्णोः परमं पदम् ॥ ९ ॥
yastvavijñānavānbhavatyamanaskaḥ sadā'śuciḥ
na sa tatpadamāpnoti saṃsāraṃ cādhigacchati
3.8
yastu vijñānavānbhavati samanaskaḥ sadā śuciḥ
sa tu tatpadamāpnoti yasmādbhūyo na jāyate
3.9
vijñānasārathiryastu manaḥ pragrahavānnaraḥ
so'dhvanaḥ pāramāpnoti tadviṣṇoḥ paramaṃ padam

3.7The one who is without understanding, thoughtless, always unclean, does not reach that step, and comes to the round of birth and death. The one who has understanding, thoughtful, always clean, reaches that step, from which he is not born again. But the man who has understanding for his driver and holds the reins of the mind reaches the end of the road, that highest step of Vishnu.

The model is precise about who does what. The rider owns the journey and does not steer; the intellect steers and does not own; the mind is not a faculty but a linkage, the thing through which the driver's decision reaches the horses, and it is the part that can be slack. The word for the roads the horses travel, gocarah, means cow pastures, and it became a technical term in Sankhya. Plato uses the same image in the Phaedrus, less worked out. Two verses later the text turns the chariot into a ladder.

इन्द्रियेभ्यः परा ह्यर्था अर्थेभ्यश्च परं मनः ।
मनसस्तु परा बुद्धिर्बुद्धेरात्मा महान्परः ॥ १० ॥
महतः परमव्यक्तमव्यक्तात्पुरुषः परः ।
पुरुषान्न परं किंचित्सा काष्ठा सा परा गतिः ॥ ११ ॥
एष सर्वेषु भूतेषु गूढोऽऽत्मा न प्रकाशते ।
दृश्यते त्वग्र्यया बुद्ध्या सूक्ष्मया सूक्ष्मदर्शिभिः ॥ १२ ॥
यच्छेद्वाङ्मनसी प्राज्ञस्तद्यच्छेज्ज्ञान आत्मनि ।
ज्ञानमात्मनि महति नियच्छेत्तद्यच्छेच्छान्त आत्मनि ॥ १३ ॥
indriyebhyaḥ parā hyarthā arthebhyaśca paraṃ manaḥ
manasastu parā buddhirbuddherātmā mahānparaḥ
3.11
mahataḥ paramavyaktamavyaktātpuruṣaḥ paraḥ
puruṣānna paraṃ kiṃcitsā kāṣṭhā sā parā gatiḥ
3.12
eṣa sarveṣu bhūteṣu gūḍho''tmā na prakāśate
dṛśyate tvagryayā buddhyā sūkṣmayā sūkṣmadarśibhiḥ
3.13
yacchedvāṅmanasī prājñastadyacchejjñāna ātmani
jñānamātmani mahati niyacchettadyacchecchānta ātmani

3.10Beyond the senses are the objects; beyond the objects is the mind; beyond the mind is the intellect; beyond the intellect is the great Self. Beyond the great is the unmanifest; beyond the unmanifest is the Person. Beyond the Person there is nothing at all: that is the limit, that is the highest goal. Hidden in all beings, this Self does not shine out. It is seen by those who see what is fine, with a fine and pointed understanding. Let the wise man hold back speech in the mind; let him hold that in the knowing self; let him hold knowing in the great self; let him hold that in the self that is at peace.

The order in verse 10 is the one that surprises, and Nagler insists it is what the text actually says: the senses do not come first and the objects second, but the other way about. Sense objects are ranked above the senses and below the mind, which is to say that the world as encountered is already a product of the equipment that encounters it. Verse 13 turns the same ladder into an instruction by running it upward one rung at a time, each faculty folded into the one above it.

उत्तिष्ठत जाग्रत
प्राप्य वरान्निबोधत ।
क्षुरस्य धारा निशिता दुरत्यया
दुर्गं पथस्तत्कवयो वदन्ति ॥ १४ ॥
अशब्दमस्पर्शमरूपमव्ययं
तथाऽरसं नित्यमगन्धवच्च यत् ।
अनाद्यनन्तं महतः परं ध्रुवं
निचाय्य तन्मृत्युमुखात् प्रमुच्यते ॥ १५ ॥
नाचिकेतमुपाख्यानं मृत्युप्रोक्तं सनातनम् ।
उक्त्वा श्रुत्वा च मेधावी ब्रह्मलोके महीयते ॥ १६ ॥
य इमं परमं गुह्यं श्रावयेद् ब्रह्मसंसदि ।
प्रयतः श्राद्धकाले वा तदानन्त्याय कल्पते ।
तदानन्त्याय कल्पत इति ॥ १७ ॥
uttiṣṭhata jāgrata
prāpya varānnibodhata
kṣurasya dhārā niśitā duratyayā
durgaṃ pathastatkavayo vadanti
3.15
aśabdamasparśamarūpamavyayaṃ
tathā'rasaṃ nityamagandhavacca yat
anādyanantaṃ mahataḥ paraṃ dhruvaṃ
nicāyya tanmṛtyumukhāt pramucyate
3.16
nāciketamupākhyānaṃ mṛtyuproktaṃ sanātanam
uktvā śrutvā ca medhāvī brahmaloke mahīyate
3.17
ya imaṃ paramaṃ guhyaṃ śrāvayed brahmasaṃsadi
prayataḥ śrāddhakāle vā tadānantyāya kalpate
tadānantyāya kalpata iti

3.14Arise, awake; having found the great ones, learn. Sharp as a razor's edge and hard to cross: the poets call that path hard to go. Soundless, untouchable, formless, undecaying; likewise tasteless, lasting, and without smell; without beginning or end, beyond the great, firm — knowing that, one is released from the mouth of death. The everlasting story of Nachiketa, told by Death — telling it and hearing it, the wise man is great in the world of Brahman. Whoever recites this highest secret in an assembly of those who know Brahman, or at the time of the offering to the dead, being pure, is fitted for endlessness — fitted for endlessness.

Verse 14 is the line the Katha is quoted for, and Easwaran takes its severity at face value: the ascent is dangerous, which is why the relation with a teacher who has been up and come back matters more in this collection than anything else. Verses 16 and 17 close the third chapter with the formula that marks the end of a unit of recitation, and in editions that divide the text into two parts of three chapters they close the first part.

पराञ्चि खानि व्यतृणत् स्वयम्भूस्तस्मात्पराङ्पश्यति नान्तरात्मन् ।
कश्चिद्धीरः प्रत्यगात्मानमैक्षदावृत्तचक्षुरमृतत्वमिच्छन् ॥ १ ॥
पराचः कामाननुयन्ति बालास्ते मृत्योर्यन्ति विततस्य पाशम् ।
अथ धीरा अमृतत्वं विदित्वा
ध्रुवमध्रुवेष्विह न प्रार्थयन्ते ॥ २ ॥
येन रूपं रसं गन्धं शब्दान् स्पर्शांश्च मैथुनान् ।
एतेनैव विजानाति किमत्र परिशिष्यते । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ३ ॥
स्वप्नान्तं जागरितान्तं चोभौ येनानुपश्यति ।
महान्तं विभुमात्मानं मत्वा धीरो न शोचति ॥ ४ ॥
parāñci khāni vyatṛṇat svayambhūstasmātparāṅpaśyati nāntarātman
kaściddhīraḥ pratyagātmānamaikṣadāvṛttacakṣuramṛtatvamicchan
4.2
parācaḥ kāmānanuyanti bālāste mṛtyoryanti vitatasya pāśam
atha dhīrā amṛtatvaṃ viditvā
dhruvamadhruveṣviha na prārthayante
4.3
yena rūpaṃ rasaṃ gandhaṃ śabdān sparśāṃśca maithunān
etenaiva vijānāti kimatra pariśiṣyate | etadvai tat
4.4
svapnāntaṃ jāgaritāntaṃ cobhau yenānupaśyati
mahāntaṃ vibhumātmānaṃ matvā dhīro na śocati

4.1The self-existent pierced the openings outward; therefore one looks outward and not into the self within. Some wise man, wanting the deathless, turned his eyes around and saw the Self within. Children run after outward desires and walk into the snare of wide-spread death. The wise, knowing the deathless, do not look here among unstable things for what stands. By which one knows form and taste and smell and sounds and touches and coupling — by this alone one knows. What is left over here? This is That. By which one sees both the end of dream and the end of waking — knowing that as the great and pervading Self, the wise man does not grieve.

The fourth chapter opens with a piece of anatomy used as an argument. The openings of the senses are cut outward, so the natural direction of attention is outward, and the one operation the whole collection asks for is described as avritta-chakshus, the eye turned back. The refrain that closes verse 3 and returns through the chapter, etad vai tat, this is That, is the chapter's signature; each verse points at something and says the pointing is the same in every case.

य इमं मध्वदं वेद आत्मानं जीवमन्तिकात् ।
ईशानं भूतभव्यस्य न ततो विजुगुप्सते । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ५ ॥
यः पूर्वं तपसो जातमद्भ्यः पूर्वमजायत ।
गुहां प्रविश्य तिष्ठन्तं यो भूतेभिर्व्यपश्यत । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ६ ॥
या प्राणेन संभवत्यदितिर्देवतामयी ।
गुहां प्रविश्य तिष्ठन्तीं या भूतेभिर्व्यजायत । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ७ ॥
अरण्योर्निहितो जातवेदा गर्भ इव सुभृतो गर्भिणीभिः ।
दिवे दिवे ईड्यो जागृवद्भिर्हविष्मद्भिर्मनुष्येभिरग्निः । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ८ ॥
ya imaṃ madhvadaṃ veda ātmānaṃ jīvamantikāt
īśānaṃ bhūtabhavyasya na tato vijugupsate | etadvai tat
4.6
yaḥ pūrvaṃ tapaso jātamadbhyaḥ pūrvamajāyata
guhāṃ praviśya tiṣṭhantaṃ yo bhūtebhirvyapaśyata | etadvai tat
4.7
yā prāṇena saṃbhavatyaditirdevatāmayī
guhāṃ praviśya tiṣṭhantīṃ yā bhūtebhirvyajāyata | etadvai tat
4.8
araṇyornihito jātavedā garbha iva subhṛto garbhiṇībhiḥ
dive dive īḍyo jāgṛvadbhirhaviṣmadbhirmanuṣyebhiragniḥ | etadvai tat

4.5Whoever knows this Self, the eater of honey, the living one, from close by, the ruler of what was and what will be, shrinks from nothing after that. This is That. Whoever sees the one born before the heat, born before the waters, who has entered the cave and stands there, looking out through the beings — this is That. That which comes to be through breath, the infinite mother of the gods, who has entered the cave and stands there, who was born through the beings — this is That. The knower of all born hidden in the two fire-sticks, carried like a well-tended child by pregnant women, to be worshipped day after day by waking men who bring the offering — this is That.

The honey-eater in verse 5 is the individual soul, taking the taste of its own works, and the same phrase runs through the Mundaka and the Shvetashvatara as one half of the image of two birds on a tree. Verse 8 puts the Self where fire is before it is struck: present in the two sticks, invisible in both, produced by neither alone.

यतश्चोदेति सूर्योऽस्तं यत्र च गच्छति ।
तं देवाः सर्वेऽर्पितास्तदु नात्येति कश्चन । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ९ ॥
यदेवेह तदमुत्र यदमुत्र तदन्विह ।
मृत्योः स मृत्युमाप्नोति य इह नानेव पश्यति ॥ १० ॥
मनसैवेदमाप्तव्यं नेह नानाऽस्ति किंचन ।
मृत्योः स मृत्युं गच्छति य इह नानेव पश्यति ॥ ११ ॥
अङ्गुष्ठमात्रः पुरुषो मध्य आत्मनि तिष्ठति ।
ईशानं भूतभव्यस्य न ततो विजुगुप्सते । एतद्वै तत् ॥ १२ ॥
yataścodeti sūryo'staṃ yatra ca gacchati
taṃ devāḥ sarve'rpitāstadu nātyeti kaścana | etadvai tat
4.10
yadeveha tadamutra yadamutra tadanviha
mṛtyoḥ sa mṛtyumāpnoti ya iha nāneva paśyati
4.11
manasaivedamāptavyaṃ neha nānā'sti kiṃcana
mṛtyoḥ sa mṛtyuṃ gacchati ya iha nāneva paśyati
4.12
aṅguṣṭhamātraḥ puruṣo madhya ātmani tiṣṭhati
īśānaṃ bhūtabhavyasya na tato vijugupsate | etadvai tat

4.9From where the sun rises and where it goes down — on that all the gods are fixed, and nobody goes past it. This is That. What is here is there, and what is there is here as well. He goes from death to death who sees anything like difference here. This is to be reached by the mind alone. There is no difference at all here. He goes from death to death who sees anything like difference here. The Person the size of a thumb stands in the middle of the self, ruler of what was and what will be. From that one shrinks no more. This is That.

Verses 10 and 11 are the same sentence twice with the second half changed, and the pair is the clearest statement of non-duality in the Katha. The thumb-sized person is the text's oddest image and its most repeated. It is not a homunculus; the measure is a way of locating the Self in the body without granting it a body.

अङ्गुष्ठमात्रः पुरुषो ज्योतिरिवाधूमकः ।
ईशानो भूतभव्यस्य स एवाद्य स उ श्वः । एतद्वै तत् ॥ १३ ॥
यथोदकं दुर्गे वृष्टं पर्वतेषु विधावति ।
एवं धर्मान् पृथक् पश्यंस्तानेवानुविधावति ॥ १४ ॥
यथोदकं शुद्धे शुद्धमासिक्तं तादृगेव भवति ।
एवं मुनेर्विजानत आत्मा भवति गौतम ॥ १५ ॥
aṅguṣṭhamātraḥ puruṣo jyotirivādhūmakaḥ
īśāno bhūtabhavyasya sa evādya sa u śvaḥ | etadvai tat
4.14
yathodakaṃ durge vṛṣṭaṃ parvateṣu vidhāvati
evaṃ dharmān pṛthak paśyaṃstānevānuvidhāvati
4.15
yathodakaṃ śuddhe śuddhamāsiktaṃ tādṛgeva bhavati
evaṃ munervijānata ātmā bhavati gautama

4.13The Person the size of a thumb is like a light without smoke, ruler of what was and what will be. He is today and he is tomorrow. This is That. As water rained on a height runs away among the hills, so one who sees the laws as separate runs after them. As pure water poured into pure water becomes just that, so becomes the self of the sage who knows, Gautama.

The two water images are placed together and point in opposite directions. Rain on a ridge is divided by the shape it falls on and scatters; water poured into water takes on no shape at all and cannot afterwards be told from what it was poured into. Between them they give the text's account of what is lost and what is not lost when separateness ends.

पुरमेकादशद्वारमजस्यावक्रचेतसः ।
अनुष्ठाय न शोचति विमुक्तश्च विमुच्यते । एतद्वै तत् ॥ १ ॥
हंसः शुचिषद्वसुरन्तरिक्षसद्होता वेदिषदतिथिर्दुरोणसत् ।
नृषद्वरसदृतसद्व्योमसद्
अब्जा गोजा ऋतजा अद्रिजा ऋतं बृहत् ॥ २ ॥
ऊर्ध्वं प्राणमुन्नयत्यपानं प्रत्यगस्यति ।
मध्ये वामनमासीनं विश्वे देवा उपासते ॥ ३ ॥
puramekādaśadvāramajasyāvakracetasaḥ
anuṣṭhāya na śocati vimuktaśca vimucyate | etadvai tat
5.2
haṃsaḥ śuciṣadvasurantarikṣasadhotā vediṣadatithirduroṇasat
nṛṣadvarasadṛtasadvyomasad
abjā gojā ṛtajā adrijā ṛtaṃ bṛhat
5.3
ūrdhvaṃ prāṇamunnayatyapānaṃ pratyagasyati
madhye vāmanamāsīnaṃ viśve devā upāsate

5.1Of the unborn whose thought is not crooked there is a city with eleven gates. Attending to it one does not grieve; released, one is free. This is That. The swan in the clear sky, the bright one in the middle air, the priest at the altar, the guest in the house; among men, among gods, in the order of things, in the sky; born of water, born of earth, born of the order, born of the rock — the great order. He leads the upward breath up and pushes the downward breath down. The dwarf seated in the middle is what all the gods worship.

The eleven gates are the body's openings, counted as the seven in the head, the two below, the navel and the opening at the crown. Verse 2 is lifted from a Rig Veda hymn and is the oldest-sounding thing in the Katha; verse 3 puts the same occupant in the middle of the breathing, between the breath that goes up and the breath that goes down.

अस्य विस्रंसमानस्य शरीरस्थस्य देहिनः ।
देहाद्विमुच्यमानस्य किमत्र परिशिष्यते । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ४ ॥
न प्राणेन नापानेन मर्त्यो जीवति कश्चन ।
इतरेण तु जीवन्ति यस्मिन्नेतावुपाश्रितौ ॥ ५ ॥
हन्त त इदं प्रवक्ष्यामि गुह्यं ब्रह्म सनातनम् ।
यथा च मरणं प्राप्य आत्मा भवति गौतम ॥ ६ ॥
योनिमन्ये प्रपद्यन्ते शरीरत्वाय देहिनः ।
स्थाणुमन्येऽनुसंयन्ति यथाकर्म यथाश्रुतम् ॥ ७ ॥
asya visraṃsamānasya śarīrasthasya dehinaḥ
dehādvimucyamānasya kimatra pariśiṣyate | etadvai tat
5.5
na prāṇena nāpānena martyo jīvati kaścana
itareṇa tu jīvanti yasminnetāvupāśritau
5.6
hanta ta idaṃ pravakṣyāmi guhyaṃ brahma sanātanam
yathā ca maraṇaṃ prāpya ātmā bhavati gautama
5.7
yonimanye prapadyante śarīratvāya dehinaḥ
sthāṇumanye'nusaṃyanti yathākarma yathāśrutam

5.4When this body-dweller, seated in the body, comes loose and is released from the body, what is left over here? This is That. No mortal lives by the upward breath or the downward breath. Men live by another, on which these two depend. I will tell you the secret, the everlasting Brahman, and how it is with the self when it has come to dying, Gautama. Some body-dwellers enter a womb to take a body; others go into the stationary, according to their works and according to what they have learned.

Verse 5 separates life from breathing, which is the distinction on which the Prashna will build a whole chapter. Verse 7 gives the Katha's one sentence on what decides a rebirth, and it names two things: yatha-karma, according to what was done, and yatha-shrutam, according to what was heard and learned. Knowledge is treated there as a cause and not only as a result.

य एष सुप्तेषु जागर्ति कामं कामं पुरुषो निर्मिमाणः ।
तदेव शुक्रं तद्ब्रह्म तदेवामृतमुच्यते ।
तस्मिंल्लोकाः श्रिताः सर्वे तदु नात्येति कश्चन । एतद्वै तत् ॥ ८ ॥
अग्निर्यथैको भुवनं प्रविष्टो
रूपं रूपं प्रतिरूपो बभूव ।
एकस्तथा सर्वभूतान्तरात्मा
रूपं रूपं प्रतिरूपो बहिश्च ॥ ९ ॥
वायुर्यथैको भुवनं प्रविष्टो
रूपं रूपं प्रतिरूपो बभूव ।
एकस्तथा सर्वभूतान्तरात्मा
रूपं रूपं प्रतिरूपो बहिश्च ॥ १० ॥
सूर्यो यथा सर्वलोकस्य चक्षुः
न लिप्यते चाक्षुषैर्बाह्यदोषैः ।
एकस्तथा सर्वभूतान्तरात्मा
न लिप्यते लोकदुःखेन बाह्यः ॥ ११ ॥
ya eṣa supteṣu jāgarti kāmaṃ kāmaṃ puruṣo nirmimāṇaḥ
tadeva śukraṃ tadbrahma tadevāmṛtamucyate
tasmiṃllokāḥ śritāḥ sarve tadu nātyeti kaścana | etadvai tat
5.9
agniryathaiko bhuvanaṃ praviṣṭo
rūpaṃ rūpaṃ pratirūpo babhūva
ekastathā sarvabhūtāntarātmā
rūpaṃ rūpaṃ pratirūpo bahiśca
5.10
vāyuryathaiko bhuvanaṃ praviṣṭo
rūpaṃ rūpaṃ pratirūpo babhūva
ekastathā sarvabhūtāntarātmā
rūpaṃ rūpaṃ pratirūpo bahiśca
5.11
sūryo yathā sarvalokasya cakṣuḥ
na lipyate cākṣuṣairbāhyadoṣaiḥ
ekastathā sarvabhūtāntarātmā
na lipyate lokaduḥkhena bāhyaḥ

5.8The Person who stays awake in those who sleep, shaping desire after desire — that is the bright, that is Brahman, that alone is called the deathless. On it all the worlds rest, and nobody goes past it. This is That. As one fire, having entered the world, takes the shape of every shape, so the one inner Self of all beings takes the shape of every shape, and is outside them. As one wind, having entered the world, takes the shape of every shape, so the one inner Self of all beings takes the shape of every shape, and is outside them. As the sun, the eye of the whole world, is not stained by the faults of eyes outside it, so the one inner Self of all beings is not stained by the sorrow of the world, being outside it.

The three comparisons in verses 9 to 11 are a set, and the third is not a repetition. Fire and wind show how one thing can be many without dividing; the sun shows the limit of what contact costs. What the eye looks at does not soil the light by which it looks, and the claim carried across is that the Self is present in suffering without being an instance of it.

एको वशी सर्वभूतान्तरात्मा
एकं रूपं बहुधा यः करोति ।
तमात्मस्थं येऽनुपश्यन्ति धीराः
तेषां सुखं शाश्वतं नेतरेषाम् ॥ १२ ॥
नित्योऽनित्यानां चेतनश्चेतनानाम्
एको बहूनां यो विदधाति कामान् ।
तमात्मस्थं येऽनुपश्यन्ति धीराः
तेषां शान्तिः शाश्वती नेतरेषाम् ॥ १३ ॥
तदेतदिति मन्यन्तेऽनिर्देश्यं परमं सुखम् ।
कथं नु तद्विजानीयां किमु भाति विभाति वा ॥ १४ ॥
न तत्र सूर्यो भाति न चन्द्रतारकं
नेमा विद्युतो भान्ति कुतोऽयमग्निः ।
तमेव भान्तमनुभाति सर्वं
तस्य भासा सर्वमिदं विभाति ॥ १५ ॥
eko vaśī sarvabhūtāntarātmā
ekaṃ rūpaṃ bahudhā yaḥ karoti
tamātmasthaṃ ye'nupaśyanti dhīrāḥ
teṣāṃ sukhaṃ śāśvataṃ netareṣām
5.13
nityo'nityānāṃ cetanaścetanānām
eko bahūnāṃ yo vidadhāti kāmān
tamātmasthaṃ ye'nupaśyanti dhīrāḥ
teṣāṃ śāntiḥ śāśvatī netareṣām
5.14
tadetaditi manyante'nirdeśyaṃ paramaṃ sukham
kathaṃ nu tadvijānīyāṃ kimu bhāti vibhāti vā
5.15
na tatra sūryo bhāti na candratārakaṃ
nemā vidyuto bhānti kuto'yamagniḥ
tameva bhāntamanubhāti sarvaṃ
tasya bhāsā sarvamidaṃ vibhāti

5.12The one ruler, the inner Self of all beings, who makes one form into many — the wise who see him standing in the self, and not others, have lasting happiness. The everlasting among things that pass, the conscious among the conscious, the one among many, who deals out desires — the wise who see him standing in the self, and not others, have lasting peace. They think: this is it, the highest happiness that cannot be pointed at. How then shall I know it? Does it shine, or does it shine by reflection? There the sun does not shine, nor the moon and stars, nor do these lightnings shine; how then this fire? All shine after it as it shines; by its light all this is lit.

Verse 14 is a student's question of the most practical kind — by what light would one see the thing that is being described — and verse 15 refuses the terms of the question rather than answering it. The verse is quoted more than any other line in the Katha, and the Mundaka and the Shvetashvatara both take it over word for word.

ऊर्ध्वमूलोऽवाक्शाख एषोऽश्वत्थः सनातनः ।
तदेव शुक्रं तद्ब्रह्म तदेवामृतमुच्यते ।
तस्मिंल्लोकाः श्रिताः सर्वे तदु नात्येति कश्चन । एतद्वै तत् ॥ १ ॥
ūrdhvamūlo'vākśākha eṣo'śvatthaḥ sanātanaḥ
tadeva śukraṃ tadbrahma tadevāmṛtamucyate
tasmiṃllokāḥ śritāḥ sarve tadu nātyeti kaścana | etadvai tat

6.1Roots above, branches below, this everlasting fig tree stands. That is the bright, that is Brahman, that alone is called the deathless. On it all the worlds rest, and nobody goes past it. This is That.

The inverted tree is an old image and the Gita takes it over in its fifteenth chapter. What it encodes is a claim about the direction of dependence: the visible part, the part that grows and is cut and grows again, is downstream of a source that does not appear among the things it supports.

यदिदं किं च जगत् सर्वं प्राण एजति निःसृतम् ।
महद्भयं वज्रमुद्यतं य एतद्विदुरमृतास्ते भवन्ति ॥ २ ॥
भयादस्याग्निस्तपति भयात्तपति सूर्यः ।
भयादिन्द्रश्च वायुश्च मृत्युर्धावति पञ्चमः ॥ ३ ॥
इह चेदशकद्बोद्धुं प्राक्षरीरस्य विस्रसः ।
ततः सर्गेषु लोकेषु शरीरत्वाय कल्पते ॥ ४ ॥
yadidaṃ kiṃ ca jagat sarvaṃ prāṇa ejati niḥsṛtam
mahadbhayaṃ vajramudyataṃ ya etadviduramṛtāste bhavanti
6.3
bhayādasyāgnistapati bhayāttapati sūryaḥ
bhayādindraśca vāyuśca mṛtyurdhāvati pañcamaḥ
6.4
iha cedaśakadboddhuṃ prākṣarīrasya visrasaḥ
tataḥ sargeṣu lokeṣu śarīratvāya kalpate

6.2All this world, whatever there is, moves in the breath that has come forth from it. It is a great terror, a raised thunderbolt. Those who know that become deathless. For fear of it the fire burns; for fear the sun burns; for fear Indra and the wind, and death as the fifth, run on. If one has been able to wake to it here, before the body falls away, then he is fit to take a body in the worlds that are made.

The terror of verse 2 is not a threat but a description of scale, and verse 3 turns the machinery of nature into obedience: what looks like the regularity of fire and sun is read as fear, which is the archaic way of saying that nothing in the series is self-moving. Verse 4 states the deadline the Kena also states. The work is to be done here, before the body goes.

यथाऽऽदर्शे तथाऽऽत्मनि यथा स्वप्ने तथा पितृलोके ।
यथाऽप्सु परीव ददृशे तथा गन्धर्वलोके
छायातपयोरिव ब्रह्मलोके ॥ ५ ॥
इन्द्रियाणां पृथग्भावमुदयास्तमयौ च यत् ।
पृथगुत्पद्यमानानां मत्वा धीरो न शोचति ॥ ६ ॥
इन्द्रियेभ्यः परं मनो मनसः सत्त्वमुत्तमम् ।
सत्त्वादधि महानात्मा महतोऽव्यक्तमुत्तमम् ॥ ७ ॥
अव्यक्तात्तु परः पुरुषो व्यापकोऽलिङ्ग एव च ।
यं ज्ञात्वा मुच्यते जन्तुरमृतत्वं च गच्छति ॥ ८ ॥
yathā''darśe tathā''tmani yathā svapne tathā pitṛloke
yathā'psu parīva dadṛśe tathā gandharvaloke
chāyātapayoriva brahmaloke
6.6
indriyāṇāṃ pṛthagbhāvamudayāstamayau ca yat
pṛthagutpadyamānānāṃ matvā dhīro na śocati
6.7
indriyebhyaḥ paraṃ mano manasaḥ sattvamuttamam
sattvādadhi mahānātmā mahato'vyaktamuttamam
6.8
avyaktāttu paraḥ puruṣo vyāpako'liṅga eva ca
yaṃ jñātvā mucyate janturamṛtatvaṃ ca gacchati

6.5As in a mirror, so in the self; as in a dream, so in the world of the fathers; as seen in water, so in the world of the spirits; as shadow and light, so in the world of Brahman. Knowing the separate nature of the senses, and their rising and setting as things that come to be apart, the wise man does not grieve. Beyond the senses is the mind; beyond the mind is the best of what is; beyond that is the great Self; beyond the great is the unmanifest. Beyond the unmanifest is the Person, all-pervading and without a mark. Knowing him, a creature is freed and goes to the deathless.

Verse 5 grades the clarity of the vision available in four conditions, and it is the closest thing in the Upanishads to a scale of resolution: sharp as a reflection in a mirror, blurred as a dream, broken as a face seen in water, and in the world of Brahman as distinct as light is from shadow. Verses 7 and 8 repeat the ladder of the third chapter with one rung renamed.

न संदृशे तिष्ठति रूपमस्य
न चक्षुषा पश्यति कश्चनैनम् ।
हृदा मनीषा मनसाऽभिक्लृप्तो
य एतद्विदुरमृतास्ते भवन्ति ॥ ९ ॥
यदा पञ्चावतिष्ठन्ते ज्ञानानि मनसा सह ।
बुद्धिश्च न विचेष्टते तामाहुः परमां गतिम् ॥ १० ॥
तां योगमिति मन्यन्ते स्थिरामिन्द्रियधारणाम् ।
अप्रमत्तस्तदा भवति योगो हि प्रभवाप्ययौ ॥ ११ ॥
नैव वाचा न मनसा प्राप्तुं शक्यो न चक्षुषा ।
अस्तीति ब्रुवतोऽन्यत्र कथं तदुपलभ्यते ॥ १२ ॥
na saṃdṛśe tiṣṭhati rūpamasya
na cakṣuṣā paśyati kaścanainam
hṛdā manīṣā manasā'bhiklṛpto
ya etadviduramṛtāste bhavanti
6.10
yadā pañcāvatiṣṭhante jñānāni manasā saha
buddhiśca na viceṣṭate tāmāhuḥ paramāṃ gatim
6.11
tāṃ yogamiti manyante sthirāmindriyadhāraṇām
apramattastadā bhavati yogo hi prabhavāpyayau
6.12
naiva vācā na manasā prāptuṃ śakyo na cakṣuṣā
astīti bruvato'nyatra kathaṃ tadupalabhyate

6.9His form does not stand in the range of sight; nobody sees him with the eye. He is fashioned by the heart, by the understanding, by the mind. Those who know that become deathless. When the five knowings come to a stand together with the mind, and the intellect does not stir, they call that the highest way. This they hold to be yoga: the steady holding of the senses. Then one is not careless, for yoga comes and goes. Not by speech, not by mind, not by the eye can it be reached. How is it to be grasped, other than by saying that it is?

The definition in verse 11 is guarded in a way that most definitions of yoga are not: the steady holding of the senses is called yoga, and in the same breath the text says that yoga arises and passes away. What is being described is a state that has to be maintained and that will lapse. Verse 12 states the minimum any account of it can contain, and the next verse takes that minimum as sufficient.

अस्तीत्येवोपलब्धव्यस्तत्त्वभावेन चोभयोः ।
अस्तीत्येवोपलब्धस्य तत्त्वभावः प्रसीदति ॥ १३ ॥
यदा सर्वे प्रमुच्यन्ते कामा येऽस्य हृदि श्रिताः ।
अथ मर्त्योऽमृतो भवत्यत्र ब्रह्म समश्नुते ॥ १४ ॥
यदा सर्वे प्रभिद्यन्ते हृदयस्येह ग्रन्थयः ।
अथ मर्त्योऽमृतो भवत्येतावद्ध्यनुशासनम् ॥ १५ ॥
शतं चैका च हृदयस्य नाड्यस्तासां मूर्धानमभिनिःसृतैका ।
तयोर्ध्वमायन्नमृतत्वमेति
विष्वङ्ङन्या उत्क्रमणे भवन्ति ॥ १६ ॥
astītyevopalabdhavyastattvabhāvena cobhayoḥ
astītyevopalabdhasya tattvabhāvaḥ prasīdati
6.14
yadā sarve pramucyante kāmā ye'sya hṛdi śritāḥ
atha martyo'mṛto bhavatyatra brahma samaśnute
6.15
yadā sarve prabhidyante hṛdayasyeha granthayaḥ
atha martyo'mṛto bhavatyetāvaddhyanuśāsanam
6.16
śataṃ caikā ca hṛdayasya nāḍyastāsāṃ mūrdhānamabhiniḥsṛtaikā
tayordhvamāyannamṛtatvameti
viṣvaṅṅanyā utkramaṇe bhavanti

6.13It is to be grasped as being, and by its being what it is in both. To one who has grasped it as being, its real nature becomes clear. When all the desires that are lodged in the heart are let go, then a mortal becomes immortal and reaches Brahman here. When all the knots of the heart are cut here, then a mortal becomes immortal. This much is the teaching. A hundred and one are the channels of the heart. One of them runs out through the crown. Going up by it one reaches the deathless; the others lead out in all directions.

Verse 15 says etavat, this much, and stops, and what it stops after is a metaphor: the knots are the places where the sense of a separate self is tied to something. Verse 16 is the only piece of physiology in the Katha and the source of a long later literature; the Prashna and the Chandogya both repeat the count of a hundred and one channels.

अङ्गुष्ठमात्रः पुरुषोऽन्तरात्मा
सदा जनानां हृदये संनिविष्टः ।
तं स्वाच्छरीरात्प्रवृहेन्मुञ्जादिवेषीकां धैर्येण ।
तं विद्याच्छुक्रममृतं तं विद्याच्छुक्रममृतमिति ॥ १७ ॥
मृत्युप्रोक्तां नचिकेतोऽथ लब्ध्वा
विद्यामेतां योगविधिं च कृत्स्नम् ।
ब्रह्मप्राप्तो विरजोऽभूद्विमृत्युरन्योऽप्येवं यो विदध्यात्ममेव ॥ १८ ॥
aṅguṣṭhamātraḥ puruṣo'ntarātmā
sadā janānāṃ hṛdaye saṃniviṣṭaḥ
taṃ svāccharīrātpravṛhenmuñjādiveṣīkāṃ dhairyeṇa
taṃ vidyācchukramamṛtaṃ taṃ vidyācchukramamṛtamiti
6.18
mṛtyuproktāṃ naciketo'tha labdhvā
vidyāmetāṃ yogavidhiṃ ca kṛtsnam
brahmaprāpto virajo'bhūdvimṛtyuranyo'pyevaṃ yo vidadhyātmameva

6.17The Person the size of a thumb, the inner self, is always seated in the heart of people. Draw it out from one's own body with steadiness, as one draws the reed from the munja grass. Know it to be the bright, the deathless; know it to be the bright, the deathless. Then Nachiketa, having received this knowledge told by Death and the whole rule of yoga, reached Brahman, free of dust and free of death — and so does anyone else who knows the Self in this way.

The munja reed comes out of its sheath whole and clean, and the comparison is about the manner of the operation rather than its violence: steadily, and without tearing what it is drawn from. The last verse returns the reader to the boy, which is the structural point Nagler makes about the Katha: the interview that was dropped when the teaching began is picked up and closed, and the closing clause extends the result to whoever repeats the procedure.

Brihadaranyaka: the great forest

Brihad-aranyaka means of the great forest, and Nagler takes the name as a description of the reading. It is by far the longest of the Upanishads and the one most often quoted, and it sits inside the Shatapatha Brahmana of the White Yajur Veda, which makes it part of the largest ritual manual in the Vedic literature. Its six chapters are divided into forty-seven sections, and the sections are counted straight through here, so that the dialogue with Maitreyi, which editions number II.4, is section ten. Nothing in the text is arranged as an argument. Paths lead off in unpredictable directions, and what holds them together is one figure: Yajnavalkya, who wins a debate against the assembled learning of two kingdoms, answers a woman who has beaten him in public, and twice takes a thousand cows home.
