One line from its third section has travelled further than anything else in the collection, because it is said at the beginning of ceremonies and because Easwaran prints it at the head of the Chandogya.

तदेतानि जपेदसतो मा सद् गमय
तमसो मा ज्योतिर्गमय मृत्योर्माऽमृतं गमयेति ।
tadetāni japedasato mā sad gamaya
tamaso mā jyotirgamaya mṛtyormā'mṛtaṃ gamayeti

3.28Let him repeat these: from the unreal lead me to the real, from darkness lead me to light, from death lead me to the deathless.

The context is a chant. A priest is about to sing, and these are the words he says under his breath first, and the text immediately explains them in its own flat way: the unreal is death, the real is the deathless, so the second half of each line says what the first half means. Nothing in the prayer asks for anything to be added. All three verbs ask to be taken from one place to another.

What is loved in anything loved

The dialogue that follows is the one Nagler calls the most poignant discussion in the wisdom of any tradition. Yajnavalkya is leaving home for the third stage of life, and is settling his property.

मैत्रेयीति होवाच याज्ञवल्क्य उद्यास्यन्वा अरेऽहमस्मात्स्थानादस्मि
हन्त तेऽनया कात्यायन्याऽन्तं करवाणीति ॥ १
सा होवाच मैत्रेयी यन्नु म इयं भगोः सर्वा पृथिवी वित्तेन
पूर्णा स्यात् कथं तेनामृता स्यामिति । नेति होवाच याज्ञवल्क्यो
यथैवोपकरणवतां जीवितं तथैव ते जीवितं स्यादमृतत्वस्य
तु नाऽऽशाऽस्ति वित्तेनेति ॥ २
सा होवाच मैत्रेयी येनाहं नामृता स्यां किमहं तेन कुर्याम् । यदेव
भगवान्वेद तदेव मे ब्रूहीति ॥ ३
स होवाच याज्ञवल्क्यः प्रिया बतारे नः सती प्रियं भाषस एह्यास्स्व
व्याख्यास्यामि ते । व्याचक्षाणस्य तु मे निदिध्यासस्वेति ॥ ४
maitreyīti hovāca yājñavalkya udyāsyanvā are'hamasmātsthānādasmi
hanta te'nayā kātyāyanyā'ntaṃ karavāṇīti
10.2
sā hovāca maitreyī yannu ma iyaṃ bhagoḥ sarvā pṛthivī vittena
pūrṇā syāt kathaṃ tenāmṛtā syāmiti | neti hovāca yājñavalkyo
yathaivopakaraṇavatāṃ jīvitaṃ tathaiva te jīvitaṃ syādamṛtatvasya
tu nā''śā'sti vitteneti
10.3
sā hovāca maitreyī yenāhaṃ nāmṛtā syāṃ kimahaṃ tena kuryām | yadeva
bhagavānveda tadeva me brūhīti
10.4
sa hovāca yājñavalkyaḥ priyā batāre naḥ satī priyaṃ bhāṣasa ehyāssva
vyākhyāsyāmi te | vyācakṣāṇasya tu me nididhyāsasveti

10.1Maitreyi, said Yajnavalkya, I am going to go forth from this place. Come, let me make a settlement between you and Katyayani. Maitreyi said: if this whole earth filled with wealth were mine, would I be deathless by it? No, said Yajnavalkya: your life would be like the life of those who have means. Of deathlessness there is no hope through wealth. Maitreyi said: what should I do with a thing by which I do not become deathless? Tell me what you know. Yajnavalkya said: dear you have been to us, and dear is what you say. Come, sit; I will explain it to you. As I explain, meditate on it.

The exchange is a refusal and a correction in four sentences. Maitreyi is offered half of everything and asks one question about it, and the question is not whether the share is fair but whether the thing has the property she wants. Her husband agrees that it does not, and what he says next is the doctrine.

न वा अरे पत्युः कामाय पतिः प्रियो भवत्यात्मनस्तु कामाय पतिः प्रियो भवति ।
न वा अरे जायायै कामाय जाया प्रिया भवत्यात्मनस्तु कामाय जाया प्रिया भवति ।
न वा अरे सर्वस्य कामाय सर्वं प्रियं भवत्यात्मनस्तु कामाय सर्वं प्रियं भवति ।
आत्मा वा अरे द्रष्टव्यः श्रोतव्यो मन्तव्यो निदिध्यासितव्यः ।
ब्रह्म तं परादाद्योऽन्यत्रात्मनो ब्रह्म वेद ।
लोकास्तं परादुर्योऽन्यत्रात्मनो लोकान्वेद ।
इदं ब्रह्मेदं क्षत्रमिमे लोका इमे देवा इमानि भूतानीदं सर्वं यदयमात्मा ।
स यथा दुन्दुभेर्हन्यमानस्य न बाह्याञ्छब्दाञ्छक्नुयाद् ग्रहणाय
दुन्दुभेस्तु ग्रहणेन दुन्दुभ्याघातस्य वा शब्दो गृहीतः ॥ ७
na vā are patyuḥ kāmāya patiḥ priyo bhavatyātmanastu kāmāya patiḥ priyo bhavati
na vā are jāyāyai kāmāya jāyā priyā bhavatyātmanastu kāmāya jāyā priyā bhavati
na vā are sarvasya kāmāya sarvaṃ priyaṃ bhavatyātmanastu kāmāya sarvaṃ priyaṃ bhavati
ātmā vā are draṣṭavyaḥ śrotavyo mantavyo nididhyāsitavyaḥ
10.6
brahma taṃ parādādyo'nyatrātmano brahma veda
lokāstaṃ parāduryo'nyatrātmano lokānveda
idaṃ brahmedaṃ kṣatramime lokā ime devā imāni bhūtānīdaṃ sarvaṃ yadayamātmā
10.7
sa yathā dundubherhanyamānasya na bāhyāñchabdāñchaknuyād grahaṇāya
dundubhestu grahaṇena dundubhyāghātasya vā śabdo gṛhītaḥ

10.5Not for the husband's sake is the husband dear, but for the sake of the Self is the husband dear. Not for the wife's sake is the wife dear, but for the sake of the Self is the wife dear. Not for the sake of the all is the all dear, but for the sake of the Self is the all dear. The Self is to be seen, to be heard, to be reflected on, to be meditated on. Priesthood abandons the one who knows priesthood as other than the Self. Worlds abandon the one who knows the worlds as other than the Self. This priesthood, this nobility, these worlds, these gods, these beings, all this — it is the Self. As when a drum is being beaten one cannot catch the outward sounds, but by catching the drum, or the beater of the drum, the sound is caught.

The list in verse 5 runs through husband, wife, sons, wealth, priesthood, nobility, the worlds, the gods, all beings and finally everything, and the same sentence is repeated each time with one word changed. The word Easwaran translates as for the sake of is kamaya, for love of; what the sentence denies is that anything is loved in its own right, and what it asserts is that the thing actually loved in every case is one and the same. The four verbs at the end — seen, heard, reflected on, meditated on — became the standard programme of Vedanta, and the last of them, nididhyasana, is the word for meditation the whole collection rests on.

स यथा सर्वासामपां समुद्र एकायनम् ।
एवं सर्वेषां स्पर्शानां त्वगेकायनम् ।
एवं सर्वासां विद्यानां हृदयमेकायनम् ।
स यथा सैन्धवखिल्य उदके प्रास्त उदकमेवानुविलीयेत
न हास्योद्ग्रहणाय स्याद् यतो यतस्त्वाददीत लवणमेव ।
एवं वा अर इदं महद् भूतमनन्तमपारं विज्ञानघन एव ।
सा होवाच मैत्रेय्यत्रैव मा भगवानमूमुहद् न प्रेत्य सञ्ज्ञाऽस्तीति
स होवाच न वा अरेऽहं मोहं ब्रवीम्यलं वा अर इदं विज्ञानाय ॥ १३
sa yathā sarvāsāmapāṃ samudra ekāyanam
evaṃ sarveṣāṃ sparśānāṃ tvagekāyanam
evaṃ sarvāsāṃ vidyānāṃ hṛdayamekāyanam
10.12
sa yathā saindhavakhilya udake prāsta udakamevānuvilīyeta
na hāsyodgrahaṇāya syād yato yatastvādadīta lavaṇameva
evaṃ vā ara idaṃ mahad bhūtamanantamapāraṃ vijñānaghana eva
10.13
sā hovāca maitreyyatraiva mā bhagavānamūmuhad na pretya sañjñā'stīti
sa hovāca na vā are'haṃ mohaṃ bravīmyalaṃ vā ara idaṃ vijñānāya

10.11As the sea is the one meeting place of all waters; as the skin is the one meeting place of all touches; as the heart is the one meeting place of all kinds of knowledge. As a lump of salt thrown into water dissolves into the water, and there is no picking it up again, but wherever one takes it up it is salt — so this great being is endless, unlimited, a single mass of knowing. Maitreyi said: here you have bewildered me. Yajnavalkya said: I am saying nothing bewildering. This is enough for knowing.

The salt of section ten is not the salt of the Chandogya, though the two are often confused. In the Chandogya the point is that what cannot be seen can be tasted everywhere. Here the point is the dissolving: what goes into the water does not survive as a thing that could be lifted out, and Maitreyi understands the implication at once and objects to it, because what she has just been told is that there is no consciousness after death in the sense she meant. The reply is that nothing has been taken away.

यत्र हि द्वैतमिव भवति तदितर इतरं पश्यति तदितर इतरं विजानाति ।
यत्र वा अस्य सर्वमात्मैवाभूत् तत्केन कं पश्येत् तत्केन कं विजानीयात् ।
येनेदं सर्वं विजानाति तं केन विजानीयाद् विज्ञातारमरे केन विजानीयादिति ।
yatra hi dvaitamiva bhavati taditara itaraṃ paśyati taditara itaraṃ vijānāti
yatra vā asya sarvamātmaivābhūt tatkena kaṃ paśyet tatkena kaṃ vijānīyāt
yenedaṃ sarvaṃ vijānāti taṃ kena vijānīyād vijñātāramare kena vijānīyāditi

10.14Where there is duality, as it were, there one sees another, one knows another. But where everything has become the Self, by what should one see whom, by what should one know whom? By what should one know that by which all this is known — by what should one know the knower?

The last clause is the most quoted sentence in the Upanishads and the hardest to answer. It is not a mystery offered for contemplation but a consequence: knowing has a structure with two ends, and the thing in question occupies one of them permanently. The same sentence closes the same dialogue when it is told a second time in the fourth chapter, and there it ends with the line that this much is deathlessness.

एतावदरे खल्वमृतत्वमिति होक्त्वा याज्ञवल्क्यो विजहार ।
etāvadare khalvamṛtatvamiti hoktvā yājñavalkyo vijahāra

26.15This much, then, is deathlessness. Having said that, Yajnavalkya went away.

The honey

The section that follows the dialogue is the madhu-vidya, the honey doctrine, and it is the most systematic passage in the text. It is built out of one sentence repeated fourteen times.

इयं पृथिवी सर्वेषां भूतानां मध्वस्यै पृथिव्यै सर्वाणि भूतानि मधु ।
यश्चायमस्यां पृथिव्यां तेजोमयोऽमृतमयः पुरुषः ।
यश्चायमध्यात्मं शारीरस्तेजोमयोऽमृतमयः पुरुषोऽयमेव सः ।
यो योऽयमात्मेदममृतमिदं ब्रह्मेदं सर्वम् ।
स वा अयमात्मा सर्वेषां भूतानामधिपतिः सर्वेषां भूतानां राजा ।
तद्यथा रथनाभौ च रथनेमौ चाराः सर्वे समर्पिताः ।
एवमेवास्मिन्नात्मनि सर्वाणि भूतानि सर्वे देवाः सर्वे लोकाः सर्वे प्राणाः समर्पिताः ।
रूपंरूपं प्रतिरूपो बभूव तदस्य रूपं प्रतिचक्षणाय ।
इन्द्रो मायाभिः पुरुरूप ईयते युक्ता ह्यस्य हरयः शता दशेति ।
तदेतद्ब्रह्मापूर्वमनपरमनन्तरमबाह्यमयमात्मा ब्रह्म सर्वानुभूरित्यनुशासनम् ।
iyaṃ pṛthivī sarveṣāṃ bhūtānāṃ madhvasyai pṛthivyai sarvāṇi bhūtāni madhu
yaścāyamasyāṃ pṛthivyāṃ tejomayo'mṛtamayaḥ puruṣaḥ
yaścāyamadhyātmaṃ śārīrastejomayo'mṛtamayaḥ puruṣo'yameva saḥ
yo yo'yamātmedamamṛtamidaṃ brahmedaṃ sarvam
11.15
sa vā ayamātmā sarveṣāṃ bhūtānāmadhipatiḥ sarveṣāṃ bhūtānāṃ rājā
tadyathā rathanābhau ca rathanemau cārāḥ sarve samarpitāḥ
evamevāsminnātmani sarvāṇi bhūtāni sarve devāḥ sarve lokāḥ sarve prāṇāḥ samarpitāḥ
11.19
rūpaṃrūpaṃ pratirūpo babhūva tadasya rūpaṃ praticakṣaṇāya
indro māyābhiḥ pururūpa īyate yuktā hyasya harayaḥ śatā daśeti
tadetadbrahmāpūrvamanaparamanantaramabāhyamayamātmā brahma sarvānubhūrityanuśāsanam

11.1This earth is the honey of all beings, and all beings are the honey of this earth. The bright and deathless person in this earth, and the bright and deathless person in the body — he is the same. This is the Self, the deathless, Brahman, the all. This Self is the overlord of all beings, the king of all beings. As all the spokes are fixed in the hub and the rim of a wheel, so all beings, all gods, all worlds, all breaths are fixed in this Self. Shape by shape he became the counter-shape; that shape of his is for the seeing. Indra by his powers goes in many shapes; his horses are yoked, a hundred and ten. He is Brahman, with nothing before, nothing after, nothing inside, nothing outside.

Fourteen times the text names something — earth, water, fire, air, sun, space, moon, lightning, thunder, ether, law, truth, humanity, the Self — and says that it is the honey of all beings and that all beings are its honey, and then locates the same bright person in the thing and in the corresponding part of the body. The word madhu is honey in the plain sense and the figure is the one from beekeeping: what many flowers give becomes one substance, and the substance belongs to no flower. The doctrine is attributed to Dadhyanch, who is said to have taught it to the Ashvins at the cost of his head.

Neti neti

The formula that the tradition treats as the Upanishads' method appears twice and both times as a conclusion rather than a beginning.

अथात आदेशो नेति नेति न ह्येतस्मादिति नेत्यन्यत् परमस्ति ।
अथ नामधेयं सत्यस्य सत्यमिति ।
स एष नेति नेत्यात्माऽगृह्यो न हि गृह्यते ।
अशीर्यो न हि शीर्यतेऽसङ्गो न हि सज्यतेऽसितो न व्यथते न रिष्यति ।
athāta ādeśo neti neti na hyetasmāditi netyanyat paramasti
atha nāmadheyaṃ satyasya satyamiti
21.26
sa eṣa neti netyātmā'gṛhyo na hi gṛhyate
aśīryo na hi śīryate'saṅgo na hi sajyate'sito na vyathate na riṣyati

9.6Then the instruction: not this, not this. There is nothing higher than this, than to say: it is not so. Then the name: the truth of the truth. That Self is not this, not this. It is ungraspable, for it is not grasped; indestructible, for it is not destroyed; unattached, for it does not attach; unbound, it does not tremble and is not harmed.

Neti neti is two words, na iti, not so, said twice, and the doubling is not emphasis. The first denial refuses the description just given; the second refuses the refusal as a description. The passage in section nine has just offered a list of colours for the appearance of the person — a saffron robe, white wool, a scarlet beetle, a flame, a white lotus, a sudden flash of lightning — and it is those that the denial cancels. The list of negative properties in section twenty-one is the set that survives every such cancellation, and it is short: not graspable, not destructible, not attached, not bound, not shaken, not injured.

Gargi, and what holds the worlds

The third chapter is a public contest. King Janaka of Videha pens a thousand cows with ten gold coins tied between the horns of each and offers them to the most learned man present; Yajnavalkya tells his pupil to drive them home before the debate has begun, and then has to earn them. The questioners come in turn, and two of them are remembered. The first is Gargi, daughter of Vachaknu.

अथ हैनं गार्गी वाचक्नवी पप्रच्छ ।
यदिदं सर्वमप्स्वोतं च प्रोतं च कस्मिन्नु खल्वाप ओताश्च प्रोताश्चेति ।
वायौ गार्गीति । कस्मिन्नु खलु वायुरोतश्च प्रोतश्चेति ।
अन्तरिक्षलोकेषु गार्गीति ।
atha hainaṃ gārgī vācaknavī papraccha
yadidaṃ sarvamapsvotaṃ ca protaṃ ca kasminnu khalvāpa otāśca protāśceti
vāyau gārgīti | kasminnu khalu vāyurotaśca protaśceti
antarikṣalokeṣu gārgīti

18.1Then Gargi Vachaknavi asked him: since all this is woven, warp and woof, on water, on what then is water woven, warp and woof? On air, Gargi. And on what is air woven? On the worlds of the middle air, Gargi.

She continues upward through the worlds of the gandharvas, the sun, the moon, the stars, the gods, Indra, Prajapati and Brahma, and at the last step Yajnavalkya stops her with a threat that is also a rule of method: do not ask too much, Gargi, or your head will fall off; you are asking too much about a divinity about which one must not ask too much. She sits down. The exchange with Uddalaka that follows introduces the phrase the later tradition made a doctrine of, the inner controller.

यः पृथिव्यां तिष्ठन्पृथिव्या अन्तरो यं पृथिवी न वेद
यस्य पृथिवी शरीरं यः पृथिवीमन्तरो यमयत्येष त
आत्माऽन्तर्याम्यमृतः ॥ ३
एष त आत्माऽन्तर्याम्यमृतोऽदृष्टो द्रष्टाऽश्रुतः श्रोताऽमतो मन्ताऽविज्ञतो विज्ञाता ।
नान्योऽतोऽस्ति द्रष्टा नान्योऽतोऽस्ति श्रोता नान्योऽतोऽस्ति मन्ता नान्योऽतोऽस्ति विज्ञाता ।
yaḥ pṛthivyāṃ tiṣṭhanpṛthivyā antaro yaṃ pṛthivī na veda
yasya pṛthivī śarīraṃ yaḥ pṛthivīmantaro yamayatyeṣa ta
ātmā'ntaryāmyamṛtaḥ
19.23
eṣa ta ātmā'ntaryāmyamṛto'dṛṣṭo draṣṭā'śrutaḥ śrotā'mato mantā'vijñato vijñātā
nānyo'to'sti draṣṭā nānyo'to'sti śrotā nānyo'to'sti mantā nānyo'to'sti vijñātā

19.3He who dwells in the earth, who is within the earth, whom the earth does not know, whose body the earth is, who controls the earth from within — he is your Self, the inner controller, the deathless. He is your Self, the inner controller, the deathless: unseen, he sees; unheard, he hears; unthought, he thinks; unknown, he knows. There is no other seer than he, no other hearer, no other thinker, no other knower.

The formula of section nineteen is repeated twenty-three times for twenty-three things, from the earth to the semen, and each time with the same four clauses: he is in it, it does not know him, it is his body, he controls it from within. The last sentence supplies the reason the list can be closed. Whatever else is in the series, the one who runs it is not an item that could be added to the series, because the position of knower is already occupied.

Then Gargi stands up a second time and says she has two questions, and that if he answers them nobody will beat him in argument about Brahman.

सा होवाच यदूर्ध्वं याज्ञवल्क्य दिवो यदवाक्पृथिव्या यदन्तरा
द्यावापृथिवी इमे यद्भूतं च भवच्च भविष्यच्चेत्याचक्षते
कस्मिंस्तदोतं च प्रोतं चेति ॥ ३
स होवाच यदूर्ध्वं गार्गि दिवो यदवाक्पृथिव्या यदन्तरा
द्यावापृथिवी इमे यद्भूतं च भवच्च भविष्यच्चेत्याचक्षत
आकाशे तदोतं च प्रोतं चेति ॥ ४
स होवाच यदूर्ध्वं गार्गि दिवो यदवाक्पृथिव्या यदन्तरा
द्यावापृथिवी इमे यद्भूतं च भवच्च भविष्यच्चेत्याचक्षत
आकाश एव तदोतं च प्रोतं चेति । कस्मिन्नु खल्वाकाश ओतश्च
प्रोतश्चेति ॥ ७
sā hovāca yadūrdhvaṃ yājñavalkya divo yadavākpṛthivyā yadantarā
dyāvāpṛthivī ime yadbhūtaṃ ca bhavacca bhaviṣyaccetyācakṣate
kasmiṃstadotaṃ ca protaṃ ceti
20.4
sa hovāca yadūrdhvaṃ gārgi divo yadavākpṛthivyā yadantarā
dyāvāpṛthivī ime yadbhūtaṃ ca bhavacca bhaviṣyaccetyācakṣata
ākāśe tadotaṃ ca protaṃ ceti
20.7
sa hovāca yadūrdhvaṃ gārgi divo yadavākpṛthivyā yadantarā
dyāvāpṛthivī ime yadbhūtaṃ ca bhavacca bhaviṣyaccetyācakṣata
ākāśa eva tadotaṃ ca protaṃ ceti | kasminnu khalvākāśa otaśca
protaśceti

20.3She said: what is above the sky, what is below the earth, what is between these two, what men call the past and the present and the future — on what is that woven, warp and woof? He said: on space, Gargi. He said: on space, Gargi, that is woven, warp and woof. And on what is space woven, warp and woof?

The second question is the first question asked once. Gargi has learned from being stopped, and instead of climbing she goes straight to the term that answers everything else and asks what answers that. The reply is the longest single description in the text and it is made entirely of negatives.

स होवाचैतद्वै तदक्षरं गार्गि ब्राह्मणा अभिवदन्ति ।
अस्थूलमनण्वह्रस्वमदीर्घमलोहितमस्नेहमच्छायमतमः ।
अवाय्वनाकाशमसङ्गमचक्षुष्कमश्रोत्रमवागमनः ।
न तदश्नाति किं चन न तदश्नाति कश्चन ।
एतस्य वा अक्षरस्य प्रशासने गार्गि सूर्याचन्द्रमसौ विधृतौ तिष्ठतः ।
एतस्य वा अक्षरस्य प्रशासने गार्गि द्यावापृथिव्यौ विधृते तिष्ठतः ।
एतस्य वा अक्षरस्य प्रशासने गार्गि प्राच्योऽन्या नद्यः स्यन्दन्ते ।
यो वा एतदक्षरं गार्ग्यविदित्वास्मिंल्लोके जुहोति यजते तपस्तप्यते बहूनि वर्षसहस्राणि ।
अन्तवदेवास्य तद्भवति ।
यो वा एतदक्षरं गार्ग्यविदित्वास्माल्लोकात्प्रैति स कृपणः ।
तद्वा एतदक्षरं गार्ग्यदृष्टं द्रष्टृ अश्रुतं श्रोतृ अमतं मन्तृ अविज्ञातं विज्ञातृ ।
नान्यदतोऽस्ति द्रष्टृ नान्यदतोऽस्ति श्रोतृ नान्यदतोऽस्ति मन्तृ नान्यदतोऽस्ति विज्ञातृ ।
एतस्मिन्नु खल्वक्षरे गार्ग्याकाश ओतश्च प्रोतश्चेति ।
सा होवाच ब्राह्मणा भगवन्तस्तदेव बहु मन्येध्वं यदस्मान्नमस्कारेण
मुच्येध्वं न वै जातु युष्माकमिमं कश्चिद्ब्रह्मोद्यं जेतेति ततो ह
वाचक्नव्युपरराम ॥ १२
sa hovācaitadvai tadakṣaraṃ gārgi brāhmaṇā abhivadanti
asthūlamanaṇvahrasvamadīrghamalohitamasnehamacchāyamatamaḥ
avāyvanākāśamasaṅgamacakṣuṣkamaśrotramavāgamanaḥ
na tadaśnāti kiṃ cana na tadaśnāti kaścana
20.9
etasya vā akṣarasya praśāsane gārgi sūryācandramasau vidhṛtau tiṣṭhataḥ
etasya vā akṣarasya praśāsane gārgi dyāvāpṛthivyau vidhṛte tiṣṭhataḥ
etasya vā akṣarasya praśāsane gārgi prācyo'nyā nadyaḥ syandante
20.10
yo vā etadakṣaraṃ gārgyaviditvāsmiṃlloke juhoti yajate tapastapyate bahūni varṣasahasrāṇi
antavadevāsya tadbhavati
yo vā etadakṣaraṃ gārgyaviditvāsmāllokātpraiti sa kṛpaṇaḥ
20.11
tadvā etadakṣaraṃ gārgyadṛṣṭaṃ draṣṭṛ aśrutaṃ śrotṛ amataṃ mantṛ avijñātaṃ vijñātṛ
nānyadato'sti draṣṭṛ nānyadato'sti śrotṛ nānyadato'sti mantṛ nānyadato'sti vijñātṛ
etasminnu khalvakṣare gārgyākāśa otaśca protaśceti
20.12
sā hovāca brāhmaṇā bhagavantastadeva bahu manyedhvaṃ yadasmānnamaskāreṇa
mucyedhvaṃ na vai jātu yuṣmākamimaṃ kaścidbrahmodyaṃ jeteti tato ha
vācaknavyupararāma

20.8He said: that, Gargi, is what the knowers of Brahman call the imperishable. Not coarse, not fine, not short, not long, not red, not moist, without shadow, without darkness, without air, without space, unattached, without eyes, without ears, without speech, without mind. It eats nothing, and nothing eats it. At the command of this imperishable, Gargi, sun and moon stand held apart. At the command of this imperishable, heaven and earth stand held apart. At the command of this imperishable, some rivers run east from the snowy mountains. Whoever, Gargi, without knowing this imperishable, offers and sacrifices and performs austerities in this world for many thousands of years — what he gets has an end. Whoever, Gargi, departs from this world without knowing this imperishable is wretched. That imperishable, Gargi, is unseen but sees, unheard but hears, unthought but thinks, unknown but knows. There is no other seer than it, no other hearer, no other thinker, no other knower. On this imperishable, Gargi, space is woven, warp and woof. She said: revered brahmins, count yourselves fortunate if you get away from this man with a bow. None of you will beat him in an argument about Brahman. Then Gargi sat down.

The negatives in verse 8 are not a refusal to answer. Each one removes a property that something woven on space would have to have, and the two clauses about eating remove the last relation left, since anything inside a chain of dependence either consumes something or is consumed. Verse 9 is the positive half and it is about regularity: what holds sun and moon apart and sends rivers in fixed directions is the same thing the negatives were about. Gargi's closing speech concedes for the whole assembly, and she does it by naming the prize, which is to get out with one's reputation.

The states of consciousness

The fourth chapter is a private conversation between Yajnavalkya and Janaka, and Yajnavalkya arrives resolved to say nothing and is trapped by a promise. It is the fullest account of waking, dream and sleep in the collection, and everything the later tradition says about the three states begins here.

याज्ञवल्क्य किञ्ज्योतिरयं पुरुष इत्यादित्यज्योतिः सम्राड् इति
होवाचाऽऽदित्येनैवायं ज्योतिषाऽऽस्ते पल्ययते कर्म कुरुते
विपल्येतीत्येवमेवैतद् याज्ञवल्क्य ॥ २
अस्तमित आदित्ये याज्ञवल्क्य चन्द्रमस्यस्तमिते शान्तेऽग्नौ शान्तायां
वाचि किञ्ज्योतिरेवायं पुरुष इत्यात्मैवास्य ज्योतिर्भवत्यात्मनैवायं
ज्योतिषाऽऽस्ते पल्ययते कर्म कुरुते विपल्येतीति ॥ ६
yājñavalkya kiñjyotirayaṃ puruṣa ityādityajyotiḥ samrāḍ iti
hovācā''dityenaivāyaṃ jyotiṣā''ste palyayate karma kurute
vipalyetītyevamevaitad yājñavalkya
24.6
astamita āditye yājñavalkya candramasyastamite śānte'gnau śāntāyāṃ
vāci kiñjyotirevāyaṃ puruṣa ityātmaivāsya jyotirbhavatyātmanaivāyaṃ
jyotiṣā''ste palyayate karma kurute vipalyetīti

24.2Yajnavalkya, what light does this person have? The light of the sun, your majesty, he said; by the light of the sun he sits, moves about, does his work, and comes back. When the sun has set, Yajnavalkya, and the moon has set, and the fire has gone out, and speech is stilled, what light does this person have? The Self is his light; by the light of the Self he sits, moves about, does his work, and comes back.

The question is answered four times before it is answered properly, and the order is a subtraction. Take away the sun and there is the moon; take away the moon and there is fire; take away fire and there is speech, by which a man in the dark can still find his way to the person speaking; take away speech and something is still working. What the sequence establishes is not that the Self is a fifth lamp but that the series of lamps has a floor.

कतम आत्मेति । योऽयं विज्ञानमयः प्राणेषु हृद्यन्तर्ज्योतिः पुरुषः ।
स समानः सन्नुभौ लोकावनुसञ्चरति । ध्यायतीव लेलायतीव ।
स हि स्वप्नो भूत्वेमं लोकमतिक्रामति मृत्यो रूपाणि ।
तस्य वा एतस्य पुरुषस्य द्वे एव स्थाने भवत इदं च परलोकस्थानं च ।
सन्ध्यं तृतीयं स्वप्नस्थानम् ।
स यत्र प्रस्वपित्यस्य लोकस्य सर्वावतो मात्रामपादाय स्वयं विहत्य स्वयं निर्माय ।
स्वेन भासा स्वेन ज्योतिषा प्रस्वपित्यत्रायं पुरुषः स्वयं ज्योतिर्भवति ।
katama ātmeti | yo'yaṃ vijñānamayaḥ prāṇeṣu hṛdyantarjyotiḥ puruṣaḥ
sa samānaḥ sannubhau lokāvanusañcarati | dhyāyatīva lelāyatīva
sa hi svapno bhūtvemaṃ lokamatikrāmati mṛtyo rūpāṇi
24.9
tasya vā etasya puruṣasya dve eva sthāne bhavata idaṃ ca paralokasthānaṃ ca
sandhyaṃ tṛtīyaṃ svapnasthānam
sa yatra prasvapityasya lokasya sarvāvato mātrāmapādāya svayaṃ vihatya svayaṃ nirmāya
svena bhāsā svena jyotiṣā prasvapityatrāyaṃ puruṣaḥ svayaṃ jyotirbhavati

24.7Which is the Self? The person here made of knowing, among the breaths, the light within the heart. Remaining the same, he moves through both worlds. He seems to think; he seems to move about. Becoming a dream, he passes beyond this world, beyond the forms of death. There are just two places for this person, this one and the place of the other world; the third, the joining place, is the place of dream. When he goes to sleep, he takes the material of this all-embracing world, strikes it down himself, builds it himself, and sleeps by his own brightness, by his own light. Here this person is self-illumined.

The phrase svayam jyotih, self-illumined, is the centre of the chapter. In waking the light comes from outside and in dream it does not, and the whole argument turns on that one observation, which anybody can make. Dreaming is used not because it is interesting but because it is the one ordinary state in which the equipment produces its own objects and its own light.

न तत्र रथा न रथयोगा न पन्थानो भवन्ति ।
अथ रथान् रथयोगान् पथः सृजते ।
न तत्र वेशान्ताः पुष्करिण्यः स्रवन्त्यो भवन्त्यथ वेशान्तान् पुष्करिणीः स्रवन्तीः सृजते ।
स हि कर्ता ।
तदेते श्लोका भवन्ति स्वप्नेन शारीरमभिप्रहत्या सुप्तः
सुप्तानभिचाकशीति । शुक्रमादाय पुनरैति स्थानं हिरण्मयः
पुरुष एकहंसः ॥ ११
स्वप्नान्त उच्चावचमीयमानो रूपाणि देवः कुरुते बहूनि । उतेव स्त्रीभिः
सह मोदमानो जक्षदुतेवापि भयानि पश्यन् ॥ १३
na tatra rathā na rathayogā na panthāno bhavanti
atha rathān rathayogān pathaḥ sṛjate
na tatra veśāntāḥ puṣkariṇyaḥ sravantyo bhavantyatha veśāntān puṣkariṇīḥ sravantīḥ sṛjate
sa hi kartā
24.11
tadete ślokā bhavanti svapnena śārīramabhiprahatyā suptaḥ
suptānabhicākaśīti | śukramādāya punaraiti sthānaṃ hiraṇmayaḥ
puruṣa ekahaṃsaḥ
24.13
svapnānta uccāvacamīyamāno rūpāṇi devaḥ kurute bahūni | uteva strībhiḥ
saha modamāno jakṣadutevāpi bhayāni paśyan

24.10There are no chariots there, no teams, no roads; he emits chariots, teams and roads. There are no pools there, no ponds, no rivers; he emits pools, ponds and rivers. For he is the maker. On this there are verses. Striking down the body in sleep, himself sleepless, he looks down on the sleeping. Taking the light he comes back again to his place, the golden person, the solitary swan. Moving up and down in the dream, the god makes many shapes: now taking pleasure with women, now laughing, now seeing terrible things.

The inventory in verse 10 is the earliest list of its kind, and the word for what the dreamer does with chariots and roads is srijate, he emits or pours out. The verses in 11 and 13 are older than the prose around them, and the swan of verse 11 is the same bird the tradition puns on: hamsa, swan, read backwards is so'ham, I am he.

तद्यथा महामत्स्य उभे कूलेऽनुसञ्चरति पूर्वं चापरं च ।
एवमेवायं पुरुष एतावुभावन्तावनुसञ्चरति स्वप्नान्तं च बुद्धान्तं च ।
तद्यथास्मिन्नाकाशे श्येनो वा सुपर्णो वा विपरिपत्य श्रान्तः संहत्य पक्षौ संलयायैव ध्रियते ।
एवमेवायं पुरुष एतस्मा अन्ताय धावति ।
यत्र सुप्तो न कं चन कामं कामयते न कं चन स्वप्नं पश्यति ।
तद्वा अस्यैतदतिच्छन्दा अपहतपाप्माभयं रूपम् ।
तद्यथा प्रियया स्त्रिया सम्परिष्वक्तो न बाह्यं किं चन वेद नान्तरम् ।
एवमेवायं पुरुषः प्राज्ञेनात्मना सम्परिष्वक्तो न बाह्यं किं चन वेद नान्तरम् ।
अत्र पिताऽपिता भवति माताऽमाता लोका अलोका देवा अदेवा वेदा अवेदाः ।
अत्र स्तेनोऽस्तेनो भवति चाण्डालोऽचण्डालः श्रमणोऽश्रमणस्तापसोऽतापसः ।
अनन्वागतं पुण्येनानन्वागतं पापेन तीर्णो हि तदा सर्वाञ्छोकान् हृदयस्य भवति ।
tadyathā mahāmatsya ubhe kūle'nusañcarati pūrvaṃ cāparaṃ ca
evamevāyaṃ puruṣa etāvubhāvantāvanusañcarati svapnāntaṃ ca buddhāntaṃ ca
24.19
tadyathāsminnākāśe śyeno vā suparṇo vā viparipatya śrāntaḥ saṃhatya pakṣau saṃlayāyaiva dhriyate
evamevāyaṃ puruṣa etasmā antāya dhāvati
yatra supto na kaṃ cana kāmaṃ kāmayate na kaṃ cana svapnaṃ paśyati
24.21
tadvā asyaitadaticchandā apahatapāpmābhayaṃ rūpam
tadyathā priyayā striyā sampariṣvakto na bāhyaṃ kiṃ cana veda nāntaram
evamevāyaṃ puruṣaḥ prājñenātmanā sampariṣvakto na bāhyaṃ kiṃ cana veda nāntaram
24.22
atra pitā'pitā bhavati mātā'mātā lokā alokā devā adevā vedā avedāḥ
atra steno'steno bhavati cāṇḍālo'caṇḍālaḥ śramaṇo'śramaṇastāpaso'tāpasaḥ
ananvāgataṃ puṇyenānanvāgataṃ pāpena tīrṇo hi tadā sarvāñchokān hṛdayasya bhavati

24.18As a great fish swims along both banks, the near one and the far one, so this person moves along both these boundaries, the boundary of dream and the boundary of waking. As a hawk or an eagle, having flown about in the sky and being tired, folds its wings and settles down to rest, so this person runs to that condition where, asleep, he desires no desire and sees no dream. That form of his is beyond desire, free from evil, without fear. As a man in the arms of the woman he loves knows nothing of what is outside or inside, so this person, in the arms of the Self that knows, knows nothing of what is outside or inside. There a father is not a father, a mother not a mother, worlds not worlds, gods not gods, the Vedas not the Vedas. There a thief is not a thief, an outcaste not an outcaste, a wanderer not a wanderer, an ascetic not an ascetic. He is not followed by good and not followed by evil; he has then crossed over all the sorrows of the heart.

The fish is the image Nagler singles out, and the point of it is command rather than motion: the fish is at home in the whole river and is not a resident of either bank. The eagle is the opposite motion, and what returns it to the nest is exhaustion. The third image is the one the text calls its boldest, and the list that follows in verse 22 is what it costs. Every category a person can be described by is suspended, including the ones a society most wants kept — the thief, the outcaste, the killer of an unborn child — and the sentence does not say they are forgiven.

यत्र वा अन्यदिव स्यात् तत्रान्योऽन्यत्पश्येदन्योऽन्यज्जिघ्रेत् ।
अन्योऽन्यद्वदेदन्योऽन्यन्मन्वीतान्योऽन्यद्विजानीयात् ।
सलिल एको द्रष्टाद्वैतो भवत्येष ब्रह्मलोकः सम्राडिति ।
एषास्य परमा गतिरेषास्य परमा सम्पदेषोऽस्य परमो लोक एषोऽस्य परम आनन्दः ।
एतस्यैवानन्दस्यान्यानि भूतानि मात्रामुपजीवन्ति ।
yatra vā anyadiva syāt tatrānyo'nyatpaśyedanyo'nyajjighret
anyo'nyadvadedanyo'nyanmanvītānyo'nyadvijānīyāt
24.32
salila eko draṣṭādvaito bhavatyeṣa brahmalokaḥ samrāḍiti
eṣāsya paramā gatireṣāsya paramā sampadeṣo'sya paramo loka eṣo'sya parama ānandaḥ
etasyaivānandasyānyāni bhūtāni mātrāmupajīvanti

24.31Where there is something else, as it were, there one sees another, smells another, speaks to another, thinks of another, knows another. Become water, one, the seer without a second — this is the world of Brahman, your majesty. This is his highest way, his highest prize, his highest world, his highest joy. On a portion of this joy other beings live.

The last clause is the one the Taittiriya will turn into arithmetic. Everything anyone has ever enjoyed is a fraction taken off one quantity, and the fraction is what the word matram means: a measure, a part cut to size.

तद्यथानः सुसमाहितमुत्सर्जद्यायात् ।
एवमेवायं शारीर आत्मा प्राज्ञेनात्मनान्वारूढ उत्सर्जन् याति यत्रैतदूर्ध्वोच्छ्वासी भवति ।
स यत्रायमणिमानं न्येति जरया वोपतपता वाणिमानं निगच्छति ।
तद्यथाम्रं वोदुम्बरं वा पिप्पलं वा बन्धनात्प्रमुच्यते ।
एवमेवायं पुरुष एभ्योऽङ्गेभ्यः सम्प्रमुच्य पुनः प्रतिन्यायं प्रतियोन्याद्रवति प्राणायैव ।
tadyathānaḥ susamāhitamutsarjadyāyāt
evamevāyaṃ śārīra ātmā prājñenātmanānvārūḍha utsarjan yāti yatraitadūrdhvocchvāsī bhavati
24.36
sa yatrāyamaṇimānaṃ nyeti jarayā vopatapatā vāṇimānaṃ nigacchati
tadyathāmraṃ vodumbaraṃ vā pippalaṃ vā bandhanātpramucyate
evamevāyaṃ puruṣa ebhyo'ṅgebhyaḥ sampramucya punaḥ pratinyāyaṃ pratiyonyādravati prāṇāyaiva

24.35As a heavily loaded cart goes creaking, so the self of the body goes creaking, carrying the self that knows, when the breathing is only upward. When this body comes to thinness through old age or through illness — as a mango or a fig or a pipal fruit comes loose from its stalk, so this person comes loose from these limbs and goes back, by the way he came, to the breath.

The two images of dying are placed one after the other and neither is dramatic. The cart is a description of sound; the fruit is a description of ripeness. Nothing cuts the stalk in the second image, and nothing is subtracted in the first.

Desire and what comes of it

एकीभवति न पश्यतीत्याहुरेकीभवति न विजानातीत्याहुः ।
तस्य हैतस्य हृदयस्याग्रं प्रद्योतते । तेन प्रद्योतेनैष आत्मा निष्क्रामति ।
चक्षुष्टो वा मूर्ध्नो वान्येभ्यो वा शरीरदेशेभ्यः ।
तं विद्याकर्मणी समन्वारभेते पूर्वप्रज्ञा च ।
तद्यथा तृणजलायुका तृणस्यान्तं
गत्वाऽन्यमाक्रममाक्रम्यात्मानमुपसंहरत्येवमेवायमात्मेदं
शरीरं निहत्याविद्यां
गमयित्वाऽन्यमाक्रममाक्रम्याऽऽत्मानमुपसंहरति ॥ ३
तद्यथा पेशस्कारी पेशसो मात्रामपादायान्यन्नवतरं कल्याणतरं रूपं तनुते ।
एवमेवायमात्मेदं शरीरं निहत्याविद्यां गमयित्वान्यन्नवतरं कल्याणतरं रूपं कुरुते ।
अथो खल्वाहुः काममय एवायं पुरुष इति ।
स यथाकामो भवति तत्क्रतुर्भवति ।
यत्क्रतुर्भवति तत्कर्म कुरुते । यत्कर्म कुरुते तदभिसम्पद्यते ।
ekībhavati na paśyatītyāhurekībhavati na vijānātītyāhuḥ
tasya haitasya hṛdayasyāgraṃ pradyotate | tena pradyotenaiṣa ātmā niṣkrāmati
cakṣuṣṭo vā mūrdhno vānyebhyo vā śarīradeśebhyaḥ
taṃ vidyākarmaṇī samanvārabhete pūrvaprajñā ca
25.3
tadyathā tṛṇajalāyukā tṛṇasyāntaṃ
gatvā'nyamākramamākramyātmānamupasaṃharatyevamevāyamātmedaṃ
śarīraṃ nihatyāvidyāṃ
gamayitvā'nyamākramamākramyā''tmānamupasaṃharati
25.4
tadyathā peśaskārī peśaso mātrāmapādāyānyannavataraṃ kalyāṇataraṃ rūpaṃ tanute
evamevāyamātmedaṃ śarīraṃ nihatyāvidyāṃ gamayitvānyannavataraṃ kalyāṇataraṃ rūpaṃ kurute
25.5
atho khalvāhuḥ kāmamaya evāyaṃ puruṣa iti
sa yathākāmo bhavati tatkraturbhavati
yatkraturbhavati tatkarma kurute | yatkarma kurute tadabhisampadyate

25.2It becomes one, they say; he does not see, he does not know. The point of the heart lights up, and by that light the Self goes out, through the eye, or through the crown, or through some other part of the body. Knowledge and works take hold of him, and so does former understanding. As a caterpillar, having come to the end of a blade of grass, draws itself together and reaches out for the next, so the Self, having struck down this body and put ignorance aside, draws itself together and reaches out for the next. As a goldsmith, taking a piece of an ornament, makes another newer and more beautiful shape, so the Self, having struck down this body and put ignorance aside, makes another newer and more beautiful shape. They also say: this person is made of desire. As his desire is, so is his resolve; as his resolve is, so is the act he does; as the act he does is, so is what he comes to.

The caterpillar is the text's answer to the question of whether there is a gap, and the answer is no: the front end is on the next blade before the back end leaves this one. The goldsmith says the same about what the new shape is made of. The sentence in verse 5 is the one Easwaran puts at the front of his edition, and it is a chain of four terms in which each one is fixed by the one before it, which is what makes it a practical teaching rather than a doctrine of fate. The only term anybody has a grip on is the first.

तदेव सक्तः सह कर्मणैति लिङ्गं मनो यत्र निषक्तमस्य ।
प्राप्यान्तं कर्मणस्तस्य यत्किञ्चेह करोत्ययम् ।
तस्माल्लोकात्पुनरैत्यस्मै लोकाय कर्मणे ।
तद्यथाऽहिनिर्व्लयनी वल्मीके मृता प्रत्यस्ता शयीत ।
एवमेवेदं शरीरं शेतेऽथायमशरीरोऽमृतः प्राणो ब्रह्मैव तेज एव ।
tadeva saktaḥ saha karmaṇaiti liṅgaṃ mano yatra niṣaktamasya
prāpyāntaṃ karmaṇastasya yatkiñceha karotyayam
tasmāllokātpunaraityasmai lokāya karmaṇe
25.7
tadyathā'hinirvlayanī valmīke mṛtā pratyastā śayīta
evamevedaṃ śarīraṃ śete'thāyamaśarīro'mṛtaḥ prāṇo brahmaiva teja eva

25.6Attached, he goes with his work to wherever his mind is fastened. Coming to the end of the work, whatever he does here, he comes back from that world to this world, to work. As the sloughed skin of a snake lies cast off on an anthill, dead, so this body lies. Then the bodiless one is the deathless, is breath, is Brahman, is light. I give you a thousand cows, said Janaka of Videha.

The skin on the anthill is the last of the images and the most economical: what is left is not damaged, and it is not the animal. Janaka's response is to give away another thousand cows, which is the second of three times he pays, and the third time he offers the kingdom and himself with it.

Da

The last passage in Easwaran's selection is a fable about hearing, and it takes three lines.

तेभ्यो हैतदक्षरमुवाच द इति व्यज्ञासिष्टेति ।
व्यज्ञासिष्मेति होचुर्दाम्यतेति न आत्थेत्योमिति होवाच व्यज्ञासिष्टेति ।
अथ हैनं मनुष्या ऊचुर्ब्रवीतु नो भवानिति ।
तेभ्यो हैतदेवाक्षरमुवाच द इति । व्यज्ञासिष्मेति होचुर्दत्तेति न आत्थेति ।
अथ हैनमसुरा ऊचुर्ब्रवीतु नो भवानिति ।
तेभ्यो हैतदेवाक्षरमुवाच द इति । व्यज्ञासिष्मेति होचुर्दयध्वमिति न आत्थेति ।
तदेतदेवैषा दैवी वागनुवदति स्तनयित्नुर्द द द इति दाम्यत दत्त दयध्वमिति ।
tebhyo haitadakṣaramuvāca da iti vyajñāsiṣṭeti
vyajñāsiṣmeti hocurdāmyateti na ātthetyomiti hovāca vyajñāsiṣṭeti
29.2
atha hainaṃ manuṣyā ūcurbravītu no bhavāniti
tebhyo haitadevākṣaramuvāca da iti | vyajñāsiṣmeti hocurdatteti na āttheti
29.3
atha hainamasurā ūcurbravītu no bhavāniti
tebhyo haitadevākṣaramuvāca da iti | vyajñāsiṣmeti hocurdayadhvamiti na āttheti
tadetadevaiṣā daivī vāganuvadati stanayitnurda da da iti dāmyata datta dayadhvamiti

29.1The three kinds of children of Prajapati lived with their father as students: the gods, human beings and the anti-gods. To the gods he said this one syllable: da. Did you understand? We understood, they said; you said to us, restrain yourselves. Yes, he said; you understood. Then the human beings said to him: tell us something. To them he said the same syllable, da. We understood, they said; you said to us, give. Then the anti-gods said to him: tell us something. To them he said the same syllable, da. We understood, they said; you said to us, be compassionate. That same thing the divine voice, the thunder, repeats: da da da — restrain yourselves, give, be compassionate. These three are to be learned: restraint, giving, compassion.

Three groups are given the identical instruction and all three answers are accepted. What determines the meaning is not the teacher and not the sound but what the hearer is short of, and the fable is careful to make the gods, who have everything, the ones who need restraint. T. S. Eliot ended The Waste Land with the three words, and the thunder that says them there is the thunder of this passage.

Chandogya: the sacred song

The Chandogya belongs to the Sama Veda, the Veda that is sung, and its name comes from chandoga, the singer of the chanted portion, and behind that from chandas, song. Nagler's summary of its cosmology is that the world came out of the unchanging as a meaningful sound is uttered: not a big bang but a big OM. Its eight chapters hold a hundred and fifty-four sections, and as elsewhere here the sections are counted straight through, so that the sixth chapter, where Uddalaka teaches his son, runs from section ninety-eight to section a hundred and thirteen. Two principles run under the whole of it. One is rita, order or regularity, without which nothing could be learned from experience at all. The other is yajna, sacrifice, which Nagler glosses as the giving up of personal gain, and which is what a creature does that is living in accordance with the first.

The third chapter contains the text's own version of the honey doctrine and the sentence that Shankara built a system on.

असौ वा आदित्यो देवमधु तस्य द्यौरेव तिरश्चीनवंशः ।
अन्तरिक्षमपूपो मरीचयः पुत्राः ।
गायत्री वा इदं सर्वं भूतं यदिदं किं च ।
वाग्वै गायत्री वाग्वा इदं सर्वं भूतं गायति च त्रायते च ।
सर्वं खल्विदं ब्रह्म तज्जलानिति शान्त उपासीत ।
अथ खलु क्रतुमयः पुरुषो यथाक्रतुरस्मिंल्लोके पुरुषो भवति तथेतः प्रेत्य भवति ।
एष म आत्मान्तर्हृदय एतद्ब्रह्म ।
एतमितः प्रेत्याभिसम्भविताऽस्मीति यस्य स्यादद्धा न विचिकित्सास्ति ।
इति ह स्माह शाण्डिल्यः शाण्डिल्यः ।
asau vā ādityo devamadhu tasya dyaureva tiraścīnavaṃśaḥ
antarikṣamapūpo marīcayaḥ putrāḥ
49.1
gāyatrī vā idaṃ sarvaṃ bhūtaṃ yadidaṃ kiṃ ca
vāgvai gāyatrī vāgvā idaṃ sarvaṃ bhūtaṃ gāyati ca trāyate ca
51.1
sarvaṃ khalvidaṃ brahma tajjalāniti śānta upāsīta
atha khalu kratumayaḥ puruṣo yathākraturasmiṃlloke puruṣo bhavati tathetaḥ pretya bhavati
51.4
eṣa ma ātmāntarhṛdaya etadbrahma
etamitaḥ pretyābhisambhavitā'smīti yasya syādaddhā na vicikitsāsti
iti ha smāha śāṇḍilyaḥ śāṇḍilyaḥ

38.1That sun over there is the honey of the gods. Heaven is its crossbeam, the middle air its honeycomb, the rays of light its brood. The Gayatri is all this, whatever has come to be. Speech is the Gayatri, and speech sings all this that has come to be, and protects it. All this is Brahman; being quiet, one should worship it as that from which one comes, into which one goes, by which one breathes. A person is made of resolve. As his resolve is in this world, so, having gone from here, he becomes. This is my Self within the heart. This is Brahman. Going hence I shall reach it. One who has this and does not doubt it — so said Shandilya, so said Shandilya.

The word rendered here as that from which one comes, into which one goes, by which one breathes is a single coined word, tajjalan, made of three fragments, and Shankara's comment unpacks it as those three clauses. The passage is called the wisdom of Shandilya and it is one long sentence: everything is Brahman, a person is what he resolves, therefore resolve accordingly. It is the same claim about desire that the Brihadaranyaka makes, set inside a cosmology instead of beside a deathbed.

Uddalaka and Shvetaketu

The sixth chapter is the most sustained argument in the Upanishads and the passage the tradition treats as its centre. A boy is sent away at twelve, studies for twelve years, and comes back pleased with himself.

श्वेतकेतो यन्नु सोम्येदं महामना अनूचानमानी स्तब्धोऽसि ।
उत तमादेशमप्राक्ष्यः येनाश्रुतं श्रुतं भवत्यमतं मतमविज्ञातं विज्ञातमिति ।
कथं नु भगवः स आदेशो भवतीति ।
यथा सोम्यैकेन मृत्पिण्डेन सर्वं मृन्मयं विज्ञातं स्यात् ।
वाचारम्भणं विकारो नामधेयं मृत्तिकेत्येव सत्यम् ।
यथा सोम्यैकेन नखनिकृन्तनेन सर्वं कार्ष्णायसं विज्ञातं स्यात् ।
वाचारम्भणं विकारो नामधेयं कृष्णायसमित्येव सत्यम् ।
एवं सोम्य स आदेशो भवतीति ।
śvetaketo yannu somyedaṃ mahāmanā anūcānamānī stabdho'si
uta tamādeśamaprākṣyaḥ yenāśrutaṃ śrutaṃ bhavatyamataṃ matamavijñātaṃ vijñātamiti
kathaṃ nu bhagavaḥ sa ādeśo bhavatīti
98.4
yathā somyaikena mṛtpiṇḍena sarvaṃ mṛnmayaṃ vijñātaṃ syāt
vācārambhaṇaṃ vikāro nāmadheyaṃ mṛttiketyeva satyam
98.6
yathā somyaikena nakhanikṛntanena sarvaṃ kārṣṇāyasaṃ vijñātaṃ syāt
vācārambhaṇaṃ vikāro nāmadheyaṃ kṛṣṇāyasamityeva satyam
evaṃ somya sa ādeśo bhavatīti

98.3Shvetaketu, since you are now proud, thinking yourself learned, and stiff — did you ask for that instruction by which the unheard becomes heard, the unthought thought, the unknown known? How, sir, is that instruction? As by one lump of clay everything made of clay may be known — the change is a turn of speech, a name; the clay alone is real. As by one nail-clipper everything made of iron may be known — the change is a turn of speech, a name; the iron alone is real.

The three illustrations of clay, gold and iron are the warrant for everything that follows, and they make an epistemological claim before they make a metaphysical one. Knowing one lump exhausts the class, because what distinguishes the pot from the plate is nama-rupa, name and shape, and the phrase Easwaran's Sanskrit uses for it, vacharambhanam, means a thing got hold of by speech. The father is not saying that pots are unreal. He is saying that a pot is not a second substance.

सदेव सोम्येदमग्र आसीदेकमेवाद्वितीयम् ।
तद्धैक आहुरसदेवेदमग्र आसीदेकमेवाद्वितीयम् ।
तस्मादसतः सज्जायत ।
तदैक्षत बहु स्यां प्रजायेयेति तत्तेजोऽसृजत ।
तत्तेज ऐक्षत बहु स्यां प्रजायेयेति तदपोऽसृजत ।
sadeva somyedamagra āsīdekamevādvitīyam
taddhaika āhurasadevedamagra āsīdekamevādvitīyam
tasmādasataḥ sajjāyata
99.3
tadaikṣata bahu syāṃ prajāyeyeti tattejo'sṛjata
tatteja aikṣata bahu syāṃ prajāyeyeti tadapo'sṛjata

99.1In the beginning, dear one, this was being alone, one without a second. Some say that in the beginning this was non-being alone, one without a second, and that from non-being being was born. It thought: let me be many, let me bring forth. It sent forth heat. The heat thought: let me be many, let me bring forth. It sent forth water.

The rejection in verse 1 is the earliest philosophical disagreement recorded in the collection, and it is set out fairly: the rival position is stated in the rival's own words before it is refused. What replaces it is not a creator outside the world but a sequence of self-multiplications, and the verb at each step is aikshata, it looked or considered, which is why the chain is one of thought rather than of making.

उद्दालको हारुणिः श्वेतकेतुं पुत्रमुवाच स्वप्नान्तं मे सोम्य विजानीहीति ।
यत्रैतत्पुरुषः स्वपिति नाम सता सोम्य तदा सम्पन्नो भवति स्वमपीतो भवति ।
तस्मादेनं स्वपितीत्याचक्षते स्वं ह्यपीतो भवति ।
स यथा शकुनिः सूत्रेण प्रबद्धो दिशं दिशं पतित्वा ।
अन्यत्रायतनमलब्ध्वा बन्धनमेवोपश्रयते ।
एवमेव खलु सोम्य तन्मनो दिशं दिशं पतित्वान्यत्रायतनमलब्ध्वा प्राणमेवोपश्रयते ।
प्राणबन्धनं हि सोम्य मन इति ।
uddālako hāruṇiḥ śvetaketuṃ putramuvāca svapnāntaṃ me somya vijānīhīti
yatraitatpuruṣaḥ svapiti nāma satā somya tadā sampanno bhavati svamapīto bhavati
tasmādenaṃ svapitītyācakṣate svaṃ hyapīto bhavati
105.2
sa yathā śakuniḥ sūtreṇa prabaddho diśaṃ diśaṃ patitvā
anyatrāyatanamalabdhvā bandhanamevopaśrayate
evameva khalu somya tanmano diśaṃ diśaṃ patitvānyatrāyatanamalabdhvā prāṇamevopaśrayate
prāṇabandhanaṃ hi somya mana iti

105.1Uddalaka Aruni said to his son Shvetaketu: learn from me what sleep is. When a man here sleeps, as they call it, then, dear one, he has gone to being; he has gone to his own. That is why they say of him, he sleeps: he has gone to his own. As a bird tied by a string flies in every direction and, finding no resting place elsewhere, settles down on the tether — so, dear one, the mind flies in every direction, and, finding no resting place elsewhere, settles down on breath. For the mind, dear one, is tethered to breath.

The pun in verse 1 is the text's own and it is the reason the passage exists: svapiti, he sleeps, is heard as sva plus apita, gone to his own. The tethered bird that follows is the image Nagler calls the most poignant in the collection, and what it claims is that the mind's wandering is not free. It ends where it does not because it has chosen to but because the string has run out.

स य एषोऽणिमैतदात्म्यमिदं सर्वं तत्सत्यं स आत्मा तत्त्वमसि श्वेतकेतो इति ।
sa ya eṣo'ṇimaitadātmyamidaṃ sarvaṃ tatsatyaṃ sa ātmā tattvamasi śvetaketo iti

105.7That which is the finest part — all this has that for its self. That is the real. That is the Self. You are that, Shvetaketu.

The sentence repeats nine times, once at the end of each illustration, and it is the first of the four great formulae. Easwaran is careful about the pronoun: the "you" is not the boy's personality but, in his phrase, the consciousness which makes the eye see and the mind think. What follows is the series of nine illustrations, each of which claims something different about the same identity.

यथा सोम्य मधु मधुकृतो निस्तिष्ठन्ति ।
नानात्ययानां वृक्षाणां रसान् समवहारमेकतां रसं गमयन्ति ।
ते यथा तत्र न विवेकं लभन्तेऽमुष्याहं वृक्षस्य रसोऽस्मीति ।
एवमेव खलु सोम्येमाः सर्वाः प्रजाः सति सम्पद्य न विदुः सति सम्पद्यामह इति ।
इमाः सोम्य नद्यः पुरस्तात्प्राच्यः स्यन्दन्ते पश्चात्प्रतीच्यः ।
ताः समुद्रात्समुद्रमेवापियन्ति स समुद्र एव भवति ।
ता यथा तत्र न विदुरियमहमस्मीयमहमस्मीति ।
एवमेव खलु सोम्येमाः सर्वाः प्रजाः सत आगम्य न विदुः सत आगच्छामह इति ।
yathā somya madhu madhukṛto nistiṣṭhanti
nānātyayānāṃ vṛkṣāṇāṃ rasān samavahāramekatāṃ rasaṃ gamayanti
106.2
te yathā tatra na vivekaṃ labhante'muṣyāhaṃ vṛkṣasya raso'smīti
evameva khalu somyemāḥ sarvāḥ prajāḥ sati sampadya na viduḥ sati sampadyāmaha iti
107.1
imāḥ somya nadyaḥ purastātprācyaḥ syandante paścātpratīcyaḥ
tāḥ samudrātsamudramevāpiyanti sa samudra eva bhavati
tā yathā tatra na viduriyamahamasmīyamahamasmīti
107.2
evameva khalu somyemāḥ sarvāḥ prajāḥ sata āgamya na viduḥ sata āgacchāmaha iti

106.1As the bees, dear one, make honey by gathering the juices of trees standing far apart, and reduce them to one juice. And as those juices have there no discrimination, so as to say, I am the juice of this tree, I am the juice of that — so, dear one, all these creatures, having reached being, do not know that they have reached being. These rivers, dear one, flow east in the east and west in the west. From the sea they go to the sea. It becomes the sea itself. And as they do not know there, I am this one, I am that one. So, dear one, all these creatures, having come from being, do not know that they have come from being.

Bees and rivers make the same point from opposite ends. The honey is a merger of things that were distinct and can no longer be told apart; the rivers merge into something that was there first, and the sentence that closes each is not that they have become one but that they do not know it. The teaching is about ignorance rather than about unity, which is why it can be taught at all.

अस्य सोम्य महतो वृक्षस्य यो मूलेऽभ्याहन्याज्जीवन् स्रवेत् ।
यो मध्येऽभ्याहन्याज्जीवन् स्रवेद्योऽग्रेऽभ्याहन्याज्जीवन् स्रवेत् ।
स एष जीवेनात्मनानुप्रभूतः पेपीयमानो मोदमानस्तिष्ठति ।
जीवापेतं वाव किलेदं म्रियते न जीवो म्रियत इति ।
न्यग्रोधफलमत आहरेति । इदं भगव इति । भिन्द्धीति । भिन्नं भगव इति ।
किमत्र पश्यसीति । अण्व्य इवेमा धाना भगव इति ।
आसामङ्गैकां भिन्द्धीति । भिन्ना भगव इति । किमत्र पश्यसीति । न किञ्चन भगव इति ।
तं होवाच यं वै सोम्यैतमणिमानं न निभालयसे ।
एतस्य वै सोम्यैषोऽणिम्न एवं महान्यग्रोधस्तिष्ठति । श्रद्धत्स्व सोम्येति ।
asya somya mahato vṛkṣasya yo mūle'bhyāhanyājjīvan sravet
yo madhye'bhyāhanyājjīvan sravedyo'gre'bhyāhanyājjīvan sravet
sa eṣa jīvenātmanānuprabhūtaḥ pepīyamāno modamānastiṣṭhati
108.3
jīvāpetaṃ vāva kiledaṃ mriyate na jīvo mriyata iti
109.1
nyagrodhaphalamata āhareti | idaṃ bhagava iti | bhinddhīti | bhinnaṃ bhagava iti
kimatra paśyasīti | aṇvya ivemā dhānā bhagava iti
āsāmaṅgaikāṃ bhinddhīti | bhinnā bhagava iti | kimatra paśyasīti | na kiñcana bhagava iti
109.2
taṃ hovāca yaṃ vai somyaitamaṇimānaṃ na nibhālayase
etasya vai somyaiṣo'ṇimna evaṃ mahānyagrodhastiṣṭhati | śraddhatsva somyeti

108.1Of this great tree, dear one: if one struck at the root it would bleed and live; if one struck at the middle it would bleed and live; if one struck at the top it would bleed and live. Pervaded by the living self it stands, drinking and rejoicing. What is left by the living dies; the living itself does not die. Bring me a fruit from that fig tree. Here it is, sir. Split it. It is split, sir. What do you see there? These seeds, very fine. Split one of them. It is split, sir. What do you see there? Nothing at all, sir. He said to him: that fineness which you do not perceive — from that fineness, dear one, this great fig tree stands. Have faith, dear one.

The tree and the seed are placed together and they hold opposite ends of the same claim. The tree shows that life can be withdrawn by parts and that the part that withdraws is not the part that bleeds; the seed shows that what accounts for the whole tree is not a small thing but no thing that inspection finds. The instruction Uddalaka gives when the boy reports seeing nothing is not an argument. It is shraddhatsva, have faith, which in this text means take the next step and see.

लवणमेतदुदकेऽवधाय अथ मा प्रातरुपसीदथा इति ।
स ह तथा चकार । तं होवाच यद्दोषा लवणमुदकेऽवाधाः अङ्ग तदाहरेति ।
तद्धावमृश्य न विवेद ।
यथा विलीनमेवाङ्ग । अस्यान्तादाचामेति । कथमिति । लवणमिति ।
मध्यादाचामेति । कथमिति । लवणमिति । अन्तादाचामेति । कथमिति । लवणमिति ।
तं होवाचात्र वाव किल तत्सोम्य न निभालयसेऽत्रैव किलेति ।
यथा सोम्य पुरुषं गन्धारेभ्योऽभिनद्धाक्षमानीय तं ततोऽतिजने विसृजेत् ।
स यथा तत्र प्राङ्वोदङ्वाधराङ्वा प्रत्यङ्वा प्रध्मायीत ।
अभिनद्धाक्ष आनीतोऽभिनद्धाक्षो विसृष्टः ।
तस्य यथाभिनहनं प्रमुच्य प्रब्रूयादेतां दिशं गन्धारा एतां दिशं व्रजेति ।
स ग्रामाद्ग्रामं पृच्छन् पण्डितो मेधावी गन्धारानेवोपसम्पद्येत ।
एवमेवेहाचार्यवान् पुरुषो वेद ।
lavaṇametadudake'vadhāya atha mā prātarupasīdathā iti
sa ha tathā cakāra | taṃ hovāca yaddoṣā lavaṇamudake'vādhāḥ aṅga tadāhareti
taddhāvamṛśya na viveda
110.2
yathā vilīnamevāṅga | asyāntādācāmeti | kathamiti | lavaṇamiti
madhyādācāmeti | kathamiti | lavaṇamiti | antādācāmeti | kathamiti | lavaṇamiti
taṃ hovācātra vāva kila tatsomya na nibhālayase'traiva kileti
111.1
yathā somya puruṣaṃ gandhārebhyo'bhinaddhākṣamānīya taṃ tato'tijane visṛjet
sa yathā tatra prāṅvodaṅvādharāṅvā pratyaṅvā pradhmāyīta
abhinaddhākṣa ānīto'bhinaddhākṣo visṛṣṭaḥ
111.2
tasya yathābhinahanaṃ pramucya prabrūyādetāṃ diśaṃ gandhārā etāṃ diśaṃ vrajeti
sa grāmādgrāmaṃ pṛcchan paṇḍito medhāvī gandhārānevopasampadyeta
evamevehācāryavān puruṣo veda

110.1Put this salt in water and come to me in the morning. He did so. He said to him: bring me the salt you put in the water last night. He felt for it and did not find it. It has dissolved. Taste from this end. How is it? Salt. Taste from the middle. How is it? Salt. Taste from that end. How is it? Salt. He said to him: here indeed you do not perceive it, and here indeed it is. As a man might be brought from the land of Gandhara with his eyes bandaged and then let go in a deserted place, and there might drift east or north or south or west, having been brought blindfold and let go blindfold. And as, if someone took the bandage off and told him, Gandhara is in that direction, go that way, he would ask from village to village and, being informed and able, reach Gandhara — so a man who has a teacher knows.

The salt is the experiment the chapter is remembered for, and it is designed so that the failure comes first: the boy is made to look for the salt and not find it before he is told to taste. The blindfolded man is the answer to the obvious objection, which is why a teacher should be needed at all if the thing is everywhere. The teacher does two things in the story and only two: he takes off the bandage and says which way. The walking from village to village, asking at each, is left to the man.

पुरुषं सोम्योतोपतापिनं ज्ञातयः पर्युपासते जानासि मां जानासि मामिति ।
तस्य यावन्न वाङ्मनसि सम्पद्यते मनः प्राणे प्राणस्तेजसि तेजः परस्यां देवतायाम् ।
तावज्जानाति ।
अथ यदास्य वाङ्मनसि सम्पद्यते मनः प्राणे प्राणस्तेजसि तेजः परस्यां देवतायाम् ।
अथ न जानाति ।
एतदात्म्यमिदं सर्वं तत्सत्यं स आत्मा तत्त्वमसि श्वेतकेतो इति ।
तद्धास्य विजज्ञाविति विजज्ञाविति ।
puruṣaṃ somyotopatāpinaṃ jñātayaḥ paryupāsate jānāsi māṃ jānāsi māmiti
tasya yāvanna vāṅmanasi sampadyate manaḥ prāṇe prāṇastejasi tejaḥ parasyāṃ devatāyām
tāvajjānāti
112.2
atha yadāsya vāṅmanasi sampadyate manaḥ prāṇe prāṇastejasi tejaḥ parasyāṃ devatāyām
atha na jānāti
113.3
etadātmyamidaṃ sarvaṃ tatsatyaṃ sa ātmā tattvamasi śvetaketo iti
taddhāsya vijajñāviti vijajñāviti

112.1When a man, dear one, is lying ill, his relatives sit round him and ask, do you know me, do you know me? So long as his speech has not gone into mind, mind into breath, breath into heat, heat into the highest divinity, he knows them. When his speech has gone into mind, mind into breath, breath into heat, heat into the highest divinity, then he does not know them. All this has that for its self. That is the real. That is the Self. You are that, Shvetaketu. And he understood it — he understood it.

Dying is described as a sequence of mergings, and what the sequence explains is a fact anyone at a deathbed has seen, that recognition fails at a definite point while the body has not stopped. The chapter ends with the boy understanding, which is not how most Upanishadic dialogues end. The two illustrations Easwaran leaves out of his selection are the fast and the ordeal of the heated axe, and the chapter as printed goes straight from the dying man to the closing line.

Narada asks for something else

The seventh chapter begins with the longest list of studies in Sanskrit literature and the shortest verdict on it.

अधीहि भगव इति होपससाद सनत्कुमारं नारदः ।
तं होवाच यद्वेत्थ तेन मोपसीद ततस्त ऊर्ध्वं वक्ष्यामीति । स होवाच ।
ऋग्वेदं भगवोऽध्येमि यजुर्वेदं सामवेदमाथर्वणं चतुर्थम् ।
इतिहासपुराणं पञ्चमं वेदानां वेदं पित्र्यं राशिं दैवं निधिं वाकोवाक्यम् ।
एकायनं देवविद्यां ब्रह्मविद्यां भूतविद्यां क्षत्रविद्यां नक्षत्रविद्याम् ।
सर्पदेवजनविद्यामेतद्भगवोऽध्येमि ।
सोऽहं भगवो मन्त्रविदेवास्मि नात्मवित् ।
श्रुतं ह्येव मे भगवद्दृशेभ्यस्तरति शोकमात्मविदिति ।
सोऽहं भगवः शोचामि तं मा भगवाञ्छोकस्य पारं तारयत्विति ।
तं होवाच यद्वै किञ्चैतदध्यगीष्ठा नामैवैतत् ।
adhīhi bhagava iti hopasasāda sanatkumāraṃ nāradaḥ
taṃ hovāca yadvettha tena mopasīda tatasta ūrdhvaṃ vakṣyāmīti | sa hovāca
114.2
ṛgvedaṃ bhagavo'dhyemi yajurvedaṃ sāmavedamātharvaṇaṃ caturtham
itihāsapurāṇaṃ pañcamaṃ vedānāṃ vedaṃ pitryaṃ rāśiṃ daivaṃ nidhiṃ vākovākyam
ekāyanaṃ devavidyāṃ brahmavidyāṃ bhūtavidyāṃ kṣatravidyāṃ nakṣatravidyām
sarpadevajanavidyāmetadbhagavo'dhyemi
114.3
so'haṃ bhagavo mantravidevāsmi nātmavit
śrutaṃ hyeva me bhagavaddṛśebhyastarati śokamātmaviditi
so'haṃ bhagavaḥ śocāmi taṃ mā bhagavāñchokasya pāraṃ tārayatviti
taṃ hovāca yadvai kiñcaitadadhyagīṣṭhā nāmaivaitat

114.1Teach me, sir, said Narada, coming to Sanatkumara. He said to him: come to me with what you know, and I shall tell you what is beyond it. He said: I know the Rig Veda, sir, the Yajur, the Sama, the Atharva as the fourth, the epic and the old stories as the fifth, the Veda of the Vedas, the rites for the fathers, arithmetic, divination, the science of times, logic, statecraft, the science of the gods, the science of scripture, the science of the elements, the science of war, the science of the stars, the science of snakes and of the fine arts. With all this, sir, I know the hymns and not the Self. I have heard from those like you that one who knows the Self crosses over sorrow. I am in sorrow, sir. Take me across it. He said to him: whatever you have learned here is only a name.

The list is meant to be complete: it runs from the four Vedas to snake-charming, and Narada's complaint is not that he has learned the wrong things but that he has learned everything there is and it did not do the one thing he needed. Sanatkumara's reply is four words in Sanskrit. What follows in the original is a ladder of sixteen steps, each transcending the last, from speech to mind to will and up to happiness; Easwaran prints the bottom and the top of it and leaves the rungs out.

यो वै भूमा तत्सुखं नाल्पे सुखमस्ति भूमैव सुखम् ।
भूमा त्वेव विजिज्ञासितव्य इति ।
यत्र नान्यत्पश्यति नान्यच्छृणोति नान्यद्विजानाति स भूमा ।
अथ यत्रान्यत्पश्यत्यन्यच्छृणोत्यन्यद्विजानाति तदल्पम् ।
यो वै भूमा तदमृतमथ यदल्पं तन्मर्त्यम् ।
स एवाधस्तात्स उपरिष्टात्स पश्चात्स पुरस्तात्स दक्षिणतः स उत्तरतः ।
स एवेदं सर्वमिति ।
आत्मैवाधस्तादात्मोपरिष्टादात्मा पश्चादात्मा पुरस्तात् ।
आत्मैवेदं सर्वमिति ।
स वा एष एवं पश्यन्नेवं मन्वान एवं विजानन्नात्मरतिरात्मक्रीड आत्ममिथुन आत्मानन्दः स स्वराड्भवति ।
अथ येऽन्यथातो विदुरन्यराजानस्ते क्षय्यलोका भवन्ति ।
yo vai bhūmā tatsukhaṃ nālpe sukhamasti bhūmaiva sukham
bhūmā tveva vijijñāsitavya iti
137.1
yatra nānyatpaśyati nānyacchṛṇoti nānyadvijānāti sa bhūmā
atha yatrānyatpaśyatyanyacchṛṇotyanyadvijānāti tadalpam
yo vai bhūmā tadamṛtamatha yadalpaṃ tanmartyam
138.1
sa evādhastātsa upariṣṭātsa paścātsa purastātsa dakṣiṇataḥ sa uttarataḥ
sa evedaṃ sarvamiti
138.2
ātmaivādhastādātmopariṣṭādātmā paścādātmā purastāt
ātmaivedaṃ sarvamiti
sa vā eṣa evaṃ paśyannevaṃ manvāna evaṃ vijānannātmaratirātmakrīḍa ātmamithuna ātmānandaḥ sa svarāḍbhavati
atha ye'nyathāto viduranyarājānaste kṣayyalokā bhavanti

136.1What is vast is happiness. There is no happiness in the small. The vast alone is happiness. But the vast is what must be understood. Where one sees nothing else, hears nothing else, knows nothing else, that is the vast. Where one sees something else, hears something else, knows something else, that is small. The vast is the deathless; the small is mortal. It is below, it is above, it is behind, it is in front, it is to the south, it is to the north. It is all this. The Self is below, the Self is above, the Self is behind, the Self is in front. The Self is all this. One who sees this way, thinks this way, knows this way, takes pleasure in the Self, plays with the Self, has the Self for a companion and the Self for a joy — he is his own ruler. Those who know otherwise are ruled by another, and their worlds pass away.

The word bhuman, here rendered as the vast, is defined not by size but by the absence of a second thing, and the definition is turned into a test in verse 43: whatever is still had alongside something else is the small. The two words in the last line, svaraj and anyaraj, self-ruled and ruled-by-another, are political terms, and the chapter ends by making the whole ladder a question of which of the two one is.

आहारशुद्धौ सत्त्वशुद्धिः सत्त्वशुद्धौ ध्रुवा स्मृतिः ।
स्मृतिलम्भे सर्वग्रन्थीनां विप्रमोक्षः ।
āhāraśuddhau sattvaśuddhiḥ sattvaśuddhau dhruvā smṛtiḥ
smṛtilambhe sarvagranthīnāṃ vipramokṣaḥ

139.2When the food is pure, the nature is pure. When the nature is pure, memory stands firm. When memory is got, all the knots are loosed.

The last sentence is the one the tradition quotes as the bridge between diet and liberation, and the word for food, ahara, means what is taken in generally rather than what is eaten. What follows from purity is not virtue but smriti, memory, and what memory does is untie the knots the Katha also names.

The city of Brahman, and Indra's hundred and one years

अथ यदिदमस्मिन्ब्रह्मपुरे दहरं पुण्डरीकं वेश्म ।
दहरोऽस्मिन्नन्तराकाशस्तस्मिन्यदन्तस्तदन्वेष्टव्यं तद्वाव विजिज्ञासितव्यमिति ।
यावान्वा अयमाकाशस्तावानेषोऽन्तर्हृदय आकाशः ।
उभे अस्मिन् द्यावापृथिवी अन्तरेव समाहिते ।
उभावग्निश्च वायुश्च सूर्याचन्द्रमसावुभौ विद्युन्नक्षत्राणि ।
यच्चास्येहास्ति यच्च नास्ति सर्वं तदस्मिन्समाहितमिति ।
न अस्य जरयैतज्जीर्यति न वधेनास्य हन्यते ।
एतत्सत्यं ब्रह्मपुरमस्मिन् कामाः समाहिताः ।
एष आत्मापहतपाप्मा विजरो विमृत्युर्विशोको विजिघत्सोऽपिपासः सत्यकामः सत्यसंकल्पः ।
atha yadidamasminbrahmapure daharaṃ puṇḍarīkaṃ veśma
daharo'sminnantarākāśastasminyadantastadanveṣṭavyaṃ tadvāva vijijñāsitavyamiti
140.3
yāvānvā ayamākāśastāvāneṣo'ntarhṛdaya ākāśaḥ
ubhe asmin dyāvāpṛthivī antareva samāhite
ubhāvagniśca vāyuśca sūryācandramasāvubhau vidyunnakṣatrāṇi
yaccāsyehāsti yacca nāsti sarvaṃ tadasminsamāhitamiti
140.5
na asya jarayaitajjīryati na vadhenāsya hanyate
etatsatyaṃ brahmapuramasmin kāmāḥ samāhitāḥ
eṣa ātmāpahatapāpmā vijaro vimṛtyurviśoko vijighatso'pipāsaḥ satyakāmaḥ satyasaṃkalpaḥ

140.1Now in this city of Brahman there is a small lotus dwelling, and in it a small space. What is inside that is to be sought, and that is what one should want to know. As great as this space is, so great is the space within the heart. Both heaven and earth are gathered in it, both fire and air, sun and moon, lightning and the stars. Whatever is his here and whatever is not, all of it is gathered in it. It does not grow old with the body's age and is not killed when the body is killed. This is the real city of Brahman, and in it the desires are gathered. It is the Self, free of evil, free of age and death and sorrow, free of hunger and thirst, whose desire is the real and whose resolve is the real.

The passage is about size and it makes a claim that reverses the obvious one. The space inside the heart is not a small copy of the space outside; the two are said to be equal in extent, and it is the outer one that is being measured against the inner. The rest of the chapter gives the consequences: what is inside cannot age with the body that contains it, and what one finds there is not less than what one lost.

य आत्मापहतपाप्मा विजरो विमृत्युर्विशोको विजिघत्सोऽपिपासः ।
सत्यकामः सत्यसंकल्पः सोऽन्वेष्टव्यः स विजिज्ञासितव्यः ।
स सर्वांश्च लोकानाप्नोति सर्वांश्च कामान् ।
इति ह प्रजापतिरुवाच ।
ya ātmāpahatapāpmā vijaro vimṛtyurviśoko vijighatso'pipāsaḥ
satyakāmaḥ satyasaṃkalpaḥ so'nveṣṭavyaḥ sa vijijñāsitavyaḥ
sa sarvāṃśca lokānāpnoti sarvāṃśca kāmān
iti ha prajāpatiruvāca

146.1The Self, free of evil, free of age and death and sorrow, free of hunger and thirst, whose desire is the real and whose resolve is the real — that is what is to be sought, that is what one should want to know. So said Prajapati.

The announcement travels, and two students come to hear it: Indra on behalf of the gods and Virochana on behalf of the anti-gods. They arrive with firewood in their hands, which is how a student presents himself, and they stay thirty-two years before anything is said to them.

उदशराव आत्मानमवेक्ष्य यदात्मनो न विजानीथस्तन्मे प्रब्रूतमिति ।
तौ होदशरावेऽवेक्षाञ्चक्राते । तौ ह प्रजापतिरुवाच किं पश्यथ इति ।
तौ होचतुः सर्वमेवेदमावां भगव आत्मानं पश्याव आ लोमभ्य आ नखेभ्यः प्रतिरूपमिति ।
तौ हान्वीक्ष्य प्रजापतिरुवाच अनुपलभ्यात्मानमननुविद्य व्रजतः ।
यतर एतदुपनिषदो भविष्यन्ति देवा वासुरा वा ते पराभविष्यन्तीति ।
स ह शान्तहृदय एव विरोचनोऽसुराञ्जगाम ।
udaśarāva ātmānamavekṣya yadātmano na vijānīthastanme prabrūtamiti
tau hodaśarāve'vekṣāñcakrāte | tau ha prajāpatiruvāca kiṃ paśyatha iti
tau hocatuḥ sarvamevedamāvāṃ bhagava ātmānaṃ paśyāva ā lomabhya ā nakhebhyaḥ pratirūpamiti
147.4
tau hānvīkṣya prajāpatiruvāca anupalabhyātmānamananuvidya vrajataḥ
yatara etadupaniṣado bhaviṣyanti devā vāsurā vā te parābhaviṣyantīti
sa ha śāntahṛdaya eva virocano'surāñjagāma

147.1Look at yourself in a pan of water, and whatever you do not make out about the Self, tell me. They looked into the pan of water. Prajapati said to them: what do you see? They said: we see the whole of ourselves, sir, right to the hairs and the nails, an exact likeness. Prajapati looked at them and said: they go away without having found the Self and without having recognized it. Whichever of the two, gods or anti-gods, take this as their teaching, they will be defeated. Virochana went back to the anti-gods with a quiet heart.

The test in the pan of water is a trap and the teacher says so out loud after the students have gone. Virochana leaves satisfied because the answer confirms what he came in with, and the doctrine he takes home is that the body is what is to be served. The text then gives the etymology in its own way: people are called godless when they are without faith, and it is they who dress corpses in fine clothes.

यथैव खल्वयमस्मिञ्छरीरे साध्वलंकृते साध्वलंकृतो भवति ।
एवमेवायमस्मिन्नन्धेऽन्धो भवति स्रामे स्रामः परिवृक्णे परिवृक्णः ।
अस्यैव शरीरस्य नाशमन्वेष नश्यति । नाहमत्र भोग्यं पश्यामीति ।
य एष स्वप्ने महीयमानश्चरत्येष आत्मेति होवाच ।
एतदमृतमभयमेतद्ब्रह्मेति ।
न वधेनास्य हन्यते नास्य स्राम्येण स्रामः ।
घ्नन्ति त्वेवैनं विच्छादयन्तीवाप्रियवेत्तेव भवत्यपि रोदितीव ।
नाहमत्र भोग्यं पश्यामीति ।
तद्यत्रैतत्सुप्तः समस्तः सम्प्रसन्नः स्वप्नं न विजानात्येष आत्मेति होवाच ।
नाह खल्वयमेवं सम्प्रत्यात्मानं जानात्ययमहमस्मीति नो एवेमानि भूतानि ।
विनाशमेवापीतो भवति । नाहमत्र भोग्यं पश्यामीति ।
yathaiva khalvayamasmiñcharīre sādhvalaṃkṛte sādhvalaṃkṛto bhavati
evamevāyamasminnandhe'ndho bhavati srāme srāmaḥ parivṛkṇe parivṛkṇaḥ
asyaiva śarīrasya nāśamanveṣa naśyati | nāhamatra bhogyaṃ paśyāmīti
149.1
ya eṣa svapne mahīyamānaścaratyeṣa ātmeti hovāca
etadamṛtamabhayametadbrahmeti
149.2
na vadhenāsya hanyate nāsya srāmyeṇa srāmaḥ
ghnanti tvevainaṃ vicchādayantīvāpriyavetteva bhavatyapi roditīva
nāhamatra bhogyaṃ paśyāmīti
150.1
tadyatraitatsuptaḥ samastaḥ samprasannaḥ svapnaṃ na vijānātyeṣa ātmeti hovāca
nāha khalvayamevaṃ sampratyātmānaṃ jānātyayamahamasmīti no evemāni bhūtāni
vināśamevāpīto bhavati | nāhamatra bhogyaṃ paśyāmīti

148.1Just as this one becomes well-adorned when the body is well-adorned, so this one becomes blind when the body is blind, lame when it is lame, crippled when it is crippled. It perishes when the body perishes. I see nothing to be enjoyed in this. He who moves about in dream, magnified — that is the Self, he said; that is the deathless, the fearless; that is Brahman. It is not killed when the body is killed and is not lame with its lameness. Yet they strike it, they seem to chase it, it seems to feel what is unpleasant, it even seems to weep. I see nothing to be enjoyed in this. When one is asleep, gathered together, serene, and sees no dream — that is the Self, he said. But this one does not now know himself as I am this, nor does he know these beings. He has gone to destruction. I see nothing to be enjoyed in this.

Each of Indra's three objections is his own and each is exact. The reflection fails because it shares every defect of the body; the dreamer fails because it suffers; dreamless sleep fails because there is nobody there to whom the state belongs. Prajapati's reply each time is that Indra is thinking correctly, and the reward for thinking correctly is another term of study.

स हापराणि पञ्च वर्षाण्युवास तान्येकशतं सम्पेदुः ।
एतत्तद्यदाहुरेकशतं ह वै वर्षाणि मघवान् प्रजापतौ ब्रह्मचर्यमुवास ।
मघवन्मर्त्यं वा इदं शरीरमात्तं मृत्युना ।
तदस्यामृतस्याशरीरस्यात्मनोऽधिष्ठानम् ।
आत्तो वै सशरीरः प्रियाप्रियाभ्याम् । अशरीरं वाव सन्तं न प्रियाप्रिये स्पृशतः ।
एवमेवैष सम्प्रसादोऽस्माच्छरीरात्समुत्थाय परं ज्योतिरुपसम्पद्य स्वेन रूपेणाभिनिष्पद्यते ।
स उत्तमपुरुषः । स तत्र पर्येति जक्षत्क्रीडन् रममाणः ।
स यथा प्रयोग्य आचरणे युक्त एवमेवायमस्मिञ्छरीरे प्राणो युक्तः ।
sa hāparāṇi pañca varṣāṇyuvāsa tānyekaśataṃ sampeduḥ
etattadyadāhurekaśataṃ ha vai varṣāṇi maghavān prajāpatau brahmacaryamuvāsa
151.1
maghavanmartyaṃ vā idaṃ śarīramāttaṃ mṛtyunā
tadasyāmṛtasyāśarīrasyātmano'dhiṣṭhānam
ātto vai saśarīraḥ priyāpriyābhyām | aśarīraṃ vāva santaṃ na priyāpriye spṛśataḥ
151.3
evamevaiṣa samprasādo'smāccharīrātsamutthāya paraṃ jyotirupasampadya svena rūpeṇābhiniṣpadyate
sa uttamapuruṣaḥ | sa tatra paryeti jakṣatkrīḍan ramamāṇaḥ
sa yathā prayogya ācaraṇe yukta evamevāyamasmiñcharīre prāṇo yuktaḥ

150.3He lived another five years, which made a hundred and one in all. That is why they say: for a hundred and one years Indra lived as a student with Prajapati. Indra, this body is mortal, held by death. It is the standing place of that deathless, bodiless Self. One who has a body is held by the pleasant and the unpleasant; there is no warding off the pleasant and the unpleasant for one who has a body. The pleasant and the unpleasant do not touch one who is bodiless. So this serene one, rising out of this body and reaching the highest light, comes out in his own form. This is the highest person. He goes about there, laughing, playing, taking his pleasure. As a draught animal is yoked to a cart, so is the breath yoked in this body.

The fourth teaching is not a new object but the removal of one assumption from the third, and the assumption is that the Self has a body. The last comparison is the plainest statement of instrument in the collection: the ox is harnessed to the cart and is not a part of it, and what the passage says about the senses is that they are the harness and not the driver.

Shvetashvatara: the faces of God

The Shvetashvatara belongs to the Taittiriya school of the Black Yajur Veda and is named after a family rather than a doctrine; the name means one who has a white horse, and the tradition reads it as one whose senses are pure, taking the horse for the senses as the Katha does. It is entirely in verse, it uses the vocabulary of classical Sankhya, and it addresses a personal God — first under the Vedic names Rudra and Isha, then by the name Shiva. All three marks make it late. It begins with a list of questions that has no parallel in the collection.

ब्रह्मवादिनो वदन्ति किं कारणं ब्रह्म कुतः स्म जाताः ।
जीवाम केन क्व च सम्प्रतिष्ठा अधिष्ठिताः केन सुखेतरेषु ।
कालः स्वभावो नियतिर्यदृच्छा भूतानि योनिः पुरुष इति चिन्त्या ।
संयोग एषां न त्वात्मभावादात्माप्यनीशः सुखदुःखहेतोः ।
ते ध्यानयोगानुगता अपश्यन् देवात्मशक्तिं स्वगुणैर्निगूढाम् ।
यः कारणानि निखिलानि तानि कालात्मयुक्तान्यधितिष्ठत्येकः ।
brahmavādino vadanti kiṃ kāraṇaṃ brahma kutaḥ sma jātāḥ
jīvāma kena kva ca sampratiṣṭhā adhiṣṭhitāḥ kena sukhetareṣu
1.2
kālaḥ svabhāvo niyatiryadṛcchā bhūtāni yoniḥ puruṣa iti cintyā
saṃyoga eṣāṃ na tvātmabhāvādātmāpyanīśaḥ sukhaduḥkhahetoḥ
1.3
te dhyānayogānugatā apaśyan devātmaśaktiṃ svaguṇairnigūḍhām
yaḥ kāraṇāni nikhilāni tāni kālātmayuktānyadhitiṣṭhatyekaḥ

1.1Those who speak of Brahman say: what is the cause? Brahman? From what are we born? By what do we live? On what are we founded? Governed by whom do we live in pleasure and its opposite? Time, own-nature, necessity, chance, the elements, the womb, the person — these are to be considered. Not their combination, because there is a self; and the self too has no power over the cause of pleasure and pain. Those who followed the discipline of meditation saw the self-power of the god, hidden by its own qualities, who alone governs all those causes, from time to the self.

Seven candidate causes are named in verse 2 and all seven are rejected, including the two that the surrounding philosophies were built on, svabhava, a thing's own nature, and kala, time. The reason given is the same for each: a cause that is itself part of the series cannot account for the series, and the self, being subject to pleasure and pain, is inside it too. The term the sages are said to have seen, devatmashakti, is one compound of three words, and the translator Tyagisananda read the three as independently meaningful, giving the god of religion, the self of philosophy and the energy of science. Easwaran follows him. Other translators read one word.

वह्नेर्यथा योनिगतस्य मूर्तिर्न दृश्यते नैव च लिङ्गनाशः ।
स भूय एवेन्धनयोनिगृह्यस्तद्वोभयं वै प्रणवेन देहे ।
स्वदेहमरणिं कृत्वा प्रणवं चोत्तरारणिम् ।
ध्याननिर्मथनाभ्यासाद्देवं पश्येन्निगूढवत् ।
सर्वव्यापिनमात्मानं क्षीरे सर्पिरिवार्पितम् ।
आत्मविद्यातपोमूलं तद्ब्रह्मोपनिषत् परम् ।
vahneryathā yonigatasya mūrtirna dṛśyate naiva ca liṅganāśaḥ
sa bhūya evendhanayonigṛhyastadvobhayaṃ vai praṇavena dehe
1.14
svadehamaraṇiṃ kṛtvā praṇavaṃ cottarāraṇim
dhyānanirmathanābhyāsāddevaṃ paśyennigūḍhavat
1.16
sarvavyāpinamātmānaṃ kṣīre sarpirivārpitam
ātmavidyātapomūlaṃ tadbrahmopaniṣat param

1.13As the form of fire in its source is not seen and yet its mark is not destroyed, and it may be seized again in its source, the fuel — so both are in the body by means of OM. Making one's own body the lower firestick and OM the upper, one should see the god as hidden, by the practice of the churning that is meditation. The Self pervading all, as butter is set in milk, rooted in self-knowledge and discipline — that is the highest secret of Brahman.

The firesticks are the text's instruction and they are a piece of technology. A Vedic fire was started by twirling an upper stick in a socket cut in a lower one, and the work was hard enough that the priest was permitted to change places with his wife when tired. The word for the practice, abhyasa, is the same word the Gita uses for what makes any discipline work, and what the comparison insists on is the labour. The fire is already in the wood and no amount of that fact lights it.

वेदाहमेतं पुरुषं महान्तमादित्यवर्णं तमसः परस्तात् ।
तमेव विदित्वातिमृत्युमेति नान्यः पन्था विद्यतेऽयनाय ।
सर्वानन शिरोग्रीवः सर्वभूतगुहाशयः
सर्वव्यापी स भगवांस्तस्मात् सर्वगतः शिवः
नवद्वारे पुरे देही हंसो लेलायते बहिः ।
वशी सर्वस्य लोकस्य स्थावरस्य चरस्य च ।
अपाणिपादो जवनो ग्रहीता पश्यत्यचक्षुः स शृणोत्यकर्णः ।
स वेत्ति वेद्यं न च तस्यास्ति वेत्ता तमाहुरग्र्यं पुरुषं महान्तम् ।
vedāhametaṃ puruṣaṃ mahāntamādityavarṇaṃ tamasaḥ parastāt
tameva viditvātimṛtyumeti nānyaḥ panthā vidyate'yanāya
3.11
sarvānana śirogrīvaḥ sarvabhūtaguhāśayaḥ
sarvavyāpī sa bhagavāṃstasmāt sarvagataḥ śivaḥ
3.18
navadvāre pure dehī haṃso lelāyate bahiḥ
vaśī sarvasya lokasya sthāvarasya carasya ca
3.19
apāṇipādo javano grahītā paśyatyacakṣuḥ sa śṛṇotyakarṇaḥ
sa vetti vedyaṃ na ca tasyāsti vettā tamāhuragryaṃ puruṣaṃ mahāntam

3.8I have known this great person, the colour of the sun, beyond darkness. Knowing him alone one goes past death. There is no other road to travel. Whose face and head and neck are everywhere, who lies in the cave of every being, pervading all — he is the Lord, and therefore the all-present and kindly one. In the city of nine gates the body-dweller, the swan, moves out and in. He is the ruler of the whole world, of what stands and what moves. Without hands or feet, he is swift and a grasper; he sees without eyes, he hears without ears. He knows what is to be known, and there is nobody who knows him. They call him the first, the great person.

Verse 8 is where the text changes register. It is a first-person report, not a doctrine: veda aham, I have known, and the last line refuses every alternative road. The adjective in verse 11 is shiva, kindly, and by this point in the text the tradition takes it as a name; Deussen and others hold that the word is still an adjective and that the personal name arrives later. What is not in dispute is the direction of travel. The nine-gated city of verse 18 is the Katha's eleven-gated one with the two openings at the crown and the navel left out.

द्वा सुपर्णा सयुजा सखाया समानं वृक्षं परिषस्वजाते ।
तयोरन्यः पिप्पलं स्वाद्वत्त्यनश्नन्नन्यो अभिचाकशीति ।
समाने वृक्षे पुरुषो निमग्नोऽनीशया शोचति मुह्यमानः ।
जुष्टं यदा पश्यत्यन्यमीशमस्य महिमानमिति वीतशोकः ।
मायां तु प्रकृतिं विद्यान्मायिनं च महेश्वरम् ।
तस्यावयवभूतैस्तु व्याप्तं सर्वमिदं जगत् ।
dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyā samānaṃ vṛkṣaṃ pariṣasvajāte
tayoranyaḥ pippalaṃ svādvattyanaśnannanyo abhicākaśīti
4.7
samāne vṛkṣe puruṣo nimagno'nīśayā śocati muhyamānaḥ
juṣṭaṃ yadā paśyatyanyamīśamasya mahimānamiti vītaśokaḥ
4.10
māyāṃ tu prakṛtiṃ vidyānmāyinaṃ ca maheśvaram
tasyāvayavabhūtaistu vyāptaṃ sarvamidaṃ jagat

4.6Two birds, companions and friends, cling to the same tree. One of them eats the sweet fig; the other looks on without eating. On the same tree a person is sunk, grieving in helplessness, bewildered. When he sees the other, the lord, content, and his greatness, he is free of sorrow. Know nature to be the magic, and the great lord to be the magician. This whole world is filled with things that are parts of him.

The two birds are a verse of the Rig Veda that both this text and the Mundaka take over unchanged, and the second verse is the one that does the work. Nothing happens to the tree and nothing happens to the fruit. What changes is that the eater notices the other bird, and the grief he is described as feeling is called anisha, without power, which is the same word the first chapter used of the self that could not be the cause. Verse 10 supplies the term the later tradition took from this text above all others: maya is nature considered as a display, and the one who displays it is the lord. Nagler's gloss, which Easwaran uses throughout, is that the appearance in question is not the world but the separateness.

एको देवः सर्वभूतेषु गूढः सर्वव्यापी सर्वभूतान्तरात्मा ।
कर्माध्यक्षः सर्वभूताधिवासः साक्षी चेता केवलो निर्गुणश्च ।
एको वशी निष्क्रियाणां बहूनामेकं बीजं बहुधा यः करोति ।
तमात्मस्थं येऽनुपश्यन्ति धीरास्तेषां सुखं शाश्वतं नेतरेषाम् ।
यदा चर्मवदाकाशं वेष्टयिष्यन्ति मानवाः ।
तदा देवमविज्ञाय दुःखस्यान्तो भविष्यति ।
यस्य देवे परा भक्तिर्यथा देवे तथा गुरौ ।
तस्यैते कथिता ह्यर्थाः प्रकाशन्ते महात्मनः ।
eko devaḥ sarvabhūteṣu gūḍhaḥ sarvavyāpī sarvabhūtāntarātmā
karmādhyakṣaḥ sarvabhūtādhivāsaḥ sākṣī cetā kevalo nirguṇaśca
6.12
eko vaśī niṣkriyāṇāṃ bahūnāmekaṃ bījaṃ bahudhā yaḥ karoti
tamātmasthaṃ ye'nupaśyanti dhīrāsteṣāṃ sukhaṃ śāśvataṃ netareṣām
6.20
yadā carmavadākāśaṃ veṣṭayiṣyanti mānavāḥ
tadā devamavijñāya duḥkhasyānto bhaviṣyati
6.23
yasya deve parā bhaktiryathā deve tathā gurau
tasyaite kathitā hyarthāḥ prakāśante mahātmanaḥ

6.11One god, hidden in all beings, pervading all, the inner self of all beings, overseer of works, dwelling in all beings, the witness, the knower, alone and without qualities. The one ruler of the many who do not act, who makes one seed into many — the wise who see him standing in the self have lasting happiness, and no others.
