When men roll up space as if it were a skin, then, without knowing the god, there will be an end to sorrow. For one who has the highest love for God, and as for God so for his teacher, these things spoken of shine out.

The figure in verse 20 is an impossibility used as a measure: sorrow will end without this knowledge on the day the sky can be rolled up like a hide. The last verse of the text is the one Shankara quoted most from it, and it is the earliest place in the collection where bhakti, love, is named as the condition of understanding — and named twice, once for God and once for the teacher, in the same measure.

Mundaka: the two knowledges

The Mundaka belongs to the Atharva Veda. Its name is from munda, shaved, which may point to an order of ascetics for whom it was meant, or, as the commentary prefers, to those shorn of attachment to what is not the Self. Its six sections are counted straight through here. It is the most quoted of the Upanishads in modern India, because four of its words were made the motto of the republic, and it is the most balanced in construction: a lineage, a question, a distinction, a cosmogony, a method, a result.

शौनको ह वै महाशालोऽङ्गिरसं विधिवदुपसन्नः पप्रच्छ
कस्मिन्नु भगवो विज्ञाते सर्वमिदं विज्ञातं भवतीति
तस्मै स होवाच
द्वे विद्ये वेदितव्ये इति ह स्म
यद्ब्रह्मविदो वदन्ति परा चैवापरा च
तत्रापरा ऋग्वेदो यजुर्वेदः सामवेदोऽथर्ववेदः
शिक्षा कल्पो व्याकरणं निरुक्तं छन्दो ज्योतिषमिति
अथ परा यया तदक्षरमधिगम्यते
śaunako ha vai mahāśālo'ṅgirasaṃ vidhivadupasannaḥ papraccha
kasminnu bhagavo vijñāte sarvamidaṃ vijñātaṃ bhavatīti
1.4
tasmai sa hovāca
dve vidye veditavye iti ha sma
yadbrahmavido vadanti parā caivāparā ca
1.5
tatrāparā ṛgvedo yajurvedaḥ sāmavedo'tharvavedaḥ
śikṣā kalpo vyākaraṇaṃ niruktaṃ chando jyotiṣamiti
atha parā yayā tadakṣaramadhigamyate

1.3Shaunaka, a great householder, approached Angiras in the proper way and asked: what is it, sir, by knowing which all this becomes known? He said to him: two knowledges are to be known, so say those who know Brahman — the higher and the lower. Of these the lower is the Rig Veda, the Yajur Veda, the Sama Veda, the Atharva Veda, pronunciation, ritual, grammar, etymology, metre and astronomy. The higher is that by which the imperishable is reached.

The list of the lower knowledge is everything the Vedic schools taught, and it is put in second place by the text that is itself part of it. The words are para and apara, beyond and not beyond, and the distinction is not between religious and secular learning. The four Vedas are on the lower side. What is on the higher side is defined only by what it reaches, which is why Nagler prefers to call it realization rather than knowledge.

यत्तदद्रेश्यमग्राह्यमगोत्रमवर्णमचक्षुःश्रोत्रं तदपाणिपादम् ।
नित्यं विभुं सर्वगतं सुसूक्ष्मं तदव्ययं यद्भूतयोनिं परिपश्यन्ति धीराः ।
यथोर्णनाभिः सृजते गृह्णते च यथा पृथिव्यामोषधयः सम्भवन्ति ।
यथा सतः पुरुषात्केशलोमानि तथाक्षरात्सम्भवतीह विश्वम् ।
yattadadreśyamagrāhyamagotramavarṇamacakṣuḥśrotraṃ tadapāṇipādam
nityaṃ vibhuṃ sarvagataṃ susūkṣmaṃ tadavyayaṃ yadbhūtayoniṃ paripaśyanti dhīrāḥ
1.7
yathorṇanābhiḥ sṛjate gṛhṇate ca yathā pṛthivyāmoṣadhayaḥ sambhavanti
yathā sataḥ puruṣātkeśalomāni tathākṣarātsambhavatīha viśvam

1.6That which is not to be seen, not to be grasped, without family or class, without eye or ear, without hands or feet, everlasting, all-pervading, present everywhere, very fine, undecaying — the wise see it as the womb of beings. As the spider puts out and draws in, as plants come up from the earth, as hair grows from a living man, so the whole world comes here from the imperishable.

The three comparisons in verse 7 are chosen for one property. Nothing outside is used in any of them: the spider's thread is the spider, the plant is the earth, the hair is the man. The clause about drawing in is not decoration either, since the same act runs in both directions and no separate destruction is needed. The words denied in verse 6, gotra and varna, are the two social categories by which people place one another, and the denial reads as metaphysics and as something else at the same time.

प्लवा ह्येते अदृढा यज्ञरूपा अष्टादशोक्तमवरं येषु कर्म ।
एतच्छ्रेयो येऽभिनन्दन्ति मूढा जरामृत्युं ते पुनरेवापि यन्ति ।
अविद्यायामन्तरे वर्तमानाः स्वयं धीराः पण्डितंमन्यमानाः ।
जङ्घन्यमानाः परियन्ति मूढा अन्धेनैव नीयमाना यथान्धाः ।
परीक्ष्य लोकान् कर्मचितान् ब्राह्मणो निर्वेदमायान्नास्त्यकृतः कृतेन ।
तद्विज्ञानार्थं स गुरुमेवाभिगच्छेत् समित्पाणिः श्रोत्रियं ब्रह्मनिष्ठम् ।
plavā hyete adṛḍhā yajñarūpā aṣṭādaśoktamavaraṃ yeṣu karma
etacchreyo ye'bhinandanti mūḍhā jarāmṛtyuṃ te punarevāpi yanti
2.8
avidyāyāmantare vartamānāḥ svayaṃ dhīrāḥ paṇḍitaṃmanyamānāḥ
jaṅghanyamānāḥ pariyanti mūḍhā andhenaiva nīyamānā yathāndhāḥ
2.12
parīkṣya lokān karmacitān brāhmaṇo nirvedamāyānnāstyakṛtaḥ kṛtena
tadvijñānārthaṃ sa gurumevābhigacchet samitpāṇiḥ śrotriyaṃ brahmaniṣṭham

2.7These rafts are weak — the eighteen forms of sacrifice in which the lower work is told. Those fools who welcome this as the better go again to old age and death. Living in the middle of ignorance, wise in their own eyes, thinking themselves learned, the fools go round and round, battered, like blind men led by one who is blind. Having examined the worlds got by works, a brahmin should come to indifference: what is not made is not got by what is made. For the knowledge of that he should go, fuel in hand, to a teacher who has heard and who is settled in Brahman.

Verse 7 is the harshest sentence in the collection about the religion the collection is attached to. The eighteen are the sixteen priests of the great sacrifice with the patron and his wife, and the word for what they are is plava, a raft or float, with adridha, not firm, beside it. Verse 12 states the reason in one clause that is also a rule of logic: nasty akritah kritena, what is not made is not obtained by what is made, and no quantity of works alters the kind of thing works produce.

यथा सुदीप्तात्पावकाद्विस्फुलिङ्गाः सहस्रशः प्रभवन्ते सरूपाः ।
तथाक्षराद्विविधाः सोम्य भावाः प्रजायन्ते तत्र चैवापियन्ति ।
धनुर्गृहीत्वौपनिषदं महास्त्रं शरं ह्युपासानिशितं सन्धयीत ।
आयम्य तद्भावगतेन चेतसा लक्ष्यं तदेवाक्षरं सोम्य विद्धि ।
प्रणवो धनुः शरो ह्यात्मा ब्रह्म तल्लक्ष्यमुच्यते
अप्रमत्तेन वेद्धव्यं शरवत्तन्मयो भवेत्
yathā sudīptātpāvakādvisphuliṅgāḥ sahasraśaḥ prabhavante sarūpāḥ
tathākṣarādvividhāḥ somya bhāvāḥ prajāyante tatra caivāpiyanti
4.3
dhanurgṛhītvaupaniṣadaṃ mahāstraṃ śaraṃ hyupāsāniśitaṃ sandhayīta
āyamya tadbhāvagatena cetasā lakṣyaṃ tadevākṣaraṃ somya viddhi
4.4
praṇavo dhanuḥ śaro hyātmā brahma tallakṣyamucyate
apramattena veddhavyaṃ śaravattanmayo bhavet

3.1As from a well-lit fire sparks of the same kind come forth by the thousand, so, dear one, various beings are born from the imperishable, and go back into it. Taking the great weapon, the bow of the mystical teaching, one should fit to it the arrow sharpened by worship. Drawing it with a mind gone to the same being, know the target to be the imperishable, dear one. OM is the bow; the self is the arrow; Brahman is called the target. It is to be pierced by one who is not careless. Like the arrow, one should become one with it.

The image is given twice and the terms move. In the first version the archer holds a bow made of scripture and shoots devotion at God. In the second the bow is OM, the arrow is the person doing the shooting, and the instruction is not to hit the target but to become it. The word in the first version is aupanishadam, of the upanishads, which the commentary takes to mean any text of mystical truth rather than a named book.

द्वा सुपर्णा सयुजा सखाया समानं वृक्षं परिषस्वजाते
तयोरन्यः पिप्पलं स्वाद्वत्त्यनश्नन्नन्यो अभिचाकशीति
समाने वृक्षे पुरुषो निमग्नोऽनीशया शोचति मुह्यमानः ।
जुष्टं यदा पश्यत्यन्यमीशमस्य महिमानमिति वीतशोकः ।
यदा पश्यः पश्यते रुक्मवर्णं कर्तारमीशं पुरुषं ब्रह्मयोनिम् ।
तदा विद्वान् पुण्यपापे विधूय निरञ्जनः परमं साम्यमुपैति ।
सत्यमेव जयते नानृतं सत्येन पन्था विततो देवयानः ।
येनाक्रमन्त्यृषयो ह्याप्तकामा यत्र तत्सत्यस्य परमं निधानम् ।
dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyā samānaṃ vṛkṣaṃ pariṣasvajāte
tayoranyaḥ pippalaṃ svādvattyanaśnannanyo abhicākaśīti
5.2
samāne vṛkṣe puruṣo nimagno'nīśayā śocati muhyamānaḥ
juṣṭaṃ yadā paśyatyanyamīśamasya mahimānamiti vītaśokaḥ
5.3
yadā paśyaḥ paśyate rukmavarṇaṃ kartāramīśaṃ puruṣaṃ brahmayonim
tadā vidvān puṇyapāpe vidhūya nirañjanaḥ paramaṃ sāmyamupaiti
5.6
satyameva jayate nānṛtaṃ satyena panthā vitato devayānaḥ
yenākramantyṛṣayo hyāptakāmā yatra tatsatyasya paramaṃ nidhānam

5.1Two birds, companions and friends, cling to the same tree. One of them eats the sweet fig; the other looks on without eating. On the same tree a person is sunk, grieving in helplessness, bewildered. When he sees the other, the lord, content, and his greatness, he is free of sorrow. When the seer sees the golden-coloured one, the maker, the lord, the person, the womb of Brahman, then the knower shakes off good and evil, and, stainless, reaches the highest likeness. Truth alone wins, not untruth. By truth the road is laid out, the road the gods go, by which the sages, having got what they wanted, climb to where the highest treasure of truth is.

The four words of verse 6 are on the seal of the Indian republic, and what is usually cut from the quotation is the second half, which says what the winning consists of: a road, and people walking on it. The word for untruth is anritam, which carries the sense of being out of the rhythm, rita, of things. Gandhi's use of the verse turned on the older sense of sat: it means the true, the real and the good at once, so that what is false is also what does not exist, and what does not exist cannot win.

न चक्षुषा गृह्यते नापि वाचा नान्यैर्देवैस्तपसा कर्मणा वा ।
ज्ञानप्रसादेन विशुद्धसत्त्वस्ततस्तु तं पश्यते निष्कलं ध्यायमानः ।
नायमात्मा प्रवचनेन लभ्यो
न मेधया न बहुना श्रुतेन
यमेवैष वृणुते तेन लभ्य-
स्तस्यैष आत्मा विवृणुते तनूं स्वाम्
यथा नद्यः स्यन्दमानाः समुद्रेऽस्तं गच्छन्ति नामरूपे विहाय ।
तथा विद्वान् नामरूपाद्विमुक्तः परात्परं पुरुषमुपैति दिव्यम् ।
स यो ह वै तत्परमं ब्रह्म वेद ब्रह्मैव भवति ।
तरति शोकं तरति पाप्मानं गुहाग्रन्थिभ्यो विमुक्तोऽमृतो भवति ।
na cakṣuṣā gṛhyate nāpi vācā nānyairdevaistapasā karmaṇā vā
jñānaprasādena viśuddhasattvastatastu taṃ paśyate niṣkalaṃ dhyāyamānaḥ
6.3
nāyamātmā pravacanena labhyo
na medhayā na bahunā śrutena
yamevaiṣa vṛṇute tena labhya-
stasyaiṣa ātmā vivṛṇute tanūṃ svām
6.8
yathā nadyaḥ syandamānāḥ samudre'staṃ gacchanti nāmarūpe vihāya
tathā vidvān nāmarūpādvimuktaḥ parātparaṃ puruṣamupaiti divyam
6.9
sa yo ha vai tatparamaṃ brahma veda brahmaiva bhavati
tarati śokaṃ tarati pāpmānaṃ guhāgranthibhyo vimukto'mṛto bhavati

5.8He is not grasped by the eye, nor by speech, nor by the other powers, nor by austerity or work. By the clearness of knowing, one whose being has been made pure sees the undivided one, meditating. This Self is not to be won by teaching, nor by intelligence, nor by much hearing. It is won by the one whom it chooses; to that one the Self uncovers its own body. As rivers, flowing, go to their setting in the sea, leaving name and shape behind, so the knower, freed from name and shape, reaches the divine person beyond the beyond. Whoever knows that highest Brahman becomes Brahman. He crosses sorrow, he crosses wrong, and, freed from the knots in the cave of the heart, becomes deathless.

Verse 64.3 is the Katha's verse, word for word, and it is the clearest case in the collection of a line that belongs to no one text. The rivers in verse 8 are the same rivers the Chandogya used, but the point is different: there the fish was that the rivers do not know, here that what is lost is nama-rupa, name and shape, which is exactly the pair the Chandogya's clay and gold had already identified as the only thing distinguishing one pot from another.

Mandukya: consciousness and its phases

The Mandukya is the shortest of the principal Upanishads, twelve sentences of prose, and Shankara said that if one could study only one, it should be this. Its second sentence carries one of the four great formulae, ayam atma brahma, this Self is Brahman. It belongs to the Atharva Veda, like the other texts that centre their teaching on OM. Its name probably comes from manduka, frog, as the Taittiriya's comes from a bird, though it may simply have been the name of the sage. It was and is held to be the hardest of them to construe accurately, and it acquired the most consequential commentary in the tradition: Gaudapada, the teacher of Shankara's teacher, wrote two hundred and fifteen verses on it, the earliest exposition of Vedanta that survives, and one that argues its case on intellectual grounds without leaning on scriptural authority.

ओमित्येतदक्षरमिदं सर्वं तस्योपव्याख्यानं
भूतं भवद् भविष्यदिति सर्वमोङ्कार एव
यच्चान्यत् त्रिकालातीतं तदप्योङ्कार एव ॥ १ ॥
सर्वं ह्येतद् ब्रह्मायमात्मा ब्रह्म सोऽयमात्मा चतुष्पात् ॥ २ ॥
omityetadakṣaramidaṃ sarvaṃ tasyopavyākhyānaṃ
bhūtaṃ bhavad bhaviṣyaditi sarvamoṅkāra eva
yaccānyat trikālātītaṃ tadapyoṅkāra eva
2
sarvaṃ hyetad brahmāyamātmā brahma so'yamātmā catuṣpāt

1OM: this syllable is all this. Its explanation is this — what was, what is and what will be, all of it is OM; and whatever else there is beyond the three times, that too is OM. For all this is Brahman. This Self is Brahman. This Self has four quarters.

The word rendered as quarters is pada, foot, and the expression is literally that the Self is four-footed. It is used of the cosmos as well, which is said to have stood on the four feet of Brahman at the beginning of a cycle and now to totter on one. The text then walks through the four in order.

जागरितस्थानो बहिष्प्रज्ञः सप्ताङ्ग एकोनविंशतिमुखः
स्थूलभुग्वैश्वानरः प्रथमः पादः ॥ ३ ॥
स्वप्नस्थानोऽन्तःप्रज्ञः सप्ताङ्ग एकोनविंशतिमुखः
प्रविविक्तभुक्तैजसो द्वितीयः पादः ॥ ४ ॥
यत्र सुप्तो न कञ्चन कामं कामयते न कञ्चन स्वप्नं
पश्यति तत् सुषुप्तम् । सुषुप्तस्थान एकीभूतः प्रज्ञानघन
एवानन्दमयो ह्यानन्दभुक् चेतोमुखः प्राज्ञस्तृतीयः पादः ॥ ५ ॥
एष सर्वेश्वरः एष सर्वज्ञ एषोऽन्तर्याम्येष योनिः सर्वस्य
प्रभवाप्ययौ हि भूतानाम् ॥ ६ ॥
jāgaritasthāno bahiṣprajñaḥ saptāṅga ekonaviṃśatimukhaḥ
sthūlabhugvaiśvānaraḥ prathamaḥ pādaḥ
4
svapnasthāno'ntaḥprajñaḥ saptāṅga ekonaviṃśatimukhaḥ
praviviktabhuktaijaso dvitīyaḥ pādaḥ
5
yatra supto na kañcana kāmaṃ kāmayate na kañcana svapnaṃ
paśyati tat suṣuptam | suṣuptasthāna ekībhūtaḥ prajñānaghana
evānandamayo hyānandabhuk cetomukhaḥ prājñastṛtīyaḥ pādaḥ
6
eṣa sarveśvaraḥ eṣa sarvajña eṣo'ntaryāmyeṣa yoniḥ sarvasya
prabhavāpyayau hi bhūtānām

3The waking state, knowing what is outside, seven-limbed, nineteen-mouthed, eating the gross: Vaishvanara, the first quarter. The dream state, knowing what is inside, seven-limbed, nineteen-mouthed, eating the fine: Taijasa, the second quarter. Where one asleep desires no desire and sees no dream, that is deep sleep. The deep-sleep state, become one, a single mass of knowing, made of bliss, eating bliss, whose mouth is thought: Prajna, the third quarter. This is the lord of all, this the knower of all, this the inner controller. This is the womb of all, the coming forth and the going back of beings.

The seven limbs and nineteen mouths are one of the standard tallies of the organs of action and perception. Vaishvanara is made of vishva, all, and nara, man; Shankara reads it as the one who leads all men to their enjoyments. Taijasa is from tejas, a word for the splendour of awareness that can also mean sexual energy. Prajna is from jna, to know, with an intensifying prefix. What is striking about the sixth sentence is that the third state is suddenly described as a being rather than a state — lord, knower, inner controller — and the reason Nagler gives is that of the three states available before illumination, deep sleep is the one in which the Self is closest to what it is. The mind and body do rest in it, as sleep research finds, but the sleeper is not aware of the rest.

नान्तःप्रज्ञं न बहिष्प्रज्ञं नोभयतःप्रज्ञं न प्रज्ञानघनं
न प्रज्ञं नाप्रज्ञम् । अदृष्टमव्यवहार्यमग्राह्यमलक्षणं
अचिन्त्यमव्यपदेश्यमेकात्मप्रत्ययसारं प्रपञ्चोपशमं
शान्तं शिवमद्वैतं चतुर्थं मन्यन्ते स आत्मा स विज्ञेयः ॥ ७ ॥
nāntaḥprajñaṃ na bahiṣprajñaṃ nobhayataḥprajñaṃ na prajñānaghanaṃ
na prajñaṃ nāprajñam | adṛṣṭamavyavahāryamagrāhyamalakṣaṇaṃ
acintyamavyapadeśyamekātmapratyayasāraṃ prapañcopaśamaṃ
śāntaṃ śivamadvaitaṃ caturthaṃ manyante sa ātmā sa vijñeyaḥ

7Not knowing what is inside, not knowing what is outside, not knowing both, not a mass of knowing, not knowing, not unknowing; unseen, not to be dealt with, not to be grasped, without a mark, not to be thought, not to be pointed at, whose essence is the certainty of the one Self, in which the spread-out world has come to rest, quiet, kindly, without a second: the fourth is what they hold this to be. That is the Self. That is what is to be known.

The seventh sentence is the centre of the text and it is written entirely in denials. Six of them are about knowing and rule out every position on the map that the first three quarters have just laid down: it is not inward knowing, not outward knowing, not both at once, not the undifferentiated mass of knowing that deep sleep is, not knowing and not its absence. Six more deny it every handle by which anything is usually taken hold of — sight, transaction, grasp, mark, thought, reference. Only then do three positive words arrive, and they are shanta, shiva, advaita: quiet, kindly, without a second. Turiya means the fourth and nothing more; Nagler is explicit that it is not a fourth state of consciousness alongside the others but consciousness itself, and that the ordinal records only the order in which one arrives.

सोऽयमात्माध्यक्षरमोङ्कारोऽधिमात्रं पादा मात्रा मात्राश्च पादा
अकार उकारो मकार इति ॥ ८ ॥
जागरितस्थानो वैश्वानरोऽकारः प्रथमा मात्राऽऽप्तेरादिमत्त्वाद्
वाऽऽप्नोति ह वै सर्वान् कामानादिश्च भवति य एवं वेद ॥ ९ ॥
स्वप्नस्थानस्तैजस उकारो द्वितीया मात्रोत्कर्षात्
उभयत्वाद्वोत्कर्षति ह वै ज्ञानसन्ततिं समानश्च भवति
नास्याब्रह्मवित्कुले भवति य एवं वेद ॥ १० ॥
सुषुप्तस्थानः प्राज्ञो मकारस्तृतीया मात्रा मितेरपीतेर्वा
मिनोति ह वा इदं सर्वमपीतिश्च भवति य एवं वेद ॥ ११ ॥
अमात्रश्चतुर्थोऽव्यवहार्यः प्रपञ्चोपशमः शिवोऽद्वैत
एवमोङ्कार आत्मैव संविशत्यात्मनाऽऽत्मानं य एवं वेद ॥ १२ ॥
so'yamātmādhyakṣaramoṅkāro'dhimātraṃ pādā mātrā mātrāśca pādā
akāra ukāro makāra iti
9
jāgaritasthāno vaiśvānaro'kāraḥ prathamā mātrā''pterādimattvād
vā''pnoti ha vai sarvān kāmānādiśca bhavati ya evaṃ veda
10
svapnasthānastaijasa ukāro dvitīyā mātrotkarṣāt
ubhayatvādvotkarṣati ha vai jñānasantatiṃ samānaśca bhavati
nāsyābrahmavitkule bhavati ya evaṃ veda
11
suṣuptasthānaḥ prājño makārastṛtīyā mātrā miterapītervā
minoti ha vā idaṃ sarvamapītiśca bhavati ya evaṃ veda
12
amātraścaturtho'vyavahāryaḥ prapañcopaśamaḥ śivo'dvaita
evamoṅkāra ātmaiva saṃviśatyātmanā''tmānaṃ ya evaṃ veda

8This Self, with respect to the syllable, is OM; with respect to the parts, the quarters are the sounds and the sounds the quarters — a, u and m. The waking state, Vaishvanara, is the sound a, the first part, from reaching or from being first. One who knows this reaches all desires and becomes first. The dream state, Taijasa, is the sound u, the second part, from being higher or from being between. One who knows this raises the run of his knowing, becomes even, and has in his line nobody who does not know Brahman. The deep-sleep state, Prajna, is the sound m, the third part, from measuring or from taking in. One who knows this measures all this and takes it in. The fourth is without parts, not to be dealt with, the spread-out world at rest, kindly, without a second. So OM is the Self. One who knows this enters the Self by the Self.

The second pass is the same ground taken in sound rather than in states. Each identification is justified by a pun on the sound's name, and the puns are not decoration: a is the first letter and waking the first experience, u is what lies between, m is the closure that ends the utterance, and a and u are pronounced from the throat outwards and seem to merge into m. The fourth sound is the one that is not a sound. What the arrangement offers, in Nagler's reading, is a way to climb the chain rather than only to describe it, since OM is also what is repeated, and the Mandukya's claim is that every such repetition in every tradition leads back to this one and then past it.

Kena: by whom?

The Kena belongs to the Sama Veda and used to be called by its school's name, Talavakara. Its present title is its first word, and the word is a question: by whom. Shankara is said to have written two commentaries on it. It has four chapters: the first two are verse and argue, the last two are prose and tell a story. Nagler's framing is that the Kena asks the question modern science reached late: not what the world is made of but who the investigator is, and how the instrument of knowing is supposed to turn round and know itself.

केनेषितं पतति प्रेषितं मनः
केन प्राणः प्रथमः प्रैति युक्तः ।
केनेषितां वाचमिमां वदन्ति
चक्षुः श्रोत्रं क उ देवो युनक्ति ॥ १ ॥
श्रोत्रस्य श्रोत्रं मनसो मनो यद्
वाचो ह वाचं स उ प्राणस्य प्राणः ।
चक्षुषश्चक्षुरतिमुच्य धीराः
प्रेत्यास्माल्लोकादमृता भवन्ति ॥ २ ॥
keneṣitaṃ patati preṣitaṃ manaḥ
kena prāṇaḥ prathamaḥ praiti yuktaḥ
keneṣitāṃ vācamimāṃ vadanti
cakṣuḥ śrotraṃ ka u devo yunakti
1.2
śrotrasya śrotraṃ manaso mano yad
vāco ha vācaṃ sa u prāṇasya prāṇaḥ
cakṣuṣaścakṣuratimucya dhīrāḥ
pretyāsmāllokādamṛtā bhavanti

1.1By whom impelled and sent out does the mind fly to its object? By whom yoked does the first breath go on its way? By whom impelled is this speech that men speak? What god harnesses the eye and the ear? The ear of the ear, the mind of the mind, the speech of speech, the breath of the breath, the eye of the eye. Letting these go, the wise, gone from this world, become deathless.

The answer is given before the argument, and it is given as a grammatical trick that is also the whole doctrine. Whatever the ear is to sound, something stands in that relation to the ear. Shankara's paraphrase of the opening question, which Nagler quotes, is sharper than the verse: by whose mere presence does that desire arise which moves the universe. The mind is on the list of senses because in this scheme it is one, and the chief one; the word translated as sense is indriya, a power or faculty.

न तत्र चक्षुर्गच्छति न वाग्गच्छति नो मनः ।
न विद्मो न विजानीमो यथैतदनुशिष्यात् ॥ ३ ॥
अन्यदेव तद्विदितादथो अविदितादधि ।
इति शुश्रुम पूर्वेषां ये नस्तद्व्याचचक्षिरे ॥ ४ ॥
यद्वाचाऽनभ्युदितं येन वागभ्युद्यते ।
तदेव ब्रह्म त्वं विद्धि नेदं यदिदमुपासते ॥ ५ ॥
यन्मनसा न मनुते येनाहुर्मनो मतम् ।
तदेव ब्रह्म त्वं विद्धि नेदं यदिदमुपासते ॥ ६ ॥
na tatra cakṣurgacchati na vāggacchati no manaḥ
na vidmo na vijānīmo yathaitadanuśiṣyāt
1.4
anyadeva tadviditādatho aviditādadhi
iti śuśruma pūrveṣāṃ ye nastadvyācacakṣire
1.5
yadvācā'nabhyuditaṃ yena vāgabhyudyate
tadeva brahma tvaṃ viddhi nedaṃ yadidamupāsate
1.6
yanmanasā na manute yenāhurmano matam
tadeva brahma tvaṃ viddhi nedaṃ yadidamupāsate

1.3There the eye does not go, nor speech, nor the mind. We do not know it; we do not understand how one would teach it. It is other than the known, and it is above the unknown. So we have heard from the ancients who explained it to us. That which is not spoken by speech, by which speech is spoken: know that alone to be Brahman, not this that people worship here. That which one does not think with the mind, by which, they say, the mind is thought: know that alone to be Brahman, not this that people worship here.

The admission in verse 3 is unusual and it is made in the first person plural: we do not know, and we do not see how it would be taught. What follows is not a retraction but a change of method, since verse 4 places the thing outside both of the categories a question can use. It is not among the known and it is not among the not-yet-known either, which rules out the possibility that more looking would turn it up.

यच्चक्षुषा न पश्यति येन चक्षूंषि पश्यति ।
तदेव ब्रह्म त्वं विद्धि नेदं यदिदमुपासते ॥ ७ ॥
यच्छ्रोत्रेण न श‍ृणोति येन श्रोत्रमिदं श्रुतम् ।
तदेव ब्रह्म त्वं विद्धि नेदं यदिदमुपासते ॥ ८ ॥
यत्प्राणेन न प्राणिति येन प्राणः प्रणीयते ।
तदेव ब्रह्म त्वं विद्धि नेदं यदिदमुपासते ॥ ९ ॥
yaccakṣuṣā na paśyati yena cakṣūṃṣi paśyati
tadeva brahma tvaṃ viddhi nedaṃ yadidamupāsate
1.8
yacchrotreṇa na śaṛṇoti yena śrotramidaṃ śrutam
tadeva brahma tvaṃ viddhi nedaṃ yadidamupāsate
1.9
yatprāṇena na prāṇiti yena prāṇaḥ praṇīyate
tadeva brahma tvaṃ viddhi nedaṃ yadidamupāsate

1.7That which one does not see with the eye, by which the eyes are seen: know that alone to be Brahman, not this that people worship here. That which one does not hear with the ear, by which this hearing is heard: know that alone to be Brahman, not this that people worship here. That which one does not breathe with the breath, by which the breath is breathed on: know that alone to be Brahman, not this that people worship here.

The refrain runs five times, once for speech, mind, eye, ear and breath, and it is the same sentence each time with one word changed. The point of the repetition is not emphasis but coverage: every channel by which anything is ever known is enumerated and then disqualified. The second half of each verse is the sharper half. Not this that people worship here: whatever is set up as an object of worship is by that act made an object, and so is not it. Nagler sets Meister Eckhart beside it, on the people who expect to see God the way they see a cow.

यदि मन्यसे सुवेदेति दहरमेवापि
नूनं त्वं वेत्थ ब्रह्मणो रूपम् ।
यदस्य त्वं यदस्य देवेष्वथ नु
मीमांस्यमेव ते मन्ये विदितम् ॥ १ ॥
नाहं मन्ये सुवेदेति नो न वेदेति वेद च ।
यो नस्तद्वेद तद्वेद नो न वेदेति वेद च ॥ २ ॥
यस्यामतं तस्य मतं मतं यस्य न वेद सः ।
अविज्ञातं विजानतां विज्ञातमविजानताम् ॥ ३ ॥
प्रतिबोधविदितं मतममृतत्वं हि विन्दते ।
आत्मना विन्दते वीर्यं विद्यया विन्दतेऽमृतम् ॥ ४ ॥
yadi manyase suvedeti daharamevāpi
nūnaṃ tvaṃ vettha brahmaṇo rūpam
yadasya tvaṃ yadasya deveṣvatha nu
mīmāṃsyameva te manye viditam
2.2
nāhaṃ manye suvedeti no na vedeti veda ca
yo nastadveda tadveda no na vedeti veda ca
2.3
yasyāmataṃ tasya mataṃ mataṃ yasya na veda saḥ
avijñātaṃ vijānatāṃ vijñātamavijānatām
2.4
pratibodhaviditaṃ matamamṛtatvaṃ hi vindate
ātmanā vindate vīryaṃ vidyayā vindate'mṛtam

2.1If you think you know it well, then you know little indeed of Brahman's form — that of it which is yours, and that of it which is among the gods. So I think it is still to be looked into by you. I do not think I know it well; nor do I think that I do not know. I know and I do not know. Whoever among us understands that, understands it. He who thinks he does not know it, knows. He who thinks he knows it, does not. It is unknown to those who know, and known to those who do not know. It is known in an awakening, and so one finds the deathless. By oneself one finds strength, and by knowledge one finds the deathless.

The second chapter is where the Kena gets its reputation for difficulty, and the difficulty is deliberate. The teacher's opening move is to take away the student's gain: if you think you know it well, you know very little. The student's reply is not modesty but an exact report — I do not know that I know and I do not know that I do not know — and the teacher's summary in verse 3 states the rule that generates both. The word in verse 4 is pratibodha-viditam, known in an awakening: not known as a conclusion but known in the act of knowing anything at all, which is the same claim the first chapter made from the other direction.

इह चेदवेदीदथ सत्यमस्ति
न चेदिहावेदीन्महती विनष्टिः ।
भूतेषु भूतेषु विचित्य धीराः
प्रेत्यास्माल्लोकादमृता भवन्ति ॥ ५ ॥
iha cedavedīdatha satyamasti
na cedihāvedīnmahatī vinaṣṭiḥ
bhūteṣu bhūteṣu vicitya dhīrāḥ
pretyāsmāllokādamṛtā bhavanti

2.5If one has known it here, then there is truth; if one has not known it here, the loss is great. Discerning it in beings and beings, the wise, gone from this world, become deathless.

Verse 5 supplies the only urgency in the text, and it is temporal. Here, in this life, or the loss is great. The word for what the wise do, vichitya, means having sorted or discriminated, and the phrase repeats the word for beings twice, which is how the Sanskrit says one by one.

ब्रह्म ह देवेभ्यो विजिग्ये तस्य ह ब्रह्मणो
विजये देवा अमहीयन्त ॥ १ ॥
त ऐक्षन्तास्माकमेवायं विजयोऽस्माकमेवायं महिमेति ।
तद्धैषां विजज्ञौ तेभ्यो ह प्रादुर्बभूव तन्न व्यजानत
किमिदं यक्षमिति ॥ २ ॥
तेऽग्निमब्रुवञ्जातवेद एतद्विजानीहि
किमिदं यक्षमिति तथेति ॥ ३ ॥
तदभ्यद्रवत्तमभ्यवदत्कोऽसीत्यग्निर्वा
अहमस्मीत्यब्रवीज्जातवेदा वा अहमस्मीति ॥ ४ ॥
brahma ha devebhyo vijigye tasya ha brahmaṇo
vijaye devā amahīyanta
3.2
ta aikṣantāsmākamevāyaṃ vijayo'smākamevāyaṃ mahimeti
taddhaiṣāṃ vijajñau tebhyo ha prādurbabhūva tanna vyajānata
kimidaṃ yakṣamiti
3.3
te'gnimabruvañjātaveda etadvijānīhi
kimidaṃ yakṣamiti tatheti
3.4
tadabhyadravattamabhyavadatko'sītyagnirvā
ahamasmītyabravījjātavedā vā ahamasmīti

3.1Brahman won a victory for the gods, and in that victory of Brahman's the gods grew great. They thought: ours alone is this victory, ours alone this greatness. It knew this of them and appeared before them, and they did not make out what this spirit was. They said to Agni: knower of all, find out what this spirit is. So be it, he said. He ran up to it. It said to him: who are you? I am Agni, he said; I am the knower of all.

The third chapter is a story and the tone changes completely. The gods have won and are congratulating themselves. A presence appears that none of them can identify, and they send their strongest in turn. The word for the presence is yaksha, which means something wondrous and, later, a nature-spirit; the text gives it no shape, and what is tested is not strength but identification.

तस्मिंस्त्वयि किं वीर्यमित्यपीदं सर्वं
दहेयं यदिदं पृथिव्यामिति ॥ ५ ॥
तस्मै तृणं निदधावेतद्दहेति ।
तदुपप्रेयाय सर्वजवेन तन्न शशाक दग्धुं स तत एव
निववृते नैतदशकं विज्ञातुं यदेतद्यक्षमिति ॥ ६ ॥
अथ वायुमब्रुवन्वायवेतद्विजानीहि
किमेतद्यक्षमिति तथेति ॥ ७ ॥
तदभ्यद्रवत्तमभ्यवदत्कोऽसीति वायुर्वा
अहमस्मीत्यब्रवीन्मातरिश्वा वा अहमस्मीति ॥ ८ ॥
tasmiṃstvayi kiṃ vīryamityapīdaṃ sarvaṃ
daheyaṃ yadidaṃ pṛthivyāmiti
3.6
tasmai tṛṇaṃ nidadhāvetaddaheti
tadupapreyāya sarvajavena tanna śaśāka dagdhuṃ sa tata eva
nivavṛte naitadaśakaṃ vijñātuṃ yadetadyakṣamiti
3.7
atha vāyumabruvanvāyavetadvijānīhi
kimetadyakṣamiti tatheti
3.8
tadabhyadravattamabhyavadatko'sīti vāyurvā
ahamasmītyabravīnmātariśvā vā ahamasmīti

3.5In you, then, what power is there? Even all this that is on the earth I could burn. It laid a blade of grass before him: burn this. He went at it with all his speed and could not burn it. He came back from it: I could not find out what this spirit is. Then they said to Vayu: wind, find out what this spirit is. So be it, he said. He ran up to it. It said to him: who are you? I am Vayu, he said; I am Matarishvan.

Each god is asked the same two questions in the same order, who are you and what can you do, and each answers with a name and a maximum. Fire says he can burn everything on earth; wind says he can carry everything away. The test that follows is the maximum applied to the smallest available object, and the failure is total in both cases.

तस्मिंस्त्वयि किं वीर्यमित्यपीदं
सर्वमाददीय यदिदं पृथिव्यामिति ॥ ९ ॥
तस्मै तृणं निदधावेतदादत्स्वेति
तदुपप्रेयाय सर्वजवेन तन्न शशाकादातुं स तत एव
निववृते नैतदशकं विज्ञातुं यदेतद्यक्षमिति ॥ १० ॥
अथेन्द्रमब्रुवन्मघवन्नेतद्विजानीहि किमेतद्यक्षमिति तथेति
तदभ्यद्रवत्तस्मात्तिरोदधे ॥ ११ ॥
स तस्मिन्नेवाकाशे स्त्रियमाजगाम बहुशोभमानामुमां
हैमवतीं तांहोवाच किमेतद्यक्षमिति ॥ १२ ॥
tasmiṃstvayi kiṃ vīryamityapīdaṃ
sarvamādadīya yadidaṃ pṛthivyāmiti
3.10
tasmai tṛṇaṃ nidadhāvetadādatsveti
tadupapreyāya sarvajavena tanna śaśākādātuṃ sa tata eva
nivavṛte naitadaśakaṃ vijñātuṃ yadetadyakṣamiti
3.11
athendramabruvanmaghavannetadvijānīhi kimetadyakṣamiti tatheti
tadabhyadravattasmāttirodadhe
3.12
sa tasminnevākāśe striyamājagāma bahuśobhamānāmumāṃ
haimavatīṃ tāṃhovāca kimetadyakṣamiti

3.9In you, then, what power is there? Even all this that is on the earth I could carry off. It laid a blade of grass before him: carry this off. He went at it with all his speed and could not lift it. He came back from it: I could not find out what this spirit is. Then they said to Indra: generous one, find out what this spirit is. So be it, he said. He ran up to it, and it vanished from him. In that same space he came upon a woman of great beauty, Uma of the snow mountain, and said to her: what is this spirit?

Indra is treated differently and the difference is the point of the story. He is not given a straw, because the presence disappears before he reaches it, and what he finds in its place is a teacher. Nagler notes a joke in the sequence that is easy to miss: Agni reports back only that he failed to identify the being, not that he failed to burn a straw.

सा ब्रह्मेति होवाच ब्रह्मणो वा एतद्विजये महीयध्वमिति
ततो हैव विदाञ्चकार ब्रह्मेति ॥ १ ॥
तस्माद्वा एते देवा अतितरामिवान्यान्देवान्यदग्निर्वायुरिन्द्रस्ते
ह्येनन्नेदिष्ठं पस्पर्शुस्ते ह्येनत्प्रथमो विदाञ्चकार ब्रह्मेति ॥ २ ॥
तस्माद्वा इन्द्रोऽतितरामिवान्यान्देवान्स
ह्येनन्नेदिष्ठं पस्पर्श स ह्येनत्प्रथमो विदाञ्चकार ब्रह्मेति ॥ ३ ॥
तस्यैष आदेशो यदेतद्विद्युतो व्यद्युतदा
इतीन् न्यमीमिषदा इत्यधिदैवतम् ॥ ४ ॥
sā brahmeti hovāca brahmaṇo vā etadvijaye mahīyadhvamiti
tato haiva vidāñcakāra brahmeti
4.2
tasmādvā ete devā atitarāmivānyāndevānyadagnirvāyurindraste
hyenannediṣṭhaṃ pasparśuste hyenatprathamo vidāñcakāra brahmeti
4.3
tasmādvā indro'titarāmivānyāndevānsa
hyenannediṣṭhaṃ pasparśa sa hyenatprathamo vidāñcakāra brahmeti
4.4
tasyaiṣa ādeśo yadetadvidyuto vyadyutadā
itīn nyamīmiṣadā ityadhidaivatam

4.1She said: that is Brahman. It is in Brahman's victory that you have been growing great. From that alone he knew it was Brahman. Therefore these gods — Agni, Vayu and Indra — are as it were beyond the other gods, for they came nearest to it, and they were the first to know it was Brahman. Therefore Indra is as it were beyond the other gods, for he came nearest to it, and he was the first to know it was Brahman. This is its teaching, as regards the gods: it is that which flashed in the lightning, and which made the eye blink.

The three gods are ranked by proximity rather than by strength, and the ranking inverts the order of the story: the two who failed the test come out above the gods who were never sent, and the one who was not tested at all comes out first. What he got for going was not a victory but an explanation. Verse 4 gives the first of two examples, and it is chosen because a flash of lightning is over before it can be looked at.

अथाध्यात्मं यद्देतद्गच्छतीव च मनोऽनेन
चैतदुपस्मरत्यभीक्ष्णं सङ्कल्पः ॥ ५ ॥
तद्ध तद्वनं नाम तद्वनमित्युपासितव्यं स य एतदेवं वेदाभि
हैनं सर्वाणि भूतानि संवाञ्छन्ति ॥ ६ ॥
उपनिषदं भो ब्रूहीत्युक्ता त उपनिषद्ब्राह्मीं वाव त
उपनिषदमब्रूमेति ॥ ७ ॥
तस्यै तपो दमः कर्मेति प्रतिष्ठा वेदाः सर्वाङ्गानि
सत्यमायतनम् ॥ ८ ॥
यो वा एतामेवं वेदापहत्य पाप्मानमनन्ते स्वर्गे
लोके ज्येये प्रतितिष्ठति प्रतितिष्ठति ॥ ९ ॥
athādhyātmaṃ yaddetadgacchatīva ca mano'nena
caitadupasmaratyabhīkṣṇaṃ saṅkalpaḥ
4.6
taddha tadvanaṃ nāma tadvanamityupāsitavyaṃ sa ya etadevaṃ vedābhi
hainaṃ sarvāṇi bhūtāni saṃvāñchanti
4.7
upaniṣadaṃ bho brūhītyuktā ta upaniṣadbrāhmīṃ vāva ta
upaniṣadamabrūmeti
4.8
tasyai tapo damaḥ karmeti pratiṣṭhā vedāḥ sarvāṅgāni
satyamāyatanam
4.9
yo vā etāmevaṃ vedāpahatya pāpmānamanante svarge
loke jyeye pratitiṣṭhati pratitiṣṭhati

4.5Now as regards the self: it is that towards which the mind seems to go, that by which one remembers again and again, and the will. Its name is Tadvanam, the beloved, and as the beloved it is to be honoured. All beings long for one who knows it so. Teach me the secret teaching, sir. The secret teaching has been told to you. We have told you the secret teaching of Brahman. Austerity, restraint and work are its foundation; the Vedas are all its limbs; truth is its dwelling place. Whoever knows it in this way, having struck off wrong, is founded in the endless world of light, the highest — founded indeed.

The parable and the doctrine are the same statement. Fire cannot burn, wind cannot carry and the gods cannot win except by a power that is not theirs, and the gods who come nearest to the presence are the ones who found this out. The fourth chapter makes the transfer explicit by giving two examples at two scales: lightning, which flashes and is gone, and the blink of an eye, and then the mind, which seems to go towards its object and by which one remembers. Nagler sets Augustine's in ictu trepidantis aspectus, in the striking of a fleeting look, beside the second. The name given in verse 6, Tadvanam, is one of two Upanishadic names for God with almost no literal sense, explained as the all-pervading beloved, and the text ends by naming its own subject: the word rendered as spiritual wisdom in verse 7 is upanishad. The practical consequence Nagler draws is Gandhi's. Since we are not the doer of any of our acts, including the act of knowing, the victory of good is guaranteed but not by us, and the way to be an instrument of it is what Gandhi called making oneself zero.

Prashna: six questions

The Prashna belongs to the Atharva Veda and is the most orderly text in the collection. Six men come to the sage Pippalada, each with one question, and the six questions descend in order from the making of the world to the person in whom the world is made. The word prashna means question and is cognate with the German Frage.

सुकेशा च भारद्वाजः शैब्यश्च सत्यकामः सौर्यायणी च गार्ग्यः ।
कौसल्यश्चाश्वलायनो भार्गवो वैदर्भिः कबन्धी कात्यायनः ।
ते हैते ब्रह्मपराः परं ब्रह्मान्वेषमाणाः ।
ते ह समित्पाणयो भगवन्तं पिप्पलादमुपसन्नाः ।
तान् ह स ऋषिरुवाच भूय एव तपसा ब्रह्मचर्येण श्रद्धया संवत्सरं संवत्स्यथ ।
यथाकामं प्रश्नान् पृच्छत यदि विज्ञास्यामः सर्वं ह वो वक्ष्याम इति ।
अथ कबन्धी कात्यायन उपेत्य पप्रच्छ ।
भगवन् कुतो ह वा इमाः प्रजाः प्रजायन्त इति ।
sukeśā ca bhāradvājaḥ śaibyaśca satyakāmaḥ sauryāyaṇī ca gārgyaḥ
kausalyaścāśvalāyano bhārgavo vaidarbhiḥ kabandhī kātyāyanaḥ
te haite brahmaparāḥ paraṃ brahmānveṣamāṇāḥ
te ha samitpāṇayo bhagavantaṃ pippalādamupasannāḥ
1.2
tān ha sa ṛṣiruvāca bhūya eva tapasā brahmacaryeṇa śraddhayā saṃvatsaraṃ saṃvatsyatha
yathākāmaṃ praśnān pṛcchata yadi vijñāsyāmaḥ sarvaṃ ha vo vakṣyāma iti
1.3
atha kabandhī kātyāyana upetya papraccha
bhagavan kuto ha vā imāḥ prajāḥ prajāyanta iti

1.1Sukesha Bharadvaja, Shaibya Satyakama, Sauryayani Gargya, Kausalya Ashvalayana, Bhargava Vaidarbhi and Kabandhi Katyayana — these, devoted to Brahman, seeking the highest Brahman, came with fuel in their hands to the honoured Pippalada. The seer said to them: live with me another year, in discipline, in continence and in faith. Then ask questions as you please, and if we know we will tell you everything. Then Kabandhi Katyayana came up and asked: sir, from what are these creatures born?

The condition Pippalada sets is a year of living together before any question is asked, and the condition is not ceremonial: the questions are of a kind whose answers cannot be handed over. His reply to the first question names the pair the rest of the text works with, prana and rayi, which Nagler translates roughly as consciousness and matter, and identifies them with the sun and the moon.

तान् वरिष्ठः प्राण उवाच । मा मोहमापद्यथ ।
अहमेवैतत् पञ्चधात्मानं प्रविभज्यैतद्बाणमवष्टभ्य विधारयामीति ।
तेऽश्रद्दधाना बभूवुः ।
सोऽभिमानादूर्ध्वमुत्क्रामत इव ।
तस्मिन्नुत्क्रामत्यथेतरे सर्व एवोत्क्रामन्ते ।
तद्यथा मक्षिका मधुकरराजानमुत्क्रामन्तं सर्वा एवोत्क्रमन्ते ।
तस्मिंश्च प्रतिष्ठमाने सर्वा एव प्रतिष्ठन्ते ।
आत्मन एष प्राणो जायते ।
यथैषा पुरुषे छायैतस्मिन्नेतदाततम् ।
मनोकृतेनायात्यस्मिञ्शरीरे ।
अथैकयोर्ध्व उदानः पुण्येन पुण्यं लोकं नयति ।
पापेन पापमुभाभ्यामेव मनुष्यलोकम् ।
tān variṣṭhaḥ prāṇa uvāca | mā mohamāpadyatha
ahamevaitat pañcadhātmānaṃ pravibhajyaitadbāṇamavaṣṭabhya vidhārayāmīti
te'śraddadhānā babhūvuḥ
2.4
so'bhimānādūrdhvamutkrāmata iva
tasminnutkrāmatyathetare sarva evotkrāmante
tadyathā makṣikā madhukararājānamutkrāmantaṃ sarvā evotkramante
tasmiṃśca pratiṣṭhamāne sarvā eva pratiṣṭhante
3.3
ātmana eṣa prāṇo jāyate
yathaiṣā puruṣe chāyaitasminnetadātatam
manokṛtenāyātyasmiñśarīre
3.7
athaikayordhva udānaḥ puṇyena puṇyaṃ lokaṃ nayati
pāpena pāpamubhābhyāmeva manuṣyalokam

2.3Prana, the greatest of them, said to them: do not deceive yourselves. It is I who hold this body together, dividing myself in five and propping up this reed. Out of pride he seemed to go upward. When he went up, all the others went up; when he settled, all settled. As when the bees go up after the queen going up, and all settle when she settles. This prana is born of the Self. As a shadow is cast by a person, so this is stretched out on that. It comes into this body by the act of the mind. The upward one, udana, leads by merit to the world of merit, by wrong to the world of wrong, and by both to the world of men.

The second question asks which of the powers supporting the body is the greatest, and the answer is given by an experiment rather than an argument. The powers quarrel; prana starts to leave; everything else has to leave with it; they believe him. The bees are the comparison, and the point of the comparison is that the queen does not command and is not obeyed — the swarm simply cannot stay without her. The shadow in the third answer is the sharpest image in the text, and Nagler notes that it puts the Self and its energy in the right order: the shadow is cast, and the caster does not move when the shadow does.

यथा गार्ग्य मरीचयोऽर्कस्यास्तं गच्छतः सर्वा एतस्मिंस्तेजोमण्डल एकीभवन्ति ।
ताः पुनः पुनरुदयतः प्रचरन्ति ।
एवं ह वै तत् सर्वं परे देवे मनस्येकीभवति ।
तेन तर्ह्येष पुरुषो न शृणोति न पश्यति स्वपितीत्याचक्षते ।
एतद्वै सत्यकाम परं चापरं च ब्रह्म यदोङ्कारः ।
तस्माद्विद्वानेतेनैवायतनेनैकतरमन्वेति ।
अथ हैनं सुकेशा भारद्वाजः पप्रच्छ ।
भगवन् हिरण्यनाभः कौसल्यो राजपुत्रो मामुपेत्यैतं प्रश्नमपृच्छत ।
षोडशकलं भारद्वाज पुरुषं वेत्थ ।
तमहं कुमारमब्रुवं नाहमिमं वेद ।
तस्मै स होवाच इहैवान्तःशरीरे सोम्य स पुरुषः ।
यस्मिन्नेताः षोडशकलाः प्रभवन्तीति ।
yathā gārgya marīcayo'rkasyāstaṃ gacchataḥ sarvā etasmiṃstejomaṇḍala ekībhavanti
tāḥ punaḥ punarudayataḥ pracaranti
evaṃ ha vai tat sarvaṃ pare deve manasyekībhavati
tena tarhyeṣa puruṣo na śṛṇoti na paśyati svapitītyācakṣate
5.2
etadvai satyakāma paraṃ cāparaṃ ca brahma yadoṅkāraḥ
tasmādvidvānetenaivāyatanenaikataramanveti
6.1
atha hainaṃ sukeśā bhāradvājaḥ papraccha
bhagavan hiraṇyanābhaḥ kausalyo rājaputro māmupetyaitaṃ praśnamapṛcchata
ṣoḍaśakalaṃ bhāradvāja puruṣaṃ vettha
tamahaṃ kumāramabruvaṃ nāhamimaṃ veda
6.2
tasmai sa hovāca ihaivāntaḥśarīre somya sa puruṣaḥ
yasminnetāḥ ṣoḍaśakalāḥ prabhavantīti

4.2As the rays of the setting sun all become one in that disc of light, and spread out again as it rises again — so all this becomes one in the highest god, the mind. Then this person does not hear, does not see. They say: he sleeps. This OM, Satyakama, is both the higher and the lower Brahman. Therefore by this support alone the knower reaches one or the other. Then Sukesha Bharadvaja asked him: sir, Hiranyanabha the prince of Kosala came to me and asked this question — do you know the person with sixteen parts? I said to the boy: I do not know him. He said to him: here, within the body, dear one, is that person in whom these sixteen parts arise.

The fourth question is about sleep and the answer is the setting sun: the senses are not extinguished but withdrawn into one place, and the image supplies its own account of waking. The fifth question asks what a man gains by meditating on OM until death, and the answer refuses to make it one thing, since the syllable reaches both the lower Brahman and the higher and the meditator gets what he aimed at. The sixth question is asked by a man who was asked it himself and admitted he could not answer, which is the only such admission in the collection.

स यथेमा नद्यः स्यन्दमानाः समुद्रायणाः समुद्रं प्राप्यास्तं गच्छन्ति ।
भिद्येते तासां नामरूपे समुद्र इत्येवं प्रोच्यते ।
एवमेवास्य परिद्रष्टुरिमाः षोडशकलाः पुरुषायणाः पुरुषं प्राप्यास्तं गच्छन्ति ।
स एषोऽकलोऽमृतो भवति ।
अरा इव रथनाभौ कला यस्मिन् प्रतिष्ठिताः ।
तं वेद्यं पुरुषं वेद यथा मा वो मृत्युः परिव्यथा इति ।
sa yathemā nadyaḥ syandamānāḥ samudrāyaṇāḥ samudraṃ prāpyāstaṃ gacchanti
bhidyete tāsāṃ nāmarūpe samudra ityevaṃ procyate
evamevāsya paridraṣṭurimāḥ ṣoḍaśakalāḥ puruṣāyaṇāḥ puruṣaṃ prāpyāstaṃ gacchanti
sa eṣo'kalo'mṛto bhavati
6.6
arā iva rathanābhau kalā yasmin pratiṣṭhitāḥ
taṃ vedyaṃ puruṣaṃ veda yathā mā vo mṛtyuḥ parivyathā iti

6.5As these flowing rivers, making for the sea, on reaching the sea come to their setting, and their name and shape are broken, and it is called simply the sea — so of this onlooker these sixteen parts, making for the person, on reaching the person come to their setting. He becomes partless and deathless. As spokes are fixed in the hub of a wheel, so the parts are fixed in him. Know that person who is to be known, so that death may not distress you.

The sixteen parts are prana, faith, the five elements, the senses, mind, food, strength, austerity, mantra, work, the worlds and the name; the list is a catalogue of everything a person is made of, including the intangible items. What the last two verses do is dissolve the list. The rivers are the Mundaka's rivers and the wheel is the Brihadaranyaka's wheel, and this is one of the places where the collection can be seen quoting itself.

Taittiriya: the ascent to joy

The Taittiriya belongs to the Black Yajur Veda and is named after a bird, the partridge, which is probably onomatopoeia. It is almost all prose, and Shankara called it the essence of its tradition. It is divided into three vallis with thirty-one sections between them, counted straight through here. The first is about instruction, the second about what a person is made of, the third about a boy who keeps coming back to ask again.

वेदमनूच्याचार्योऽन्तेवासिनमनुशास्ति ।
सत्यं वद । धर्मं चर । स्वाध्यायान्मा प्रमदः ।
मातृदेवो भव । पितृदेवो भव । आचार्यदेवो भव । अतिथिदेवो भव ।
श्रद्धया देयम् । अश्रद्धयाऽदेयम् । श्रिया देयम् । ह्रिया देयम् । भिया देयम् ।
vedamanūcyācāryo'ntevāsinamanuśāsti
satyaṃ vada | dharmaṃ cara | svādhyāyānmā pramadaḥ
mātṛdevo bhava | pitṛdevo bhava | ācāryadevo bhava | atithidevo bhava
śraddhayā deyam | aśraddhayā'deyam | śriyā deyam | hriyā deyam | bhiyā deyam

11Having taught the Veda, the teacher instructs the pupil. Speak the truth. Do what is right. Do not be careless about your own study. Be one to whom mother is a god, father is a god, teacher is a god, guest is a god. Give with faith; do not give without faith. Give in proportion. Give with modesty. Give with awe.

This is the oldest convocation address on record and it is still read at Indian universities. It is a list of imperatives with no reasons attached, and the interesting part is the middle. The three qualifications on giving — shriya, hriya, bhiya — form a jingle that makes them easy to remember and hard to translate; they are roughly with modesty, with sympathy and with awe. The instruction that follows in the original tells the student what to do when he does not know what is right, and the answer is to find people who are careful, unharsh and not driven, and do what they do.

ब्रह्मविदाप्नोति परम् । तदेषाभ्युक्ता ।
सत्यं ज्ञानमनन्तं ब्रह्म । यो वेद निहितं गुहायां परमे व्योमन् ।
तस्माद्वा एतस्मादात्मन आकाशः सम्भूतः । आकाशाद्वायुः । वायोरग्निः ।
स वा एष पुरुषोऽन्नरसमयः ।
तस्माद्वा एतस्मादन्नरसमयात् । अन्योऽन्तर आत्मा प्राणमयः ।
तस्य प्राण एव शिरः । व्यानो दक्षिणः पक्षः । अपान उत्तरः पक्षः ।
आकाश आत्मा । पृथिवी पुच्छं प्रतिष्ठा ।
तस्माद्वा एतस्मात् प्राणमयात् । अन्योऽन्तर आत्मा मनोमयः ।
तस्य यजुरेव शिरः । ऋग्दक्षिणः पक्षः । सामोत्तरः पक्षः ।
यतो वाचो निवर्तन्ते । अप्राप्य मनसा सह ।
तस्माद्वा एतस्मान्मनोमयात् । अन्योऽन्तर आत्मा विज्ञानमयः ।
तस्य श्रद्धैव शिरः । ऋतं दक्षिणः पक्षः । सत्यमुत्तरः पक्षः ।
विज्ञानं यज्ञं तनुते । कर्माणि तनुतेऽपि च ।
तस्माद्वा एतस्माद्विज्ञानमयात् । अन्योऽन्तर आत्मानन्दमयः ।
तस्य प्रियमेव शिरः । मोदो दक्षिणः पक्षः । प्रमोद उत्तरः पक्षः ।
आनन्द आत्मा । ब्रह्म पुच्छं प्रतिष्ठा ।
brahmavidāpnoti param | tadeṣābhyuktā
satyaṃ jñānamanantaṃ brahma | yo veda nihitaṃ guhāyāṃ parame vyoman
tasmādvā etasmādātmana ākāśaḥ sambhūtaḥ | ākāśādvāyuḥ | vāyoragniḥ
sa vā eṣa puruṣo'nnarasamayaḥ
14
tasmādvā etasmādannarasamayāt | anyo'ntara ātmā prāṇamayaḥ
tasya prāṇa eva śiraḥ | vyāno dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ | apāna uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ
ākāśa ātmā | pṛthivī pucchaṃ pratiṣṭhā
15
tasmādvā etasmāt prāṇamayāt | anyo'ntara ātmā manomayaḥ
tasya yajureva śiraḥ | ṛgdakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ | sāmottaraḥ pakṣaḥ
16
yato vāco nivartante | aprāpya manasā saha
tasmādvā etasmānmanomayāt | anyo'ntara ātmā vijñānamayaḥ
tasya śraddhaiva śiraḥ | ṛtaṃ dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ | satyamuttaraḥ pakṣaḥ
17
vijñānaṃ yajñaṃ tanute | karmāṇi tanute'pi ca
tasmādvā etasmādvijñānamayāt | anyo'ntara ātmānandamayaḥ
tasya priyameva śiraḥ | modo dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ | pramoda uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ
ānanda ātmā | brahma pucchaṃ pratiṣṭhā

13The knower of Brahman reaches the highest. Brahman is the real, knowledge, the endless. He who knows it as set in the cave, in the highest space — from this Self space came to be, from space air, from air fire. This person is made of the essence of food. From this one made of the essence of food there is another self within, made of breath. Breath is its head; the out-breath is its right wing, the down-breath its left; space is its trunk, earth its foundation. From this one made of breath there is another self within, made of mind. The Yajur is its head; the Rig is its right wing, the Sama its left. From which words turn back, not having reached it, together with the mind. From this one made of mind there is another self within, made of understanding. Faith is its head; order is its right wing, truth its left. Understanding stretches out the sacrifice, and stretches out works as well. From this one made of understanding there is another self within, made of joy. Delight is its head; gladness is its right wing, rejoicing its left; joy is its trunk, Brahman its foundation.

Five selves are described in the same five terms and each is said to be purna, filled, by the one inside it. The parts are a bird's: head, right wing, left wing, trunk, and a tail that is the pratishtha, the thing it rests on. Easwaran renders the wings as arms and the bird disappears in the English, which is why the figure here draws what the Sanskrit says. The later tradition gave the five their names — annamaya, pranamaya, manomaya, vijnanamaya, anandamaya — and called them koshas, sheaths; the text itself never uses the word. What the structure claims is that the body is not the outermost layer of a person but the outermost of five, and that each layer is a self to whoever is standing in it.

भीषास्माद्वातः पवते । भीषोदेति सूर्यः ।
युवा स्यात्साधुयुवाध्यायकः । आशिष्ठो दृढिष्ठो बलिष्ठः ।
तस्येयं पृथिवी सर्वा वित्तस्य पूर्णा स्यात् । स एको मानुष आनन्दः ।
ते ये शतं मानुषा आनन्दाः । स एको मनुष्यगन्धर्वाणामानन्दः ।
श्रोत्रियस्य चाकामहतस्य ।
यतो वाचो निवर्तन्ते । अप्राप्य मनसा सह ।
आनन्दं ब्रह्मणो विद्वान् । न बिभेति कुतश्चनेति ।
bhīṣāsmādvātaḥ pavate | bhīṣodeti sūryaḥ
yuvā syātsādhuyuvādhyāyakaḥ | āśiṣṭho dṛḍhiṣṭho baliṣṭhaḥ
tasyeyaṃ pṛthivī sarvā vittasya pūrṇā syāt | sa eko mānuṣa ānandaḥ
te ye śataṃ mānuṣā ānandāḥ | sa eko manuṣyagandharvāṇāmānandaḥ
śrotriyasya cākāmahatasya
21
yato vāco nivartante | aprāpya manasā saha
ānandaṃ brahmaṇo vidvān | na bibheti kutaścaneti

20Through fear of this the wind blows; through fear of it the sun rises. Suppose a young man, a good young man, well read, quick, firm and strong, and suppose the whole earth full of wealth were his: that is one measure of human joy. A hundred of those human joys are one joy of the human spirits — and of one who has heard and is free from craving. From which words turn back, not having reached it, together with the mind. One who knows the joy of Brahman fears nothing at all.

The calculation has eight steps and each multiplies by a hundred: human joy, then the joy of the human spirits, the ancestors, the gods born so, the gods by work, the gods, Indra, Brihaspati, Virat and Prajapati. What makes it an argument rather than a list is the clause repeated at every rung, that the same joy belongs to a man who has heard the teaching and is free from craving. The ladder is built in order to be stepped off, and the last section takes back the terms in which it was built, since what words turn back from cannot be at the top of a series.

भृगुर्वै वारुणिः । वरुणं पितरमुपससार । अधीहि भगवो ब्रह्मेति ।
अन्नं प्राणं चक्षुः श्रोत्रं मनो वाचमिति तं होवाच ।
यतो वा इमानि भूतानि जायन्ते । येन जातानि जीवन्ति ।
यत्प्रयन्त्यभिसंविशन्ति । तद्विजिज्ञासस्व । तद्ब्रह्मेति ।
अन्नं ब्रह्मेति व्यजानात् ।
अन्नाद्ध्येव खल्विमानि भूतानि जायन्ते । अन्नेन जातानि जीवन्ति ।
तद्विज्ञाय पुनरेव वरुणं पितरमुपससार । अधीहि भगवो ब्रह्मेति ।
तं होवाच तपसा ब्रह्म विजिज्ञासस्व । तपो ब्रह्मेति ।
आनन्दो ब्रह्मेति व्यजानात् ।
आनन्दाद्ध्येव खल्विमानि भूतानि जायन्ते । आनन्देन जातानि जीवन्ति ।
सैषा भार्गवी वारुणी विद्या । परमे व्योमन् प्रतिष्ठिता ।
bhṛgurvai vāruṇiḥ | varuṇaṃ pitaramupasasāra | adhīhi bhagavo brahmeti
annaṃ prāṇaṃ cakṣuḥ śrotraṃ mano vācamiti taṃ hovāca
yato vā imāni bhūtāni jāyante | yena jātāni jīvanti
yatprayantyabhisaṃviśanti | tadvijijñāsasva | tadbrahmeti
23
annaṃ brahmeti vyajānāt
annāddhyeva khalvimāni bhūtāni jāyante | annena jātāni jīvanti
tadvijñāya punareva varuṇaṃ pitaramupasasāra | adhīhi bhagavo brahmeti
taṃ hovāca tapasā brahma vijijñāsasva | tapo brahmeti
27
ānando brahmeti vyajānāt
ānandāddhyeva khalvimāni bhūtāni jāyante | ānandena jātāni jīvanti
saiṣā bhārgavī vāruṇī vidyā | parame vyoman pratiṣṭhitā

22Bhrigu, the son of Varuna, came to his father Varuna and said: sir, teach me Brahman. He told him this: food, breath, eye, ear, mind, speech. And he said to him: that from which these beings are born, by which when born they live, into which they go and enter — seek to know that. That is Brahman. He knew that food is Brahman. For from food these beings are born, by food when born they live. Having known that, he came again to his father Varuna and said: sir, teach me Brahman. He said to him: seek to know Brahman by tapas. Tapas is Brahman. He knew that joy is Brahman. For from joy these beings are born, by joy when born they live. This is the knowledge of Bhrigu and Varuna, founded in the highest space.

Varuna never answers the question. He gives a test — find what things are born from, live by and return to — and then sends the boy away five times with the same one-line instruction. What the boy brings back each time is his own, and the five answers he brings are the five selves of the second valli in order: food, breath, mind, understanding, joy. The pedagogy is the point. The father possesses the answer throughout and refuses to say it, because the sequence is not information.

अन्नं बहु कुर्वीत । तद्व्रतम् ।
न कञ्चन वसतौ प्रत्याचक्षीत । तद्व्रतम् ।
तस्माद्यया कया च विधया बह्वन्नं प्राप्नुयात् ।
अराध्यस्मा अन्नमित्याचक्षते ।
annaṃ bahu kurvīta | tadvratam
31
na kañcana vasatau pratyācakṣīta | tadvratam
tasmādyayā kayā ca vidhayā bahvannaṃ prāpnuyāt
arādhyasmā annamityācakṣate

30Make food plentiful. That is the rule. Do not turn anyone away from your house. That is the rule. Therefore by whatever means, let a man get plenty of food. They say of such a man: food is ready for him.

Four instructions about food close the text and they are all practical. Do not despise food; do not waste it; increase it, because the earth can yield more; and refuse it to nobody who comes. Nagler treats the third as the answer to the charge that this literature is world-denying, and the sentence is hard to read any other way: it is an instruction to raise the yield.

Aitareya: the unity of life

The Aitareya belongs to the Rig Veda and sits inside the Aitareya Aranyaka. It is the shortest of the principal texts after the Mandukya; its three chapters hold five sections, counted straight through here, and it tells the story of creation as a sequence of openings.

आत्मा वा इदमेक एवाग्र आसीत् । नान्यत्किञ्चन मिषत् ।
स ईक्षत लोकान्नु सृजा इति ।
स इमांल्लोकानसृजत । अम्भो मरीचीर्मरमापः ।
अदोऽम्भः परेण दिवं द्यौः प्रतिष्ठान्तरिक्षं मरीचयः ।
पृथिवी मरो या अधस्तात्ता आपः ।
स ईक्षतेमे नु लोका लोकपालान्नु सृजा इति ।
सोऽद्भ्य एव पुरुषं समुद्धृत्यामूर्छयत् ।
तमभ्यतपत्तस्याभितप्तस्य मुखं निरभिद्यत यथाण्डम् ।
मुखाद्वाग्वाचोऽग्निर्नासिके निरभिद्येतां नासिकाभ्यां प्राणः ।
अक्षिणी निरभिद्येतामक्षीभ्यां चक्षुश्चक्षुष आदित्यः ।
हृदयं निरभिद्यत हृदयान्मनो मनसश्चन्द्रमाः ।
ātmā vā idameka evāgra āsīt | nānyatkiñcana miṣat
sa īkṣata lokānnu sṛjā iti
1.2
sa imāṃllokānasṛjata | ambho marīcīrmaramāpaḥ
ado'mbhaḥ pareṇa divaṃ dyauḥ pratiṣṭhāntarikṣaṃ marīcayaḥ
pṛthivī maro yā adhastāttā āpaḥ
1.3
sa īkṣateme nu lokā lokapālānnu sṛjā iti
so'dbhya eva puruṣaṃ samuddhṛtyāmūrchayat
1.4
tamabhyatapattasyābhitaptasya mukhaṃ nirabhidyata yathāṇḍam
mukhādvāgvāco'gnirnāsike nirabhidyetāṃ nāsikābhyāṃ prāṇaḥ
akṣiṇī nirabhidyetāmakṣībhyāṃ cakṣuścakṣuṣa ādityaḥ
hṛdayaṃ nirabhidyata hṛdayānmano manasaścandramāḥ

1.1In the beginning the Self alone was this, and nothing else whatever blinked. He thought: let me send forth worlds. He sent forth these worlds: the flood, the light-rays, the mortal, the waters. That flood is beyond the sky; the sky is its floor; the middle air is the light-rays; the earth is the mortal; what is below is the waters. He thought: here now are the worlds. Let me send forth guardians of the worlds. He drew a person out of the waters and gave him a shape. He brooded on him, and from him so brooded on the mouth split open as an egg does; from the mouth speech, from speech fire. The nostrils split open; from the nostrils breath, from breath the wind. The eyes split open; from the eyes sight, from sight the sun. The heart split open; from the heart mind, from mind the moon.

The word for what the Self does to the person, abhyatapat, is the verb of tapas and means brooded over or heated. Each opening produces two things at once, a faculty and the piece of the world that faculty deals with, so that the eye and the sun come into being in the same act. The order is anatomical from the top down, and the last two openings, the navel and the sex organ, produce the downward breath with death, and the seed with birth. The deities then enter the body by the doors they came out of; only the Self enters by a door that was never a sense, the opening at the crown, and the text says it found the three states of consciousness there.

कोऽयमात्मेति वयमुपास्महे कतरः स आत्मा ।
येन वा पश्यति येन वा शृणोति येन वा गन्धानाजिघ्रति ।
येन वा वाचं व्याकरोति येन वा स्वादु चास्वादु च विजानाति ।
एष ब्रह्मैष इन्द्र एष प्रजापतिरेते सर्वे देवाः ।
इमानि च पञ्चमहाभूतानि पृथिवी वायुराकाश आपो ज्योतींषि ।
अण्डजानि च जारुजानि च स्वेदजानि चोद्भिज्जानि च ।
अश्वा गावः पुरुषा हस्तिनो यत्किञ्चेदं प्राणि जङ्गमं च पतत्रि च ।
सर्वं तत्प्रज्ञानेत्रं प्रज्ञाने प्रतिष्ठितं प्रज्ञानेत्रो लोकः प्रज्ञा प्रतिष्ठा प्रज्ञानं ब्रह्म ।
ko'yamātmeti vayamupāsmahe kataraḥ sa ātmā
yena vā paśyati yena vā śṛṇoti yena vā gandhānājighrati
yena vā vācaṃ vyākaroti yena vā svādu cāsvādu ca vijānāti
5.3
eṣa brahmaiṣa indra eṣa prajāpatirete sarve devāḥ
imāni ca pañcamahābhūtāni pṛthivī vāyurākāśa āpo jyotīṃṣi
aṇḍajāni ca jārujāni ca svedajāni codbhijjāni ca
aśvā gāvaḥ puruṣā hastino yatkiñcedaṃ prāṇi jaṅgamaṃ ca patatri ca
sarvaṃ tatprajñānetraṃ prajñāne pratiṣṭhitaṃ prajñānetro lokaḥ prajñā pratiṣṭhā prajñānaṃ brahma

5.1Who is this Self we worship? Which is the Self? Is it that by which one sees, or by which one hears, or by which one smells odours, or by which one speaks, or by which one knows the sweet and the unsweet? This is Brahman, this is Indra, this is Prajapati, these are all the gods; and these five great elements, earth, air, space, water, light; and the creatures born of eggs, born of wombs, born of sweat, born of shoots; horses, cattle, people, elephants; whatever breathes, and moves, and flies, and stands still. All that has consciousness for its eye, is founded on consciousness. Consciousness is Brahman.

The final sentence, prajnanam brahma, is one of the four great formulae. What gives it its force is the list it comes at the end of, which is deliberately mixed: gods and elements and livestock and things that do not move, sorted only by how they are born. The compound in the last line, prajnanetram, means having consciousness as its eye, or moved by consciousness, and both readings are defended. Nagler takes the formula as the explanation of two things at once, the unity of life and its sanctity, and notes that the text makes the same claim about a human being that the Cloud of Unknowing makes: nothing in nature stands above.

Four more, and what minor means

Easwaran closes with four short texts that do not belong to Shankara's list, and Nagler is careful about what that means: a minor Upanishad is one Shankara did not comment on or cite at length, not one that is minor in wisdom or in force. Some of them, he says, carry insights of the same size as the ones Shankara singled out, and often in the same style.

TextWhat it isWhere it belongs
TejobinduThe beyondness of reality, and the instruction to go past the scriptures themselvesNo Vedic affiliation
AtmaThree selves — the body, the mind, and what is beyond bothNo Vedic affiliation
AmritabinduTwenty-two verses on the mind as the seat of the whole struggleNo Vedic affiliation
ParamahamsaThat the mendicant's staff, thread and tuft are symbols of things to be had withinWhite Yajur Veda

Four or five Upanishads have names ending in bindu, a drop, which marks them as distillations. The Amritabindu's first line is the one most often lifted out of the group — the mind is of two kinds, pure and impure, and it is the mind that frees or enslaves — and the text then reasons from the jar of air that does not change when the jar is moved and does not know when the jar is broken. The Paramahamsa takes its name from the great swan, a bird that can fly alone, and it says of its subject that he has no staff, no tuft and no sacred thread, which in the society the text was written in meant giving up the right to perform the rites at all. Nagler sets it beside the last chapter of the Dhammapada and, later, the poems of Kabir.

Since about 700 BC

Nobody knows when these texts were composed or by whom, and the sages did not care to leave names. The oldest layer is prose and the Brihadaranyaka and Chandogya belong to it; the Katha, the Isha and the Kena are verse and later; the Shvetashvatara is later still, and some texts bearing the name are medieval. Easwaran's own dating is loose to the point of being a gesture — he writes of five thousand years and of the dawn of time — and Nagler is more careful, noting that the Isha comes down as part of a Samhita, which would normally argue for high antiquity, except that the White Yajur Veda is itself relatively recent. The usual scholarly range for the principal texts runs from about the eighth or seventh century BC to a few centuries after, and no argument in the collection depends on the date.

The commentarial history is better documented than the compositional one. Gaudapada's verses on the Mandukya came first and argued the case on grounds independent of scripture. Shankara, early in the eighth century, wrote on ten of the Upanishads and in doing so gave them a secondary canonization; the list runs Isha, Kena, Katha, Prashna, Mundaka, Mandukya, Taittiriya, Aitareya, Chandogya, Brihadaranyaka, and a text's being called minor means only that it is not on that list. Nagler is careful to say that the list has been reshaped by various authorities and is not fixed. What Shankara built on the texts was a system, and when India's philosophers built systems on this foundation they found they had produced positions that disagreed with one another. Easwaran's response to that is Ramakrishna's: the systems are views from different vantage points, neither higher nor lower, and in practice they come to the same thing.

The Western history begins with Schopenhauer, who read the collection in a Latin translation of a Persian one and said the Upanishads had been the consolation of his life and would be the consolation of his death. He made two predictions. The first was that the barrier between East and West would fall and that his century's one advantage over the seventeenth would be its possession of these documents. The second, in the sentence usually cut from the quotation, was that they were "destined sooner or later to become the faith of the people". Nagler's verdict is that the old pessimist was wrong about the timing: two centuries came and went and brought neither the revolution nor much of the open-mindedness Schopenhauer himself had. Max Müller translated them, Yeats was drawn by the poetry, Huxley filed them under the perennial philosophy, and Easwaran arrived at them by accident in a college library and spent forty years with them.

Read straight through, the collection turns out to be less various than its reputation. The same verses recur in different texts — the two birds in the Mundaka and the Shvetashvatara, the sun that does not shine there in the Katha, the Mundaka and the Shvetashvatara, the line about the Self choosing whom it will in the Katha and the Mundaka — and the same half-dozen images do most of the work everywhere: the rivers reaching the sea, the salt in water, the spider and its thread, the wheel and its spokes, the two selves in one cave. What differs between the texts is temperament rather than doctrine. The Brihadaranyaka argues in public and enjoys it; the Chandogya teaches a boy at home; the Prashna answers questions in order; the Shvetashvatara prays.

What has held up is the method rather than the cosmology. The texts do not ask for belief; they describe a procedure and claim it is repeatable, and they say so in a tone that is neither indignant nor defensive — ignorance is its own punishment and wisdom its own reward. The psychology of attention they rest on has proved the most portable part: the claim that concentration can be trained, that trained concentration changes what is available to observation, and that the mind is better treated as an instrument than as the observer. What has been contested is everything that follows from the second step. That the states of consciousness are also strata; that there is a fourth which is not a state; that the observer who survives the withdrawal of body and mind is the same in everyone — these are claims about what is found, not about what is inferred, and the collection offers no way to check them from outside. Easwaran does not pretend otherwise. His argument for the texts is the one they make for themselves, which is that they are instructions, and that the only test of an instruction is to carry it out.

Claims drawn from this chapter · 9

opinionIsha: the inner ruler — Isha 1–2

The Isha Upanishad tells you to enjoy what you do not own

The Isha Upanishad's first two verses deny that anything is yours and in the same breath tell you to enjoy it. They then prescribe a hundred years of work, on the ground that work done this way leaves nothing clinging.

BecauseIsha 1–2 puts the two together — Gandhi reduced the first verse to two words, renounce and enjoy — and names a hundred years of work as the condition; it is a prescription, with the claim about residue unfalsifiable as stated.
LimitsNo measurement, and no account of what a residue would look like if it were there. Job crafting reaches a comparable result from the other direction by changing the job's boundaries and meaning rather than the person's claim on the result.
PracticeWitness practice in review — do the whole of the work and drop the claim on what it produces; the two are separable and only the second is optional.
After AIAn assistant makes the output cheap, which makes ownership of the output a stranger thing to insist on. The sentence reads now as an instruction about what to hold on to when the product stops being a good measure of the work.
opinionKatha: death as teacher — Katha 4.1

The Katha Upanishad asks the reader to turn the eye back

The Katha Upanishad says the sense organs open outward, so attention goes outward by itself. The one thing the collection asks a reader to do is turn the eye back on itself.

BecauseKatha 4.1 gives *avritta-chakshus*, the eye turned back, as anatomy used as an argument; it is an instruction with no way to check the result from outside.
LimitsAn anatomical observation made to carry a metaphysical conclusion. The direction of the sense organs explains a default of attention and not the existence of anything found by reversing it.
PracticeWitness practice in review — during a review, take one deliberate look at who is reviewing rather than only at what is under review.
After AIThe default runs harder now, because the outward opening answers back. An assistant supplies an endless outside to attend to, and the turning back has to be scheduled.
opinionKatha: death as teacher — Katha 2.1–2.2

The Katha Upanishad judges a person by what they prefer, not do

The Katha Upanishad says that at every moment two things present themselves: what is good for you and what you are fond of. What tells one person from another is which of the two they prefer, not what they end up doing.

BecauseKatha 2.1–2.2 sets *shreya* against *preya* as a standing pair rather than an occasional dilemma, and Nagler's gloss has the choice faced waking and even sleeping; it is a claim about the structure of motivation, offered without evidence.
LimitsSelf-determination theory reads the same data the other way: what a person is drawn to is evidence about which needs are being met, not a verdict on the person. The text offers no way to tell an act done from *shreya* from the same act done from *preya*.
PracticeWitness practice in review — when you notice you have reached for the easier route, record the preference rather than the action; the action is often the same either way.
After AIDelegation makes the two indistinguishable from outside. Using the assistant because it is the right tool and using it because it is the easy one produce identical commits, which leaves the preference as the only thing to watch.
opinionKatha: death as teacher — Katha 3.10 and 3.13

The Katha Upanishad ranks the mind above the senses and their objects

The world you meet is shaped by the equipment you meet it with. The Katha Upanishad puts sense objects above the senses and the mind above both, as a ladder to be climbed.

BecauseKatha 3.10 and the ladder of 3.13 state the order as a hierarchy to be climbed, in the order Nagler insists the text actually gives; the ranking is asserted, not argued.
LimitsA ladder with a top, offered as a route rather than a mechanism. Kahneman's version of the same observation is a process to be corrected from outside by a rule; the Katha's is a climb to be made from inside, and nothing in the text says how to tell progress from imagination.
PracticeProjection log — when something in a system looks obvious, record what equipment made it look that way before acting on the appearance.
After AIThe equipment now includes a model whose processing nobody can inspect. The ladder gains a rung the text does not have: what is encountered has been shaped by a system that is neither the senses nor the mind.
opinionKena: by whom? — Kena 3–4

The Kena Upanishad says what you can worship is not the reality

The Kena Upanishad says that once you can set something up in front of you and worship it, that thing is not the reality being pointed at. It also says no power a faculty uses, such as fire's burning, belongs to the faculty itself.

Becausethe second half of each Kena refrain is "not this that people worship here", and in the parable of Kena 3–4 fire cannot burn a straw and wind cannot move it, while the ranking inverts the order of the story so that the tested gods come out above the untested.
LimitsThe parable is a teaching story and the refrain is a liturgical formula; neither is an argument that could fail. Its usable half is the psychological one about projection, which is where the evidence in this vault sits.
PracticeProjection log — record what you have set up as the thing that really knows, and notice that the setting-up was yours.
After AIThe god who came nearest was the one who went to find out whose power it was. That is the posture the vault asks for towards a model: not deciding what it is, and not deciding it is nothing either.
opinionMandukya: consciousness and its phases

The Mandukya's fourth is what waking, dream and sleep are made of

The fourth is not another state alongside waking, dreaming and deep sleep. It is what those three are made of, and the text can point at it only by saying what it is not.

Becausethe Mandukya's twelve sentences close six ways of knowing and six handles in the seventh before *shanta*, *shiva*, *advaita* arrive, and Nagler is explicit that the ordinal records only the order of arrival.
LimitsA claim marked only by denials cannot be failed by any observation. Metzinger's account explains the same impression as a feature of a self-model whose construction is invisible to it.
PracticeSitting practice — sit long enough to notice that the states change and something does not, and stop short of deciding what that something is.
After AIIt is the one claim in this vault that no measurement can reach, which is why it belongs on the far side of any argument that starts from evidence. A model that reports states has states of a system; nothing here reaches the question of what they are made of.
contestedThe supreme science, and Since about 700 BC

The Upanishads offer a procedure to run, not a doctrine to believe

The Upanishads describe a repeatable procedure for turning attention on the mind itself. They offer no way for anyone who has not carried it out to check the result from outside.

BecauseEaswaran frames *brahmavidya* as a science with the mind as both instrument and specimen, and admits that the strata, the fourth, and the sameness of the observer in everyone are claims about what is found rather than what is inferred.
LimitsAn instrument that is also the specimen cannot be checked against itself, which is the objection Thompson presses. What has proved portable is the psychology of attention — that concentration can be trained and that trained concentration changes what is available to observation — and not the cosmology that follows it.
PracticeSitting practice — carry out the instruction and report what happens, and do not offer the report as evidence to anyone who has not.
After AIThe vault's method is the opposite one: a number, a date and a score. This claim marks the boundary where that method stops, and the honest thing is to leave the boundary visible rather than to dress the texts in evidence they do not have.
opinionKatha: death as teacher — Katha 2.5 and 2.9

The Upanishads warn about half-learned confidence, not ignorance

The Upanishads do not condemn ignorance. What they warn against is the confidence that comes with a little learning: the Katha describes the blind being led by the blind, and says reasoning alone will not settle the question.

BecauseKatha 2.5 has the blind led by the blind, repeated almost word for word in the Mundaka, and 2.9 says that *tarka*, reasoning, does not terminate here; both are assertions, and the psychological claim in them is the part that has since been measured elsewhere.
LimitsThe text's remedy is a teacher who has been up the path, and nothing in it is scored or scoreable. The Dunning and Kruger pattern that supplies the modern evidence has itself been contested on statistical grounds.
PracticeCalibration log — treat the arrival of confidence as the moment to check, and score the check.
After AIA middling amount of learning is what an assistant reliably produces in a hurry, and it arrives with the fluency that the text says is the danger. The remedy the vault can actually run is Tetlock's score rather than the Katha's teacher.
opinionKena: by whom? — Kena 1.3–1.4

Whoever is doing the knowing cannot be found among things known

The one doing the knowing can never be found among the things known. Looking at something only moves it from the unknown pile to the known one, and the knower is in neither pile.

BecauseKena 1.3–1.4 and the five-fold refrain disqualify speech, mind, eye, ear and breath in turn — every channel by which anything is known, enumerated and then ruled out.
LimitsAn enumeration is only as good as its list, and the argument works by exhausting the channels the text names. Hofstadter and Seth both take the opposite route, treating the self as something a system builds out of what it can observe.
PracticeWitness practice in review — stop trying to inspect the reviewer as one more object in the review; notice instead that the inspecting is happening.
After AITetlock's answer is the direct challenge: the forecaster can be made an object after all, by a Brier score and a benchmark. Both cannot be right about the same thing, which is why this pairing is worth holding open.
